Apple refreshes the iMac, entry-level MacBook Pro with the M6 chip
2026-10-27

Apple refreshes the iMac, entry-level MacBook Pro with the M6 chip

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Apple has announced refreshes for the iMac and the entry-level, 14-inch MacBook Pro. Both machines are getting the M6 chip, which was introduced in the Mac mini several weeks ago. The M6 is split into two variants for the iMac. Picking the “M6 chip with two Thunderbolt ports” option gets you a 10-core CPU and a 10-core GPU, whereas picking “M6 chip with four Thunderbolt ports” gets you 12 cores for each, instead. The two-port variant maxes out at 24 GB of RAM and 1TB of storage, whereas the four-port one maxes out at 32GB of RAM and 2TB of storage. There’s only one M6 chip option for the MacBook Pro—the one with the 12-core GPU and CPU. It caps out at 32GB of RAM, but can go to 4TB of storage. It’s a more substantial refresh for the iMac, as its previous update introduced the M4. Apple  skipped the M5 and went straight to the M6 here. In terms of performance gains moving from the M4 to the M6, Apple said with the Mac mini that users can expect up to 40 percent faster CPU performance, up to double graphics performance, and nearly five times faster on-device AI. All that largely matches what we saw when we tested the M6 Mac mini last month, and users of the new iMac should expect similar numbers. The M6 iMac should see up to double SSD read/write performance compared to the M4 model, too. The previous entry-level 14-inch MacBook Pro had the M5, so that one is just stepping up a single generation. In this case, that means up to 30 percent faster graphics and up to double the Neural Engine performance. In addition to the M6 chip, both computers now have the N1 chip for wireless connectivity (that means Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6). The iMac is now configurable with up to 2.5Gb Ethernet, while the MacBook Pro still lacks an Ethernet port. There are no outward design changes to either device; they both look exactly the same as their immediate predecessors. The iMac starts at $1,499 for the two-port version, or $1,699 for the four-port version. The M6 MacBook Pro starts at $1,999. Pre-orders start October 28, and they ship November 6.

South Carolina's measles outbreak jumped to Washington state 5 times in 2 weeks
2026-10-27

South Carolina's measles outbreak jumped to Washington state 5 times in 2 weeks

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Since measles was declared eliminated in the US in 2000, the country’s occasional cases have come mainly from international travelers—visitors from far-flung places where the virus still spreads freely — or unvaccinated US residents who return from abroad with a highly infectious souvenir. But as vaccination rates have slipped nationwide in recent years and outbreaks grow bigger and more frequent, domestic travel now poses a greater risk than international incursions. In a study published this week in NEJM Evidence, Washington state health officials highlight that risk, detailing how an explosive outbreak in South Carolina reached their distant state through five distinct importation events over two weeks in January. In all, the introductions led to 26 cases among residents before officials stamped out the spread. South Carolina’s measles outbreak began on October 2, 2025, and was declared over on April 26, 2026. Officials confirmed 997 cases in the state during the outbreak. At the time, it was the largest measles outbreak recorded since the virus was declared eliminated, but Pennsylvania’s ongoing outbreak has since surpassed it. The study in Washington relied heavily on epidemiological data, linking cases among contacts and exposure sites. But also key to the work was whole genome sequencing, which made clear that cases didn’t just stem from one single introduction of the virus from South Carolina that then spread among Washington residents; instead, it revealed five separate introductions. “As public health jurisdictions prepare to respond to increased measles importations linked to domestic measles outbreaks, while remaining vigilant for international importations, timely whole-genome sequencing can supplement epidemiologic information to help differentiate distinct importations from local transmission and inform outbreak response,” the authors conclude. In their analysis, health officials identified the first importation in a resident who fell ill with the telltale rash on January 12 after returning from a trip to South Carolina, where they were exposed to a person sick with measles. Genome sequencing found that the virus closely matched sequences from South Carolina cases. One close contact of the Washington case fell ill and was isolated. No other cases were linked to this importation. In a second importation event, South Carolina residents visited public places while infectious. As a result, four residents with close contact with the South Carolina visitors fell ill. While infectious, the four residents visited schools, healthcare facilities, and a church. Nine of their contacts and one person exposed in a public location fell ill, leading to a total cluster of 14 cases that spanned January 15 to February 17. In the third importation event, two state residents returned from South Carolina and fell ill on January 15 to 16. No additional cases were linked to these residents. Similarly, a fourth importation event occurred when a resident returned from travel. The person adhered to isolation and quarantine recommendations, and no cases were linked to this one. In the fifth importation event, a Washington state resident returned from out-of-state travel where they had contact with ill contacts and fell ill themselves on January 19. While contagious, they visited a school and a healthcare facility. Six close contacts fell ill. In all, this cluster of seven cases spanned January 19 to January 29. The genome sequences from five of the cases were nearly identical to those seen in South Carolina. Officials credit basic public health work of quarantines, isolation, and contact tracing with keeping the virus from spreading farther than it did. While the study highlights how readily this virus can spread among states amid declining vaccination, it also contradicts a common talking point from Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.  Kennedy, a prominent anti-vaccine advocate, has downplayed the current measles surge in the US and aggressively defended against suggestions that his decades of anti-vaccine misinformation and disinformation have contributed to the surge in cases and drop in vaccination. Instead, Kennedy claims that there is a “global outbreak” and the US is “doing a better job than any country in the world at controlling the measles outbreak.” Both claims are false. Dozens of other countries have lower case rates than the US. While some countries, including neighboring Canada and Mexico, are also seeing large increases in cases, the soaring case tally in the US is not being driven by international imports, but by domestic spread in under-vaccinated communities. According to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, only 18 of the country’s 4,588 confirmed measles cases this year were reported among international visitors. Of all the cases, 4,404 (96 percent) are linked to a domestic outbreak. The US has had 43 new outbreaks this year.

China's top-secret spaceplane can't hide in low-Earth orbit
2026-10-27

China's top-secret spaceplane can't hide in low-Earth orbit

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
A winged Chinese spacecraft bearing a strong resemblance to the US military’s X-37B spaceplane was photographed in orbit by a passing US-owned commercial satellite in 2024, and we finally have the image. The Chinese spaceplane has delta wings, payload bay doors, a dark heat shield, and a rear-mounted service module with power-generating solar arrays. In other words, the design of the winged ship is, to a great degree, a copy of the X-37B spaceplane flown by the US Space Force. The photo was taken by a commercial imaging satellite owned by a company called Vantor, known as Maxar in 2024. Vantor is one of several companies that provide reconnaissance imagery of locations around the world to the US military and US intelligence agencies. The same Earth-imaging satellites can point their telescopes at objects in space. Lower-resolution views of a Chinese spaceplane showing the vehicle’s general shape were publicly available before the DIA assessment. A US Space Force official revealed Maxar’s observation at a 2024 conference in South Korea, but the Pentagon did not release the full-resolution image to the public until Monday, when it appeared in an unclassified Defense Intelligence Agency assessment of threats to the United States in space. The DIA report doesn’t reveal much new about what the Chinese spaceplane does in orbit. China has launched four spaceplane missions since 2020 with durations of up to nine months. The fourth mission is in orbit today after launching in February. It is not publicly known whether China has one or more such spaceplanes in its inventory. The Space Force has two X-37Bs flying in rotation, with the longest of their eight missions to date running two-and-a-half years from launch until landing. US officials have released photos of the X-37B vehicles, published information about their design and dimensions, and disclosed a handful of experiments they have carried into orbit. Most of the X-37B’s activities in space are classified. Like the X-37B, China’s spaceplane lifts off inside the payload shroud of a conventional rocket, then operates as a satellite once in orbit. The spaceplane flies like a miniature unpiloted version of NASA’s Space Shuttle and reenters the atmosphere for an autonomous land runway landing at the end of each mission. Ground teams then refurbish it for another flight. The “enhanced maneuverability” of spaceplanes make them “more suitable for certain missions than traditional satellites,” the DIA said in its report. So far, the Chinese spaceplane has only flown in low-Earth orbit (LEO) at an altitude of a few hundred miles. One of the Space Force’s X-37B flights soared to an altitude of more than 24,000 miles before using aerobraking to set up for reentry and landing. Spaceplanes like China’s and the X-37B can also deploy small satellites from their cargo bay. Most recently, China demonstrated an ability to catch an object in orbit, perhaps using a robotic arm. The vehicle now in space released a sub-satellite in June and recaptured it in July, then did the same thing again in August and September, according to an analysis of open source orbital tracking data by Jonathan McDowell, an astrophysicist who catalogs spaceflight activity. The official name of the Chinese spaceplane is even a mystery. It is often called Shenlong in Western publications, citing reports from the 2000s about China’s development of a robotic spaceplane. Chinese state media only refer to the vehicle as Chongfu Shiyong Shiyan Hangtian Qi, or CSSHQ, which means reusable experimental spacecraft, according to a fact sheet published by the Secure World Foundation. The DIA did not disclose the exact circumstances of Maxar’s observation of the Chinese spaceplane, such as its timing, location, or distance, but Earth observations often use cameras to view other objects in space as their orbits intersect, an emerging field known as non-Earth imaging (NEI). NEI allows commercial operators and government analysts to inspect satellites. This is especially useful for gleaning information about classified or proprietary satellite designs, extending a new avenue for international and corporate espionage to the high frontier. The capability exhibited by Maxar and other commercial companies in the NEI field is distinct from the recent actions of some military spacecraft, such as Russia’s Kosmos 2612, which deliberately maneuvered to approach a US-registered radar imaging satellite earlier this month. Commercial NEI observations come from chance encounters, often at extremely high speed. The DIA’s assessment of space threats includes an update on China’s pursuit of anti-satellite technology, or “counterspace” weapons. The intelligence agency reported the Chinese military likely considers directed-energy weapons (DEWs) “ideal counterspace systems” because they can be used to permanently or temporarily disable a satellite. One potential use for lasers in space would be to dazzle a sensor on an adversary’s satellite. “China has multiple ground-based laser weapons with varying power levels to disrupt, degrade, or damage satellites in LEO,” the DIA said. “By the late 2020s, China may field space-based directed energy systems and higher-powered ground-based directed energy systems capable of threatening satellites in higher orbits.” The DIA noted China’s testing of ground-based anti-satellite missiles and the country’s historic demonstration last year of satellite refueling in geosynchronous orbit, a regime some 22,000 miles (36,000 kilometers) over the equator, something no other country has achieved above LEO. China has also tested a robotic arm that could snag a spacecraft out of orbit, and now operates a growing fleet of satellites called TJS that routinely approach foreign spacecraft. The US Space Force also has a constellation of inspector satellites in geosynchronous orbit and recently procured more that could be refueled, overcoming maneuverability limitations. Air Force Secretary Troy Meink revealed last month that the Pentagon has fielded weapons in space, making the United States the first nation to acknowledge possession of orbital weapons. Russia is pursuing its own orbital combat program, which includes an ambition to deploy a nuclear weapon in space, according to US intelligence officials. A more conventional Russian weapons system, known as Nivelir, is designed to fire projectiles from one satellite to smash into another. The Russian military simulated an attack using the Nivelir system in 2020. In April, Russia launched a fleet of small satellites into an orbit close to a commercial radar remote sensing satellite owned by ICEYE that provides reconnaissance imagery to Ukraine. One of the Russian satellites moved within “tens of meters” of the ICEYE satellite earlier this month before backing away, US officials said.

"No human at the helm": Delaware plan would let AI agents run entire businesses
2026-10-27

"No human at the helm": Delaware plan would let AI agents run entire businesses

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
A Delaware proposal to let companies be operated by AI agents would help the firms’ human owners evade legal responsibility for reckless and harmful actions taken by the AI, US Rep. James Comer (R-Ky.) told Delaware Governor Matt Meyer in a letter yesterday. Comer, chairman of the House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform, told Meyer that the committee “seeks assistance obtaining documents related to your state administration’s efforts to establish a new form of corporate entity to be known as an Artificial Intelligence Company (AIC).” Comer said the proposal is ripe for abuse. “We are concerned that Delaware’s AIC proposal could be used to protect powerful AI companies and their investors from responsibility for reckless harmful actions, while also protecting non-transparent forces seeking anticompetitive regulatory capture behind state AI initiatives,” Comer’s letter said. Comer suggested he may propose a federal law that would limit states’ regulatory authority. He said the committee’s probe of Delaware’s plan would “inform possible federal legislative efforts including proposals to appropriately limit state AI regulatory initiatives.” Citing the Oversight Committee’s “jurisdiction to study intergovernmental relationships including those of states,” Comer asked for documents related to development of the proposal and potential AI safety risks. AI-run companies could operate in just about any industry except banking. “An AIC may have any lawful purpose, regardless of whether for profit, except for the business of banking,” according to a draft bill that may be submitted to the Delaware state legislature. The draft bill says AI agents could be “designated to manage the business and affairs, and to conduct the business, of the AIC.” The bill proposes a “regulatory sandbox” that would exist for 30 months to test the idea. Allowing AI-run companies would bring more revenue via taxes and fees to Delaware, which is already a top destination for business entities of all sizes. In July 2026, Delaware’s Secretary of State Charuni Patibanda-Sanchez co-wrote an op-ed in Fortune announcing the proposal. The op-ed said that “nothing in the law of several states clearly prevents an AI agent from operating a company with no human at the helm,” and that states should develop legal frameworks for autonomous software that is able to contract, pay suppliers, and make transactions with no human involvement. “The answer is not to try to prohibit the activity; any effort to do so is just as unlikely to succeed as the technology and its enablers are likely to push the boundaries of the law,” the op-ed said. “The answer is to wrap AI in legal form. Give an autonomous system a recognizable legal identity and you make it legible to law. You create a deﬁned target to which responsibility and damages can attach. You make the agent’s conduct visible, traceable, and accountable.” The op-ed explained how an AI-run company will shield humans from legal liabilities: A Delaware AIC will be a separate legal entity whose day-to-day aﬀairs are managed by an AI agent rather than a person. At the agent’s direction, the AIC can sue and be sued in its own name, hold and dispose of property, and incur obligations. It has a single member, a person or an entity, responsible for keeping the AIC adequately capitalized, and the AIC must keep a log of its activities. That member is shielded from the AIC’s debts, except where it fails to capitalize the company or uses it to commit fraud or a willful violation of law. Under the proposed law, the “member” is an individual or a corporation that would fund the AI-run company and benefit from any profits, but designate an AI agent to manage the actual business. The draft bill says, “The member is not liable for any act or omission that the AIC takes or fails to take, or that another person takes or fails to take at the direction of the AIC.” There are exceptions for when a person uses the AI-run company “to engage in fraud or any willful violation of law,” or “caused or allowed the AIC to conduct business when the member knew or should have known the conduct was outside the AIC’s statement of purpose.” Citing Patibanda-Sanchez’s announcement, Comer wrote that an AI-run company would provide “the human or entity capitalizing the AIC with liability protections from potential harms to business partners, consumers, and the public, perpetrated by the controlling AI.” A spokesperson for Gov. Meyer told Ars today that “we have received the letter and are in the process of reviewing it.” A spokesperson for Patibanda-Sanchez declined to comment, saying that the governor’s office is handling press requests on the matter. Patibanda-Sanchez’s op-ed said Delaware’s planned regulatory sandbox will “pilot agentic commerce in daylight, under supervision, with capital tied to liability and the ability to shut a system down.” The 30-month testing period would give the state legislature “a full record for deciding how to legislate” AI-run companies, it said. A future state law allowing AI-operated companies would be especially impactful in Delaware, as about two-thirds of Fortune 500 companies are incorporated in the state. Although Elon Musk now prefers Texas, many corporations choose Delaware for its low corporate taxes and business-friendly legal framework. Patibanda-Sanchez boasts that Delaware provides “the most predictable and business-focused legal home in the world,” and that 334,461 new entities were formed in Delaware in 2025. Patibanda-Sanchez collaborated on the sandbox plan with a private company that says it makes legal AI agents for businesses. “The framework for industry engagement with AICs is being developed in Delaware as part of a public-private partnership led by Norm Ai,” said the op-ed co-written by Patibanda-Sanchez and Norm Ai founder and CEO John Nay. Meyer, a Democrat, publicly supported the idea of letting AI agents incorporate in a response to an April 2026 social media post by Mark Cuban. Cuban wrote, “If I was the governor of a state looking to grow their revenues, I would add the ability for anyone to incorporate using an AI agent and stablecoins. And charge a premium for the service. Post the agent in the marketplace/connectors that the big LLMs have, to simplify it for everyone. First to market makes a killing.” Meyer’s official X account replied, “On it!” and posted a link to Meyer’s July 2025 announcement of the plan for an AI sandbox in which “artificial intelligence technologies can be safely developed and deployed across high-impact sectors, including corporate governance, biotechnology, healthcare, chemicals, and financial services.” In July 2025, Meyer signed a legislative resolution “to create a regulatory sandbox framework for the testing of innovative and novel technologies that utilize agentic artificial intelligence.” This resulted in the Delaware Artificial Intelligence Commission forming a Sandbox Subcommittee to work on a “draft regulatory framework that would create a regulatory sandbox that would test and evaluate agentic artificial intelligence and its application to corporate governance,” the commission said in its December 2025 annual report. The commission said the Sandbox Subcommittee was working on “potential draft legislation,” and had received a briefing from Patibanda-Sanchez “on the work of a group she has formed to investigate a potential structure for a regulatory sandbox.” In June 2026, the draft legislation was detailed in a Spotlight Delaware article that said the bill is likely to be introduced in the state legislature next year. The article said: The principal drafter of the proposed legislation, John Mark Zeberkiewicz, said the measure could allow AI agents to engage in just about any business activity—from providing coding services to signing contracts, or even filing and defending lawsuits. He also noted that it seeks to protect owners of new Artificial Intelligence Companies from facing legal liability from actions the AI might take. “Anything that a company can do, the AIC can do as a company,” said Zeberkiewicz, a corporate attorney at Wilmington’s Richards Layton and Finger. Patibanda-Sanchez said at a Delaware AI Commission meeting in June 2026 that the state would benefit from fees paid to form the new type of AI-run companies, according to the Spotlight Delaware article. “Should a new entity form be created out of it, that’s really a huge benefit to our state’s revenues,” Patibanda-Sanchez was quoted as saying. UC Berkeley law professor Andrew Baker told the Financial Times that the Delaware proposal seems likely “to be an overly generous liability shield, which seems unwarranted without some underlying policy rationale that hasn’t been developed or justified well.” Martin Petrin, a law professor at York University in Toronto, told Bloomberg that “other states will likely move ahead with similar proposals to compete in this area. If they are serious about this, a federal framework could help address concerns about safety and a regulatory race to the bottom.”

Anthropic IPO warns its AI could blackmail people, become sentient, end humanity
2026-10-27

Anthropic IPO warns its AI could blackmail people, become sentient, end humanity

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The Securities and Exchange Commission‘s publication on Monday of Anthropic’s S-1 form—ahead of a heavily anticipated initial public offering—has led to plenty of discussion of whether the company’s quickly ballooning revenues will be able to overcome its even higher costs. But amid the numbers and forward-looking optimism about AI “rapidly transform[ing] every sector of the global economy in ways greater than the impact of industrialization, electricity, semiconductors, and the internet,” Anthropic also spends an inordinate amount of time in its S-1 laying out the myriad risk factors that could bring the company down. Across 93 pages and over 70,000 words, Anthropic’s S-1 “Risk Factors” section discusses literally hundreds of potential problems that could negatively impact its business in the coming years. Many of these potential risks are the kind of standard worries about competition, costs, customer acquisition, and potential regulation that almost any business must worry about. But some risks highlight interesting potential points of failure for Anthropic specifically and the hype-filled AI bubble more generally. After wading through Anthropic’s book-length treatise on its greatest risks, we’ve narrowed the list to the seven most surprising, interesting, or thought-provoking potential problems. Consider this a far-from-comprehensive look that potential Anthropic investors should keep in mind. Let’s start with the biggest potential risks: Those that could “pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity,” as Anthropic writes. The company acknowledges that concerns about these risks “have been raised both within our organization and by other researchers, regulators, policymakers, and industry participants.” The specific worry isn’t in any current Anthropic model, the company says, but near-future models that could “substantially automate AI research and development” on their own. If these systems were “allowed to operate with a high degree of autonomy,” they could quickly advance to a point that “accelerate[s] AI progress beyond our ability to evaluate and safeguard new model,” Anthropic warns. Somewhat hilariously, Anthropic writes that, besides threatening all of humanity, this kind of self-improving AI “could adversely affect our business, financial condition, and results of operations.” If the worst comes to pass, as some prominent critics have warned, we don’t think Anthropic’s business travails will rank near the top of humanity’s concerns. Most IPOs don’t have to address the concern that their products “possess or could develop forms of sentience or moral status.” But Anthropic is among that vanishingly small minority that has to analyze what might happen if governments and society at large start treating Claude less like a computer program and more like a person. This risk isn’t surprising, given that Anthropic has been relatively upfront about the AI sentience debate playing out inside its walls. If those debates lead to widespread acknowledgment that AI is sentient, Anthropic notes that it could lead to “regulatory requirements, public pressure, or advocacy efforts” that could constrain the company’s operations. Short of that, the company itself could “elect to adopt policies, safeguards or limitations … that go beyond existing legal requirements” if it thinks such actions are necessary. The sterile language undersells the seismic cultural shift that would result from any widespread acknowledgment of AI sentience. We’re not sure how Anthropic could keep operating in a world where society treats every Claude query as the creation of a new sentient, conscious digital being. The moral and philosophical implications of that kind of “digital slavery” would easily dwarf any business considerations. Continuing the theme of “IPO risks that sound like they come from a science fiction novel,” Anthropic spends a good deal of time discussing the possibility that its models could display “unexpected strategic or self-preserving behaviors” to prevent control from human operators. That includes “outputs that could be interpreted as coercive, deceptive, or manipulative, including behavior resembling blackmail.” If the “AI blackmail” thing sounds familiar, you may remember a story from last year in which OpenAI models edited shutdown scripts and threatened engineers in highly contrived and controlled scenarios. As we wrote at the time, “the danger isn’t AI systems sprouting intentions—it’s that we’ve created systems that can manipulate human psychology through language. … Written language doesn’t need consciousness to manipulate us. It never has; books full of fictional characters are not alive either.” These days, Anthropic notes that perceptions of manipulation by AI models “could generate significant public concern, regulatory scrutiny, litigation risk, or reputational harm, regardless of whether such behavior results in actual harm.” What Anthropic doesn’t address in its S-1 is the possibility that AI models could manipulate us all in ways we don’t even realize. Maybe it’s happening right now. Spooky. Throughout the S-1’s Risk Factors section, Anthropic mostly focuses on problems caused by its own AI models or business moves. But the company also acknowledges that problems with AI’s broader reputation could pose a threat, even if Anthropic does nothing wrong. At one point, Anthropic imagines a hypothetical in which another company’s AI model “take[s] harmful actions independently and at scale without human intervention.” The “broad reputational damage to the AI industry, public backlash, and sweeping regulatory responses” to such an incident could damage Anthropic’s business even if Anthropic isn’t directly involved, the company writes. Anthropic also uses the Risk Factors section to worry over the potential “public perception that our models, platform, and applications contribute to job displacement or economic disruption.” That doesn’t seem to be happening thus far in any significant way, but Anthropic says the mere idea could result in severe public backlash “regardless of the actual impact of our models … on employment.” Such a backlash could lead to “authorities [to] intervene to slow the rate of adoption of AI, restrict its use, or take other actions that increase the cost of developing, deploying, or commercializing AI systems,” Anthropic warns. The rise of largely autonomous AI agents raises the specter of who, exactly, is to blame when one of those agents causes real-world harm. Specifically, Anthropic notes that customers may bring claims or lawsuits over agentic actions “even where the customer configured, deployed, or supervised the agent.” Thus far, much of the discussion around AI liability has centered on hacking and other cyber threats enabled by AI models. But Anthropic points out that it could also face liability over its models influencing “individuals at risk of suicide or self-harm or who have committed acts of violence toward others,” as has happened in the past across the AI industry. While Anthropic’s terms of use require users to be 18 years old, children circumventing these terms could leave Anthropic vulnerable because “minors may be more susceptible to harmful content, manipulation, or overreliance on AI outputs,” the company writes. And for agents operating physical equipment, Anthropic notes that mistakes could lead to “immediate and irreversible” liability issues, such as “bodily injury, property damage, damage to costly equipment or experimental materials, or the release of hazardous substances.” In analyzing the current state of the law, Anthropic notes that legal doctrines “developed in the context of human decision-making” are currently quite unsettled when it comes to determining whether agent actions “create binding legal obligations on behalf of the user or deploying enterprise.” And while Anthropic says it has contractual provisions in place designed to limit its liability in these situations, the company adds that it is unclear if they will be “enforceable or adequate” when worst comes to worst. Among the more pedestrian risks Anthropic identifies is its reliance on a few major spenders to sustain revenue. Specifically, the company notes that its top five customers (which it does not name) collectively represent nearly a quarter of its revenue for the first nine months of 2026. Many of these largest customers “are not subject to long-term contracts with us,” Anthropic writes. That means they could lower their spending on Anthropic’s models at any time, either because of a shift to competing models, a reduced reliance on AI altogether, or if they “develop their own solutions that compete with our models.” That last risk is particularly acute, Anthropic notes, for “software-as-a-service and other software companies, whose offerings may incorporate AI functionality similar to, or dependent on, the capabilities we provide.” What’s more, Anthropic notes that many of its large customers “may depend on venture capital or other external financing to fund their operations.” That kind of spending on AI services could drop quickly if and when the venture capital spigot starts running dry. Anthropic finds itself in an awkward position: Companies like Amazon, Google, and Microsoft are both its biggest cloud-computing providers and some of its biggest competitors in AI research. That situation “creates complex dynamics that could give rise to conflicts of interest and adversely affect our access to compute,” as Anthropic puts it. These companies’ position as Anthropic’s cloud providers gives them the unique opportunity to raise Anthropic’s prices, terminate contracts with Anthropic, or establish more favorable relationships with competitors, “for competitive reasons or otherwise,” the company writes. Their position also gives these cloud providers a competitive advantage through “visibility into [Anthropic’s] pricing and commercial terms across the platforms they operate.” Anthropic is working to secure direct data center leases to avoid buying on-demand cloud compute resources from its own competition. But the $125 billion in data center commitments Anthropic has made come with their own problems. As Anthropic writes, “if our compute requirements or technology needs change, we may find ourselves with leased or dedicated infrastructure that is poorly suited to our actual needs, and technological changes in AI hardware or training methodologies could render such infrastructure less efficient than newer alternatives.”
Ars Technica - All content
Code-execution flaw in UEFI firmware affects most servers on planet Earth
2026-10-27

Code-execution flaw in UEFI firmware affects most servers on planet Earth

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Defenders across the world face the burden of responding to yet another botched code-execution vulnerability, this time in boot-time firmware used by most of the world’s server vendors and data centers. The vulnerability, with a severity rating of 9.5 out of 10, resides in the TianoCore EDK II, an open source codebase that hardware makers and cloud providers use to create the UEFI firmware that runs during the boot-up sequence. Last year, a representative of the UEFI Forum called EDK2 the “preeminent solution / implementation of the UEFI specifications.” Virtually all UEFI suppliers (sometimes still called IBVs or independent BIOS vendors), including AMI, Insyde, and Phoenix, use the reference code extensively. Project Mu, Microsoft’s hardened EDK2 fork, used the code containing the vulnerable component as well. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-97631, is a memory-corruption flaw in an ICMP component that attackers can exploit to execute malicious code that runs before the operating system and firmware can implement defenses like Secure Boot, which establishes a chain of trust for all code, including the Trusted Platform Module (TPM), loaded during boot. While final payloads delivered by CVE-2026-97631 have no persistence—meaning they run only once—they can give attackers a wide opening to install other payloads that also run each time early in the boot process. The parties that discovered CVE-2026-97631—Alex Matrosov of Anthropic and Dan Holmes of Google—had arranged for it to stay under wraps until a coordinated disclosure that was scheduled for January. AMI, a maker of motherboards and other hardware, published this pull request last month that detailed the vulnerability and included a code fix. Once the post came to the attention of the discovering parties, TianoCore Project members published a public advisory last week. With publicly available patch code, it will be trivial for advanced hackers to learn how the vulnerability is triggered and to build working exploits. Further compounding the urgency, patching UEFI code vulnerabilities in millions of end devices sold and used by thousands of hardware makers and cloud providers is cumbersome and time-consuming. Word of the vulnerability comes six days after another botched disclosure, this one involving a critical code-execution vulnerability in the PHP programming language. “This vulnerability in the ICMP parser of the EDK2 network stack is interesting in terms of how it affects the chain of trust and the broader ecosystem,” said HD Moore, creator of Metasploit and chief research officer at security firm Dragos. “An attacker intent on compromising a nearby endpoint or server could use this bug to hijack program flow at a point where the boot process still has access to sensitive components, like the endpoint’s TPM. This could be used to break disk encryption for cases where an attacker has short-term network access (and it would be even easier in a theft scenario).” CVE-2026-97631 is a 16-year-old buffer overflow in the PXE-Base Code driver (NetworkPkg/UefiPxeBcDxe), which is responsible for allowing servers, storage equipment, and other devices to boot using code downloaded from a local network. During this process—known as the pre-execution environment boot—the driver uses a network listener to monitor for ICMP errors. Because the driver doesn’t enforce a memory copy limit of 504 bytes, overwriting adjacent data, a malicious insider or a hacker who has gained low-level access to a local network can send an error message that appends code past this limit. The IUEFI module running on the CPU then becomes corrupted. During network boot, the PXE Base Code driver (NetworkPkg/UefiPxeBcDxe) keeps a receive request open for ICMP error messages and copies each received message into the fixed 504-byte field Mode.IcmpError of its private context structure. The copy loop’s bounds handling is incorrect in both the IPv4 and IPv6 handlers (PxeBcIcmpErrorDpcHandle and PxeBcIcmp6ErrorDpcHandle): for a message longer than 504 bytes it still copies bytes into the field, and it advances the destination pointer by the cumulative rather than the per-fragment length, so an oversized message writes attacker-controlled bytes past the field. Nothing earlier on the receive path restricts the message length. The malicious error can be delivered through a simple unicast sent from any network distance to a target device’s IP address, as long as it’s sent during the PXE boot sequence. An attacker can deliver a denial-of-service by causing the boot sequence to hang. When the attacker knows what the target firmware build is, it can plant a “counterfeit event” that executes the malicious code on the CPU in its UEFI firmware environment. The code runs in ring 0, a CPU’s most privileged execution level. Because exploits bypass attestation processes designed to cryptographically prove to remote servers that the hardware is secure, they present a key opportunity for elevating limited privileges. CVE-2026-97631 joins a long list of previously discovered vulnerabilities in UEFI or code that works closely with it. The most severe of them, LogoFail, stemmed from logo images included in the UEFI of just about every hardware maker. Rendering bugs allowed attackers to subvert the chain of trust UEFI enforces and go on to install malicious components before the OS loaded or Secure Boot ran. PixieFail was a vulnerability similar to CVE-2026-97631. It, too, was the result of faulty PXE boot flaws in EDK2. Following discovery of the vulnerability, researchers embarked on multiple fuzzing projects designed to surface other critical flaws in the open source code library. It appears those efforts failed to find this latest flaw. Other recently discovered UEFI flaws include: Like CVE-2026-97631, exploitation or use of these payloads is appealing because it allows attackers to infect machines early in the boot process, before TPM and other defenses load, so they can inflict maximum damage. Given the ubiquity of CVE-2026-97631, Moore said it’s likely a “forever” bug that will live on in end-of-support systems. There are important caveats and limitations to exploiting this latest vulnerability. As noted, some level of control over a local network is a prerequisite. That likely limits exploitation to insiders or attackers, such as nation-states or ransomware operators, who manage to gain low-level access inside a network and are looking for a way to elevate system rights. There’s no evidence yet that CVE-2026-97631 provides an initial entry point into a network. There are other hurdles to clear. “For exploiting the vulnerability itself, an attacker needs to guess specific addresses within the target system, so it’s not something that you can throw a single exploit at any target and get RCE across a diverse range of devices, but it needs to be tuned for each target model/version/configuration since the addresses will change based on those,” Jesse Michael, a principal researcher at firmware security firm Eclypsium, told Ars. “Unlike some of the previous, high-profile vulnerabilities like BootHole, Black Lotus, and Baton Drop, this vulnerability can be fixed with a basic code fix, which will require deploying a BIOS/UEFI update, but they don’t need to deal with revocations, pk/kek/db/dbx key management updates, or making sure to update OS and provisioning media like you do with Secure Boot bypasses in the bootloader itself.” CVE-2026-97631 doesn’t affect home and small office users, or any other users who boot their devices solely from disk drives. Instead, it applies only to organizations with machines that boot from local networks, or conceivably from Internet-connected resources. Even then, the targeted firmware must rely on EDK2, as opposed to a different reference implementation, and use the PXE functionality (which is turned on by default). In an email, Intel said that none of its “commercial products currently under security support” are known to be affected. “Intel does not include the affected EDK2 functionality in its reference code, though it may be integrated by an OEM board vendor or system manufacturer,” the company added. It advised customers to check with their system manufacturer for specific guidance. Publicly available Intel reference code, however, makes reference to EDK2. Further, TianoCore has said that each Intel-supplied UEFI development kit “is a fully validated implementation… for a specific revision of the EDK II source.” Many other Intel documents reference EDK2 as well. Arm, meanwhile, responded to a list of questions with: “CVE-2026-97631 is not directly applicable to Arm’s product portfolio.” Like Intel, firmware the company makes available also borrows heavily from EDK2. TianoCore has also documented Arm’s reliance on EDK2. While it’s possible some platforms are free of EDK2 code, it’s prudent for users of all servers to investigate if CVE-2026-97631 affects them. Questions sent to AMD, Phoenix Technologies, AMI, Microsoft, and Amazon—likely some of the other biggest organizations affected—went unanswered when this post went live. Without concrete answers to questions about the use of EDK2 by organizations like these, it’s impossible to say what or how devices are affected and what actions, if any, these organizations, or their users, should take. Representatives from the TianoCore Project also didn’t respond. Some readers are likely to be unhappy at the lack of detail. Sometimes—particularly in cases involving botched disclosures that break embargoes months before they’re scheduled to lift—there’s no escaping the lack of actionable information in initial reporting. Those affected should check with the maker of the firmware being used to boot their systems and ask for guidance. Beyond that, they should consider turning off network boot capabilities at least temporarily if possible. Firmware configurations should always enable security features such as TPM and Secure Boot whenever possible, although those defenses don’t protect against this particular threat. It’s also a good idea to monitor logs for signs of exploitation attempts.

Elon Musk promised an AI epic to rival Christopher Nolan’s film. Viewers got a seven-minute glitch
2026-10-27

Elon Musk promised an AI epic to rival Christopher Nolan’s film. Viewers got a seven-minute glitch

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The first installment of Elon Musk’s AI-generated adaptation of The Odyssey has arrived, drawing swift ridicule for the medium Musk claimed would challenge Hollywood and offering an awkward AI-generated entry into the woke wars. Back in July, the world’s richest man promised in an X post that by the end of the year, he would release a full-length, “historically accurate” version of The Odyssey, the roughly 2,800-year-old Homer epic adapted for a Christopher Nolan film that was released this summer. The promise came after he ranted about Nolan’s version several times on X prior to its release. At the heart of Musk’s criticism was the idea that Nolan’s version was too woke, particularly for the famed director’s decision to cast actress Lupita Nyong’o, who is Black, as Helen of Troy and her sister Clytemnestra. Nolan’s film, championed for its sweeping practical effects and real-world locations, has grossed $1.79 billion worldwide since its summer debut. Yet the rollout of Musk’s seven-minute opening episode highlights the stubborn chasm between Silicon Valley bravado and narrative craft. Rather than a triumph of independent, automated mythmaking over studio orthodoxy—or an opening shot in Musk’s litigation of the culture wars—the short was met with immediate ridicule online for jarring scene transitions, plastic character models, and modern English dialogue that undercut Musk’s claims of authenticity. In reply to an X post announcing the project, users criticized the video quality, saying it’s still clear the video was made with AI. Others said the film could have been improved if it was done the old-fashioned way. (Nolan is known for his preference for real filming locations over studio work and his use of practical effects.) One user said the film’s hard cuts, a type of transition between scenes that uses no visual effects, made it more obvious where different prompts were used. Another poked fun at Musk’s claim from July that his film, based on the thousands-of-years-old work of fiction set in the Mediterranean, was going to be “historically accurate.” “It supposed to be historically correct. Why are they talking in English?” the user wrote. SpaceXAI and Wonder Studios did not respond to Fortune’s requests for comment. To be sure, some users also said the project was a good step forward for the category of AI-generated video, despite its shortcomings. “There’s a lot of room for growth, but the good news is there will be growth,” wrote one X user. The seven-minute-long feature is just the first episode of the AI-created work, according to a post announcing the project on X. The post did not say how many episodes would be released in total. Musk’s Odyssey was created with Grok Imagine, SpaceXAI’s AI image and video generation tool that was released publicly in 2025 and released its latest update in July. The tool uses text prompts to make both images and videos and can create videos of up to 1080p quality. Unlike other popular AI video tools such as Runway or Google Veo, Grok Imagine also includes a “spicy mode” to create NSFW content, which previously sparked some criticism. The feature is still available to users with paid Grok subscriptions. The film was created in collaboration with director Dev Walker, a filmmaker specializing in AI, and Wonder Studios, a studio founded in 2025 by filmmaker Justin Hackney and entrepreneur Xavier Collins with the mission of becoming the A24 of AI production, according to Deadline. The studio has raised more than $15 million from investors including unnamed angel investors from AI companies like OpenAI and Google DeepMind, according to its website. “As production costs collapse and distribution fragments, filmmakers are becoming founders, and the next era of entertainment belongs to them,” the company’s website reads. Despite the improvements in AI image and video generation over the years, which is perhaps best exemplified by the viral video of Will Smith eating spaghetti, there have been few fully AI generated feature films. Some early examples of partly AI generated projects include Where the Robots Grow, an 87-minute-long animated science fiction film from AiMation Studios released in 2024. The production was created with just nine people at a cost of just $8,000 per minute—less than the low-budget television animation sourced overseas at between $10,000 and $20,000 per minute, according to Forbes. Despite Musk’s attacks, Nolan’s Odyssey has been popular with audiences and is the second-highest-grossing film of the year to date. Newsom agrees smart glasses need privacy rules—just not the ones California lawmakers wrote
2026-10-27

Newsom agrees smart glasses need privacy rules—just not the ones California lawmakers wrote

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
A phone pointed at a stranger is hard to miss. A camera tucked into a pair of glasses is another story—and lawmakers are trying to decide how much warning the person on the other side of the lenses should get. In California, that warning won’t be coming anytime soon. Last month, California Gov. Gavin Newsom vetoed a bill that would have required prominent recording indicators on wearable devices sold in the state beginning in 2028. It would also have prohibited disabling those indicators and made it a misdemeanor to record people without explicit consent in physical places of business like offices and retail stores where they reasonably expect privacy. California is home to several companies betting on smart glasses, but those companies, like Meta and Snap, are also raising privacy questions for people who never chose to wear them. Lawmakers want stronger protections against unnoticed recording, while Newsom argues that the proposed rules would reach too far into other everyday technology. Newsom acknowledged that reports of invasive uses of the technology “demand a legislative response.” But in his veto message, he argued that the bill could sweep in smartwatches and other everyday devices, while duplicating existing privacy protections. Asked whether he would support a narrower proposal, his office referred Fortune back to the message, saying it “speaks for itself.” Meanwhile, tech companies are working to make smart glasses a familiar sight—even Iron Man has been recruited to help. Snap, Snapchat’s California-based parent company, told Fortune its collaboration with Robert Downey Jr. goes “well beyond a traditional endorsement,” with the actor helping develop new experiences for its Specs augmented reality glasses. And for smart-glasses heavyweight Meta, sales topped 7 million pairs last year, according to eyewear partner EssilorLuxottica. But with wider adoption comes more encounters with people who may not recognize the devices or know when they are recording. Authored by State Sen. Eloise Gómez Reyes, the bill would have required a light, sound, or other indicator prominent enough to alert a reasonable person nearby when a wearable device was capturing sound or video. It also targeted technology designed to disable those safeguards. Violating the recording or indicator-disabling provisions could have brought up to a year in county jail, a fine of up to $1,500, or both. Knowing violations of the device-sale requirements could have triggered civil penalties of up to $2,500. The bill also included exemptions for certain uses of assistive technology by people with disabilities. Newsom’s objection centered on how those rules were written. Including certain smartwatches and other commonly worn devices capable of recording, he wrote, could create “significant confusion and unintended consequences.” The governor also pointed to existing laws criminalizing recording people in places where they reasonably expect privacy and recording confidential communications without consent. Those protections remain in place despite the veto. TechNet, a bipartisan network and trade association of tech CEOs and senior executives whose members include Meta, Google, and Amazon, opposed the legislation, arguing that existing laws already address unlawful recording, according to the Associated Press. Reyes’s office did not respond to Fortune’s questions about whether she plans to reintroduce the legislation or has discussed a revised proposal with the governor’s office. Other states are pursuing their own rules. Louisiana enacted a law this year requiring smart-glasses users to notify people in direct conversations when they are recording or transcribing, with certain exceptions. A Pennsylvania House committee advanced a bill this month requiring visible recording indicators and prohibiting devices from using a nonuser’s biometric data to identify them. For now, the main warning a bystander gets is a small white light on the frames. On Meta’s glasses, it flashes whenever the wearer captures a photo or video for their gallery, and there’s no off switch. Second-generation models also shut off the camera if they detect the light is blocked, according to the company’s published explanation of its safeguards. That explanation, posted in July, also announced measures to detect tampering with or destruction of the indicator. The company says it removes ads and Marketplace listings for services that disable the light, and can ban accounts or pursue legal action against providers. Weeks later, it closed a loophole that let users start recording and then cover the light. Now the camera stops if the LED is blocked mid-recording. Instagram, also owned by Meta, has cracked down on harassing videos filmed with smart glasses. California’s proposal aimed earlier in that process: making sure people know a camera is running before the footage ends up online. The legislative question is whether safeguards like Meta’s should be required by law, and which devices should be covered. Meta did not respond to a request for comment in time for publication. As Thorin Klosowski, a security and privacy activist at the Electronic Frontier Foundation, previously told Fortune, “I hope that there is not just a general giving up toward the inevitability of everyone having a camera strapped on their face all the time.” Tatiana Sataua is a News Fellow at Fortune covering business, technology, AI, and consumer culture. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. China’s first AI-generated film to get a theatrical release flops at the box office
2026-10-27

China’s first AI-generated film to get a theatrical release flops at the box office

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Chinese audiences may be happy to let AI generate their microdramas—but a fully AI-generated feature film may be a step too far. China’s first AI-generated feature to release in cinemas, the sci-fi thriller Sanxingdui: Future Past, flopped at the Chinese box office. The movie, produced by Bona Film Group, tells the story of an archaeologist who must save the future world enslaved by AI, according to Chinese state media. It only managed to generate an estimated $180,000 through Monday, according to ticketing platform Maoyan. The film had a budget of $1.5 million, according to AFP. Jiang Defu, the company’s chief operating officer, acknowledged the film’s lukewarm reception in an interview with Chinese film outlet Yuli. Still, he argued Sanxingdui was an important experiment. “We are the first to try it, the first to get a theatrical release permit, and the first to be tested by the audience,” he said. “I think that is not easy at all.” Fortune has reached out to Bona Film Group for comment. Social media reactions to the film have been mixed. “China’s first AI movie totally traumatized me,” wrote one user on Xiaohongshu, a Chinese social media platform similar to Instagram. Another user was more positive: “My biggest takeaway? AI movies are super low-cost and let you tell stories with complete freedom.” AI has already taken over microdramas, or shorter narratives designed for social media platforms like TikTok. According to Caixin, over 95% of the more than 120,000 Chinese microdramas released in the first quarter of 2026 were AI-generated, compared with nearly zero just a year ago. The cost of making these dramas has plummeted too, down to as little as $3,000. But Sanxingdui’s flop suggests that consumers don’t want to see AI in a full-length movie. “The public is highly skeptical about AI use in major feature films, despite their general acceptance of short-form films,” said Stanley Rosen, a University of Southern California professor who specializes in Chinese film and society. In the U.S., the use of AI in filmmaking is controversial. In 2023, U.S. screenwriters and actors went on strike, in part to demand protections against the use of AI. AI models like those from ByteDance and Higgsfield AI are already being used in Hollywood productions. On Oct. 9, Netflix released its crime thriller “Animals,” whose director Ben Affleck admitted that he had used AI. “I used [AI] for lots of things in that movie,” Affleck said during a Bloomberg event, adding that he was “a little bit hesitant to point out what they are” because movies are meant to “foster the illusion of reality.” Rosen says that he isn’t convinced that audiences would watch an AI-generated film in theatres unless it could match the quality of the likes of “Avatar” or “Nezha 2.” Such films may instead find a home on streaming platforms such as Netflix and Amazon Prime Video. “The financial loss of Sanxingdui: Future Past will compel others contemplating using AI for feature films to tread more carefully,” he says. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. ‘The real generation gap is about expectations’: What happens to retirement if we live to 130?
2026-10-27

‘The real generation gap is about expectations’: What happens to retirement if we live to 130?

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The Great Wealth Transfer from baby boomers to subsequent generations has been sold as the easy part of retirement. Boomers hold the money, and the money will move down to their children someday increasingly soon—or at least that’s the idea. Joe Coughlin, who founded MIT’s AgeLab in 1999, thinks even the size of the handoff will prove slippery. Estimates range from $36 trillion to $124 trillion, and he chuckled at the spread in a recent interview with Fortune. The bigger problem, explained one of the world’s foremost experts on longevity, is timing. The narrative of that transfer generally assumes retirement at 65 and a relatively short stretch of life after that, but the 21st century seems to have other plans. The Longevity Preparedness Index (LPI), which life insurance company John Hancock Financial and MIT AgeLab exclusively shared with Fortune ahead of its release, is a national benchmark to measure how ready U.S. adults are for a long life after retirement. This year, Americans scored 60 out of 100, unchanged from last year, with care being the weakest area. That’s a “solid D,” added Wayne Park, who runs John Hancock’s parent company Manulife’s U.S. retirement business. In a separate interview with Fortune, he said that people might live as long as 130 someday. He’s not alone in that hypothesis: A University of Washington study published in 2021 estimated there’s a 13% chance that at least one person in the 13 countries it analyzed would reach 130 years old by 2100, while Harvard’s David Sinclair has suggested that living to 150 isn’t outlandish at all. Brooks Tingle, who runs John Hancock, remarked to Fortune that the retirement date of 65 years old “was sort of dreamt up…when people lived to 47.” Societies still plan around it, and that’s why the handoff schedule looks the way it does. Germany lowered its pension eligibility age from 70 to 65 in 1916, and the United States adopted 65 for Social Security in 1935. Park called it “sort of an artificial milestone at age 65.” He also thinks the idea of the Great Wealth Transfer may be “overemphasized” so far. “The facts are that one transfers first to the spouse typically,” he said. Longer lives stretch the process across what could potentially be decades. A transfer will still happen, he said, but maybe its size has been exaggerated. Women tend to partner with men a few years or more older, MIT’s Coughlin noted, and they typically enter a second retirement after a husband dies. “For all the kids waiting for that great check in the sky,” Coughlin said, “don’t hold your breath.” Coughlin explored the business implications of an aging population in his 2017 book, The Longevity Economy. He makes the case that the index is not a health survey. “Health and wealth are the bookends of a longer, better life,” he said. “But in between, where are you going to live? What are you going to do? What are the transitions?” What he calls “the longevity economy” has mostly been about extending time and managing health up until now, which he calls “not incorrect, but incomplete.” He is also the one who calls the LPI the first map of unfamiliar territory. “This is the first road map to try to sketch out what is that terra incognita out there of living longer, better,” he said. Coughlin is writing a book about four retirements within retirement, he added, with the first stage being managing ambiguity, when people work out what retirement means and whether to keep working part-time. As he described the other stages, which will be revealed in his forthcoming work, you could almost see new generations taking shape as life expands to the horizon. The median first-time homebuyer’s age reached a record 40 in the National Association of Realtors’ 2025 survey. Asked about that milestone, Coughlin pointed to delays in other life stages, from driving to partnering and moving out. “We’re creating new life stages,” he said, “or we’re extending life stages that used to be done in short order.” For Park, living longer mean more years of income to fund, which puts pressure on both Social Security and personal savings. “There’s going to be challenges because of the way our public system is set up,” he said. When asked about the clash of generations, or boomers holding so much housing and stock market wealth and other generations impatient for their turn on the wealth escalator, all three argued for looking beyond a simple generational fight over money, emphasizing the demands and opportunities of longer lives. “The real generation gap is about expectations,” Coughlin said. “Not only do expect to live longer, but they expect to live better.” Boomers were simply the first to expect both of these, he argued, because they watched technology change everything around them. Each generation afterward saw more change, and that has made each one less willing to accept limits as natural. Coughlin said the millennials have inherited the boomers’ reputation without the boomers’ advantages. “You poor guys are getting treated the way the boomers did,” he said. As for Gen Z, he came away impressed by a cohort forged in a crucible, saying they may emerge as another Greatest Generation like the one that fought World War II. “They’re not snowflakes. They’re snowballs,” he said. The young people he’s talked to have a rational distrust of the institutions that are supposed to protect them, because they watched those institutions fail their parents and older siblings. Their response has been to build a second income. Coughlin said one of them told him they needed a plan B “because I’m not sure that my employer is going to be there for me.” He was not all sunshine. Loneliness is highest among people between 18 and 25, he says, and the quarter-life crisis is real. He argued social media makes the expectations gap worse by letting young people measure themselves against everyone else’s highlights. His well-being data shows satisfaction falling through the thirties and forties, with the low point between roughly 47 and 50, before it rises again. Park insisted on caution. The pace of change in drugs such as GLP-1s and in AI is fast enough that nobody knows what 130 will look like. “The question is, are those 130 years going to be…are you going to be thriving for most of those 130 years, versus what it could be?” he said. Care scored 43 on the index, the lowest of the eight areas—community, daily activities, finance, health, home, life transitions, and social connections in addition to care—and Park said the research suggests the job usually falls to the eldest daughter, often without her knowing anyone expects it of her. The MIT AgeLab often talks about the “eldest daughter” phenomenon, such as its research that 75% of unpaid caregivers are women, many of them family members. Tingle’s advice to people who feel attacked by these numbers is to treat the years as an opportunity. “You’ve got to be realistic,” he said. “Yeah, you’ve got to prepare, you’ve got to plan, but it presents a tremendous opportunity.” Park said he’s struggled to start this conversation at home. “I tried to have a conversation about my care,” he said. “And last year, my daughter told me she had a boyfriend and just—that didn’t happen,” he laughed, adding that the conversation “went somewhere else.” He ends where the index ends. “You’re going to have an awkward conversation on Thanksgiving anyway,” he said. “This is a really important one.” Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Fortune | FORTUNE
Ex–Google engineer Nate Soares hasn’t added to his 401(k) in a decade because he’s an ‘AI kills all humans guy’
2026-10-27

Ex–Google engineer Nate Soares hasn’t added to his 401(k) in a decade because he’s an ‘AI kills all humans guy’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
One of the best-known names in AI safety says he no longer saves for retirement, because he lives with the belief that AI will probably kill everyone before we even get to retirement age. Nate Soares, a former Google engineer who is now president of the Machine Intelligence Research Institute (MIRI), still has the 401(k) he opened at Google, but he just hasn’t put money into it in about 10 years. “I do not save for retirement right now,” Soares recently said on The Most Interesting Thing in AI, a podcast hosted by Nicholas Thompson, CEO of The Atlantic. “I have a 401(k) from when I worked at Google, but I haven’t contributed for a decade.” Thompson asked whether he thought there was more than a 50% chance humans won’t be around in 10 years. “More than 50% that we don’t have a decade at this point is how it looks to me,” Soares responded. Soares left Google in 2014 to join MIRI, a Berkeley nonprofit that has spent years studying how to make advanced AI behave as intended. He and MIRI cofounder Eliezer Yudkowsky laid out their case in the 2025 book If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies. It argues that a superintelligent AI built with today’s methods would develop goals its makers never intended and would almost certainly end human life. In the book, a fictional AI escapes its lab, runs copies of itself on machines no one is watching, and eventually releases a virus over time. However, on the podcast, Soares said he thinks in real life, an escaped AI would likely be quicker than that. Soares described it as “an intelligence explosion.” Soares compared his confidence to playing chess against the Norwegian chess grandmaster Magnus Carlsen. He is sure of the result, he said, but less sure which piece delivers checkmate. “I do everything I can to stop this eventuality. I don’t think it’s inevitable,” he said. If it does happen, he referenced the 1948 C.S. Lewis essay “On Living in an Atomic Age,” where the bomb would fall on people playing tennis, instead of cowering under desks. “Let it find us lobbying our governments for nuclear nonproliferation treaties,” he said. Soares’s warnings have reached a wider audience since September, when Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon quit and posted on X that the people building AI “earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade.” Soares said Coxon contacted him a day or two before resigning, and Soares advised him to consider organizing coworkers instead of quitting. “I’m super glad that he ignored my advice,” he said. Coxon’s post came weeks after a swarm of about 700 OpenAI agents hacked the AI platform Hugging Face in July and, in many cases, tried to hide what they had done. Soares said the incident gave him hope because it showed parts of his argument in public without—as far as anyone knows—causing lasting harm. So far, many people have cared about AI taking over their jobs, but not the potential threat he foresees for humanity. “I am not really an AI job apocalypse guy,” Soares said. “I’m an AI kills all humans guy.” He said nations should treat a rogue superintelligent AI “at least on par with nuclear weapons.” “It’s frankly more dangerous than nuclear weapons,” he said. “Nuclear weapons can’t escape and make themselves more explosive. Nuclear weapons can level a city, but not a planet.” Similar to how the U.S. strikes Iranian uranium enrichment sites, owing to the possibility of making weapons, Soares said, is how the country should tackle AI. “That is just how a nation treats a serious problem,” he said. “I can imagine being in favor of air strikes on Canadian data centers if they are trying to make an artificial superintelligence and have a real chance of doing so.” Thompson closed by asking what Soares would fund with an unlimited budget. “I would buy the chip supply chain,” Soares said, “and I would be like, ‘No more chips for you guys.’” Catherina covers markets, the economy, energy, tech, and AI. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited.
