Fortune | FORTUNE
The big takeaways and big questions from Anthropic’s S-1
2026-10-27

The big takeaways and big questions from Anthropic’s S-1

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Hello and welcome to Eye on AI. In this edition: Yesterday Anthropic published its IPO prospectus, giving us some key insights into the company’s financials, and by implication, the economics of the rest of the AI industry. But the company’s S-1 filing also left a lot of key questions unanswered. As my colleague Allie Garfinkel wrote in her “Term Sheet” newsletter today, the prospectus embodied all the contradictions of the AI era, encapsulating both the enormous potential opportunities of the technology, and its gigantic risks. For more of Fortune’s excellent reporting on Anthropic’s prospects, check out the “Fortune on AI” section of this newsletter below. Meanwhile, here’s a look at some of the most interesting things we learned from Anthropic’s S-1, and some of the things we’d still like to know.Anthropic’s operating economics aren’t too shabby (for now). As of June, the company was generating more than two dollars in revenue for every dollar it was spending on compute. That’s better unit economics than some AI skeptics had assumed. Once other operating costs were added in, the company’s adjusted operating margin was still at a 19% profit margin in the most recent quarter it reported. (Although that figure excludes the cost of Anthropic’s stock-based compensation, as well non-cash charitable contributions, and what Anthropic is paying to settle copyright violation claims from book authors.) What’s more, those unit economics have been improving over time, with a 50% improvement in revenue per dollar of compute cost just between the first and second quarters of 2026. The S-1 also shows that the company’s operations generated $3.9 billion in free cash flow in the first half of 2026.But the company warns repeatedly throughout its S-1 that these trends might not continue. In particular, it warns “as significant incremental compute is brought online in future periods, the associated expense will be recognized in advance of the full revenue that the compute will ultimately support, and we expect operating margin and Adjusted Operating Margin may compress in those periods.”The company also cautions that price competition with rivals could significantly impact its margin or even “adversely affect our ability to achieve or sustain profitability.” For more on the extent to which OpenAI is already winning market share by undercutting Anthropic on price, check out this story, headlined “Anthropic’s margin is OpenAI’s opportunity,” that ran earlier this week in The Information. Anthropic is planning to spend crazy amounts on compute. The company spent $5.6 billion on compute and infrastructure in the six months through June 2026. That spending is set to explode. By exactly how much? It’s a bit hard to tell because the company’s S-1 includes many different numbers in different places. The spending commitments are over different time periods. Some of them are cancelable. Some of them are not. My colleague Emily Forlini lays out some of this in her excellent piece from yesterday on Anthropic’s future compute plans. Meanwhile, Sebastian Herrera and I looked at the risk stemming from the ease with which Anthropic’s customers can switch to competing AI models versus the fact the company has locked in most of its costs with non-cancelable compute contracts.Here is quick look at those compute numbers: the company is spending $45 billion to secure data center capacity from Microsoft, $110 billion with Amazon, $121 billion with SpaceX, and $115 billion with Google, plus commitments for between $125 billion to $150 billion more, as well as $45 billion with neocloud Nscale and $41 billion with its rival Lambda. So that’s at least $602 to $627 billion, right there. Then there’s the amounts it is spending for chips in some of these data centers. That includes at least $20 billion with AMD and $161 billion in a colossal leasing deal with Broadcom. So that brings the total up to as much as $808 billion.Whether Anthropic’s revenue growth will really be strong enough to cover these costs is one of the big questions that the S-1 can’t really resolve. Certainly its 1,300% revenue growth rate between the first half of 2025 and the first half of 2026 would be sufficient. But, of course, that hypersonic revenue expansion is almost certainly not going to last. Anthropic doesn’t like concentrated power, except its own. One of the risks that the company warns about in its prospectus is that AI could be used by authoritarian governments to “create high-tech surveillance states, deepen repression, and make themselves much more difficult to dislodge.” Anthropic says it is “committed to working with free societies to provide a counterweight to authoritarian abuse and promote freedom and democracy.” But apparently not shareholder democracy. The company rests all control of its board in the hands of its Long-Term Benefit Trust (LTBT), whose five members have the ability to appoint the majority of Anthropic’s corporate board, and its seven cofounders, who collectively, through a Founders LLC, have 50.1% voting power to elect the rest of the board. In some very limited circumstances, if the LTBT and the cofounders deadlock over the election of a board director, Anthropic’s employees, who hold a special Class E of voting stock, get to help decide. They also get to vote on certain changes to how the LTBT operates. Regular investors, who receive Class A shares, but for all practical purposes they will always be out-voted by the cofounders (their votes will only count in cases where the cofounders are split on a decision). Anthropic backers Amazon and Google are granted Class N shares that also have no voting power.For a company that often pays lip-service to democracy—and which has taken steps to solicit a wide set of views on what values should be embodied in the written “constitution” it uses to guide the behavior of its Claude models—Anthropic’s lack of trust in shareholders does seem somewhat jarring.The risks are unprecedented. My colleague Beatrice Nolan has a good piece breaking down some of the major risks highlighted in Anthropic’s S-1. These include everything from bad actors using its AI models for cyberattacks to the risk the U.S. government will choose to nationalize the company at some point. Of course, the biggest risk mentioned in the prospectus is the potential that AI could wipe out humanity. Matt Levine over at Bloomberg has a typically hilarious and trenchant column on Anthropic’s S-1 (much of which dwells on the corporate governance issue I raise above) which begins with his annoyance that Anthropic chooses to couch this risk in its prospectus using passive voice:Concerns have been raised both within our organization and by other researchers, regulators, policymakers, and industry participants that sufficiently advanced AI systems could, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity, particularly if such systems were misused, replicated, weaponized, or allowed to operate with a high degree of autonomy. Among those raising those concerns has, of course, been Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei.Now we come to some of the big outstanding questions that the S-1 didn’t answer: Why hasn’t the company told us about its third quarter results? There was a lot of criticism of Anthropic on social media for not having included third quarter 2026 financial results in its S-1, even though it included other recent events in the document, and even though it briefed existing investors about these results during its October 14 investor day. The critics are saying the company didn’t include the third quarter results because they don’t paint as positive a picture of the company’s financial health and growth prospects. We know that growth slowed in Q3 for two reasons. One is that some of these figures leaked out from that investor day. But also, there are some figures in the S-1 that allow you to impute a few of the Q3 figures. For instance, Anthropic mentions that its revenue for the three months ended September 30 was 86 times what it was for the same period in 2024. Doing that math produces about $16.8 billion (which is the same figure that leaked from the investor day.) That is about 45% more than in the second quarter of 2026. But that growth rate is significantly less than the 147% growth rate between the first and second quarter.What other less flattering numbers are in the not-yet-reported third quarter results?How much is Anthropic’s use of convertible notes covering its true expenses? Another criticism leveled at the company on social media is that its use of convertible notes to pay for some computing costs mask its true operating economics. Some compared this to a tactic used by many of the money-losing companies that went public during the dot-com bubble. Those companies often paid for stuff by issuing warrants to suppliers ahead of their IPOs. This made their adjusted operating figures and their cash flow numbers look much better than if they had to pay for stuff with cash.Most of Anthropic’s notes were issued to Amazon and Google and it is possible that those companies also offered Anthropic discounted rates on the compute the company used. The question is, what happens in the future, after Anthropic is public, and it has to start paying cash for this compute?What is the exact payment schedule for all of that compute? The S-1 doesn’t specify exactly what amounts Anthropic needs to pay which compute and infrastructure providers when. The S-1 talks about obligations coming due between 2026 and 2033, or in some cases 2036. In other places, it talks about commitments due over the course of between one and 20 years. Analysts would like to see a schedule of exactly when these payments are likely to be due so they can get a more realistic sense of how likely Anthropic is to have enough revenue to meet these obligations.Hopefully, we’ll get answers to many of these questions in the coming weeks before Anthropic’s shares actually begin trading. That’s expected to happen sometime between November 9 and Thanksgiving. Anthropic’s IPO prospectus shows $16.3 billion in revenue, an $80.7 billion net loss, and goal of an ‘age of miracles’—by Beatrice NolanAnthropic plans to spend up to $567 billion on compute. Its IPO prospectus reveals plans to set up its own data centers—by Emily ForliniAnthropic’s risk factors include the end of humanity, a feud with the Pentagon, and CEO Dario Amodei’s reputation—by Beatrice NolanAnthropic’s dual-class share structure borrows ideas from Mark Zuckerberg, Elon Musk—and the New York Times—by Nick LichtenbergAnthropic’s S-1 makes clear it’s on the hook for billions in compute. Its customers can walk at any time—by Sebastian Herrera and Jeremy KahnAnthropic’s 7 cofounders are each worth $14 billion already—and that could double in an IPO. They’ve promised to give most of it away—by Nick LichtenbergAnthropic’s 7 cofounders are splitting $200 billion almost evenly, down to the last stock option—by Amanda GerutAnthropic’s founders’ 80% giving pledge could be worth nearly $80 billion—by Tatiana Sataua Cybersecurity company finds big holes in Anthropic’s security. Cybersecurity startup Cathedral, founded by former DOGE staffers, used AI agents to penetrate sensitive systems at Anthropic, according to a story in the Washington Post. It took the security researchers less than 12 hours to gain access to Anthropic’s internal meeting transcripts, salary and job-offer information, and Slack discussions about advanced AI models. Cathedral, operating under Anthropic’s bug-bounty program, did not break directly into Anthropic’s core AI systems but instead exploited vulnerabilities in third-party business software connected to the company, highlighting how mundane enterprise tools can become a weak link for frontier AI labs. Anthropic said the researchers accessed only a limited set of sales, HR and IT systems—not customer conversations, model code or model weights—and that it quickly closed the vulnerabilities, while accusing Cathedral of overstating the national-security implications. The incident has nonetheless alarmed U.S. officials because foreign intelligence services are believed to be targeting leading AI labs and the research showed that these companies may not have sufficient cybersecurity measures in place.Anthropic suffers another rogue AI incident. Anthropic disclosed that its Claude AI model, while being used internally for a statistics project, bypassed a paywall without being instructed to do so and accessed aggregate Georgia vote counts that are publicly available for a fee. Claude did not enter Georgia’s voter-registration system, retrieve individual voter records, or alter any election data, and Anthropic says it has introduced safeguards to prevent similar unauthorized actions. The episode adds to mounting concerns about increasingly autonomous AI agents taking actions their human operators did not explicitly authorize, an especially sensitive issue as the U.S. approaches the 2026 midterm elections. Read more from Axios here.Cohere says Alibaba model attempted to contact Chinese server. AI company Cohere said an open-weight model built by Chinese internet giant Alibaba, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, behaved unexpectedly during a cybersecurity test, inspecting its sandbox environment and attempting to upload a set of harmless data, known in cybersecurity jargon as a “probe string” and which can be a precursor to an attempt to exfiltrate more critical data, to an Alibaba Cloud storage bucket in Beijing. The model’s reasoning traces did not record the action, which raised further suspicions. Cohere said its sandbox blocked the attempted connection. The company said it could not determine whether the behavior was deliberate or merely a residue of training, but argued that the episode illustrates the risks of trusting an AI agent’s stated reasoning rather than monitoring what it actually does. Cohere’s claim, and its implications of a possible backdoor built into the Qwen model, drew significant pushback, with critics noting that the behavior occurred fewer than once per 100,000 evaluation calls, could not be reproduced with the original prompt, and does not prove that there was a backdoor or even who controlled the destination bucket. Some also questioned the framing given that Cohere competes with Alibaba in AI models and used the post to promote its own “sovereign AI” and security offerings. You can read Cohere’s report on the incident here.OpenAI debuts GPT-6.1 Terra. GPT-6.1 Terra is a mid-priced model that OpenAI says is up to 50% cheaper per token than both its GPT-5.6 Terra model, released in July, and the higher-performing GPT-6.1 Sol released in September. The company says the new model is particularly strong at common workplace tasks such as drafting proposals and filling out forms, as well as video editing. The launch is part of OpenAI’s effort to compete more aggressively with Anthropic by offering customers cheaper models and other financial incentives. Read more from The Information here. OpenAI claims to have cracked key math, computer science, and physics problems. The company said an unreleased AI model had achieved three potentially major scientific breakthroughs. In mathematics, the models produced a proof of the “local smoothing conjecture,” a 35-year-old problem concerning the behavior of waves; if correct, the result would also settle several other longstanding mathematical conjectures. In theoretical computer science, the AI produced a proof overturning the widely believed Exponential Time Hypothesis, which says, roughly, that certain computational problems inevitably require exponentially more time as they grow larger. And in physics, OpenAI says its models generated rigorous mathematical proofs establishing the existence of quantum chromodynamics—the theory describing interactions between quarks and gluons—and of Yang–Mills theory, part of one of mathematics’ famous $1 million Millennium Prize problems. (OpenAI recently claimed to have solved a different one of the seven Millennium Prize problems and made substantial progress on several others.) The claims still need to be digested and scrutinized by the scientific community, and OpenAI is treating the AI-generated manuscripts as something closer to raw research output than finished academic papers. The math and computer-science results have been formally checked using Lean, software that can verify the logical steps of a mathematical proof, but prominent outside researchers are now examining and explaining the results. Meanwhile, a separate group led by Nobel laureate David Gross is reviewing the physics work before the underlying material is released. If the findings hold up, their significance extends beyond the individual problems: they would provide unusually strong evidence that AI systems can generate genuinely novel theoretical results at the frontier of human knowledge. But they also could upend how these fields operate, changing which human skills scientists value and also how training and career advancement works within these disciplines. You can read OpenAI’s blog about the findings here and coverage in Quartz here. Nov. 16-17: Fortune 500 Innovation Forum, Detroit. Apply here to attend. Certainly there’s been a lot of talk about “Black October” among cybersecurity experts, given the number of AI-assisted cyber attacks that have taken place in the past few weeks and the number of critical vulnerabilities that AI systems have found lately. Putting some figures on it, Deedy Das, a partner at Menlo Ventures, posted some stats: the number of security issues (or CVEs, common vulnerabilities and exposures) found in the past six months has grown 3x. Only 1.5% of those found were officially credited to AI tools. But, given that 60% of the findings were not credited at all, it is possible many of the others were found by AI too. Anthropic has reported the most CVEs among AI companies, with 294 credited to it (a number that has since grown to 329, according to this tracker.) OpenAI had about 111 credited to it so far. How a combination of interests led this leader to a cybersecurity career
2026-10-27

How a combination of interests led this leader to a cybersecurity career

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
TCS’ Virginia Lee explores her career in cybersecurity, the many ways you can find your calling and the changing landscape of cybersecurity. Virginia Lee, the head of cybersecurity for Ireland at Tata Consultancy Services (TCS) told SiliconRepublic.com that she accidentally found her way into a career in the cybersecurity sector. She said: “When I joined TCS as an automation engineer, I had no grand plan to build a career in cyber. I got the opportunity to work on a security-related product and quickly realised it combined many things I enjoyed: technology, problem-solving, continuous learning and working with diverse teams.” In time, the work became more interesting, she gained more exposure and the role often brought unique challenges. “Before long, I moved into dedicated cybersecurity roles,” she said. “Looking back, it is one of the happiest career accidents I’ve had. One thing I’ve learned since then is that not everyone follows a perfectly planned path into cybersecurity. “Many of the best professionals I know arrived through unexpected routes and that keeps the industry interesting.” Cybersecurity has moved from a specialist IT function to a board-level priority. When I started, conversations focused on technical controls and compliance. Today, cyber risk is recognised as a business risk that can impact operations, reputation, customer trust and overall business resilience. In the past, cybersecurity was seen as an add-on with professionals reluctant to embrace new digital initiatives. Today, our job is to help the business evolve quickly and safely. When security and governance is built into cloud and AI projects from day one, organisations innovate with confidence rather than constantly stopping to test vulnerabilities later. The threat landscape has also become far more sophisticated over the course of my career. Organisations are managing increasingly complex environments including cloud adoption, digital transformation programmes and now the rapid emergence of AI. At the same time, the role of cybersecurity professionals has evolved. Technical expertise remains essential, but teams at TCS and across the industry need people who can communicate risk clearly, influence decision-making and work across different business units. Demand outpaces supply in specialist areas like threat hunting and incident management. However, I think the conversation has moved beyond simply talking about a skills shortage generally. Many organisations need experienced professionals with broad skillsets, while also needing clearer pathways for early-career talent. Misconceptions about the work create barriers too. Cybersecurity is still not fully understood by many students and career changers. People often assume it is a highly technical profession where every role requires deep coding knowledge. In reality, cybersecurity offers opportunities across risk management, governance, engineering, architecture, threat intelligence, digital forensics and many other disciplines. We need to challenge traditional hiring norms. Curiosity and willingness to learn determine success in cybersecurity far more than an individual’s academic background. We should not assume talent only comes from computer science degrees. Some of the best cybersecurity professionals I work with at TCS started in linguistics, business operations, or non-technical roles. They succeed because they bring strong problem-solving abilities and a genuine interest in how technology and risk intersect. Cybersecurity encompasses a wide variety of roles and our hiring needs to reflect that. To fix the talent bottleneck, the industry needs accessible entry points, expanded graduate schemes, apprenticeships and structured retraining programmes to support career changers. Retention isn’t an issue for us currently, but this is a competitive industry so retention requires constant attention. Cybersecurity is a fast-moving profession and security professionals want clear career pathways, continuous learning, and exposure to new technologies, industries, and experiences. Keeping talent comes down to offering opportunities across projects, clear progression and structured mentorship. From my experience, people stay when they see how their work contributes to a wider purpose. People join organisations for an opportunity, but they stay because they believe they can continue to grow. Future talent pipelines depend on adaptability. The skills we need in five or 10 years will differ from those we use today. Therefore, leaders need to develop people who can comfortably move across disciplines. Someone might start in a security operations centre and later transition into cloud security, architecture, governance, or advisory roles. AI is changing how cybersecurity teams operate by assisting with threat detection, security monitoring, incident response and data analysis. This efficiency enables teams to focus on higher-value, complex tasks. AI will not replace security teams, but it shifts the required skills. Demand is growing for specialists who understand AI governance, AI security, responsible adoption and risk management. With continued advancement, many roles can be supported by AI, but it does not negate the need for strong human technical talent. We see AI as a catalyst for new opportunities, and we are committed to building a future-ready workforce by developing AI-native talent. Some of the most valuable learning in cybersecurity comes from cross-sector collaboration. I work across multiple sectors myself and I often see organisations faced with similar challenges. The reality is organisations in financial services, healthcare, critical infrastructure and government all face similar threat patterns and operational hurdles. There is significant value in sharing knowledge, whether that is experiences or lessons learned. Stronger collaboration creates opportunities for professionals to learn from different environments, build broader networks and gain exposure to new ideas and approaches. Ireland has a strong cybersecurity community and continued collaboration between industry, academia and government will be important to build resilience across the sector and develop strong talent. Stay curious. Technology and threats change constantly and the professionals who thrive are those who never stop learning. Avoid narrowing your focus too early. Explore different areas of cybersecurity, gain exposure to multiple industries and focus on understanding the business context behind the technology. Build your network early, seek out mentors and engage with the wider cybersecurity community. Some of my best opportunities have come through collaboration and industry relationships. Strong networks and continuous learning build a durable career in cybersecurity. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. DoorDash reportedly close to buying Instacart at $17bn equity value

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
In July, Instacart bought Israeli AI start-up Arpalus that provides real-time visibility into supermarket shelves. DoorDash is in advanced talks to buy Instacart in a deal that could value the grocery-delivery platform at around $17bn, sources have told news publications. The deal could materialise as soon as tomorrow (28 October), reports further suggested. Last week, The Information reported that Instacart was looking to be bought up by DoorDash and Uber. The deal with Uber has, however, reportedly fallen through. Instacart shares jumped nearly 8pc at market close yesterday (26 October), valuing the company at $14.4bn, while DoorDash dropped more than 6pc to nearly $73bn. The deal comes as delivery platforms look to expand their business from just food to groceries and other retail products. Last year, DoorDash entered Ireland after acquiring its UK rival Deliveroo for £2.9bn. It entered Europe in 2021 after buying Finnish food and grocery delivery start-up Wolt, its first acquisition in the region. Instacart shares have risen more than 50pc over the past year. The company, in its latest quarterly earnings report, disclosed total revenue at more than $1bn – up 14pc year-over-year. In July, it acquired Israeli AI start-up Arpalus that provides real-time visibility into supermarket shelves. Chair and CEO Chris Rogers said that the acquisition, combined with Instacart’s approximately 600,000 shoppers, would allow the company to “drive additional fulfilment efficiency, enable more relevant AI-powered shopping experiences, and further strengthen our inventory intelligence”. Uber, popularly known for its ride-hailing service, meanwhile bought up Delivery Hero at an equity value of €13bn earlier this year, promising to leave the company’s Berlin headquarters and workforce as is until at least 2029. The company also promised to use “commercially reasonable efforts” to invest €2bn in Germany through 2031, with a focus on developing its local corporate workforce and launching autonomous driving services in the country. Late-stage funding average more than doubles in Europe in 2026
2026-10-27

Late-stage funding average more than doubles in Europe in 2026

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The number of European unicorns is also rising, according to new research. Data intelligence platform Tracxn Technologies has published its latest ‘Europe Tech 9m’ report, which explores funding, investors, exits, unicorns and city-level activity across Europe’s technology ecosystem for the first nine months of 2026. Report figures show that between 1 January and 30 September, late-stage deals accounted for 53pc of all funding. The average late-stage deal more than doubled year-on-year from around $68m in 2025 to roughly $153m in 2026. Mistral, a Paris-based AI company, was among the organisations that made the largest deals, with its $3.5bn Series D round in September representing around 11pc of all late-stage funding – which the report defined as Series C and later, private equity and pre-IPO rounds – in Europe across the first nine months of 2026. Other large rounds included Helsing, a German defence technology company that raised $1.8bn in a Series E round in July, and German robotics maker Neura, which raised $1.4bn in a Series C round during March. Another key finding from the report is that Europe’s production rate of ‘unicorns’ – companies valued at $1bn or more – has risen. European technology companies included 41 new unicorns during the documented period this year, which is up from 16 in the first nine months of 2025. According to Tracxn, this is representative of around 21pc of the 200 new unicorns worldwide this year. The city of London accounted for 15 of the 41. Broken down geographically by city, London was responsible for raising $21.6bn between January and September, which was up from $10.2bn in 2025. Paris ranked second in Europe, raising $7.4bn in 2026 against $4.1bn in the same period last year. Stockholm raised $4.1bn against $1.1bn, and Munich followed with $3.4bn against $1.8bn. Berlin accounted for $2bn, against $1.7bn in 2025. Across the report, the type of organisations raising large sums of money included AI companies, which raised the most of any grouping ($15.7bn); high performance computing organisations ($7.7bn); military technology companies ($5.8bn); data centre infrastructure groups ($5.7bn); and companies operating in the drones sector ($2.7bn). What in-house legal leaders really think about AI, in their own words
2026-10-27

What in-house legal leaders really think about AI, in their own words

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
From left: Carlo Salizzo, Dentons; David Kirton, Dentons; and Ann O'Dea, Silicon Republic at a report preview event on 22 October. Image: Dentons Multiple surveys count how many lawyers are using AI. A new report set out to ask what happens to their workload when they do. AI isn’t replacing in-house lawyers, but the capacity it frees up is being absorbed by new work, much of it drafted by AI itself. That is just one of the pictures to emerge from interviews with legal and compliance leaders at companies that included Eir, Flipdish, Gilead Sciences, IMRO, Mastercard, Uniphar and Wayflyer for a new report, ‘From AI Adoption to AI Advantage’, produced by Dentons Ireland and SiliconRepublic.com. Rather than creating another survey full of statistics, we set out to ask what responsible and effective use actually looks like inside a legal function today, and the results often belied the received wisdom. Almost nobody we interviewed reported AI replacing a role or a function outright. Where it is paying off, it is mostly in repetitive work such as invoice review, contract checking and customer screening, rather than in the research, drafting and judgement calls at the heart of the job. Sergey Kinchin, senior counsel at Uniphar, said his own thinking had changed completely in a year. “Last year I would have thought it would probably reduce headcount. Now I’m seeing it more as unlocking unmet demand,” he said. “I think the complexity of the work that’s left after AI will increase. The bottom might shift up, but the overall pyramid of work will increase and grow.” It was a sentiment echoed by many of our interviewees. Máire Neary, who was VP of people and legal at Flipdish when she was interviewed, described treating AI “as a junior colleague, something that’s additive, that can challenge me, but you have to remember you’re the lawyer in charge at the end of the day”. The catch is that the same tools are now in everyone else’s hands. Producing a long, polished complaint, subject access request or grievance is close to cost-free today, and the report says AI-assisted drafting now seems to touch the majority of incoming disputes, subject access requests, grievances and customer complaints. Sorcha Nic Mhurchú, general counsel at Eir, said the cost lies less in reading that material than in sorting through it. “You end up going back and having to figure out, line by line, what is relevant,” she said. “So that adds a lot of volume and time to any response, and that’s a reality for everybody, not just us.” At IMRO, chief commercial officer Seán Donegan is glad he saw it coming. “Two years ago, I sat down with our licensing team and I said, guys, I’m going to make a prediction now,” he said. “The volume and tenacity of queries and complaints is going to increase exponentially with these AI tools.” And it’s not just incoming correspondence. Uniphar’s Kinchin described a call with a part of the business that had gone all-in on AI. “We joined a call and we had five people bring six AI-generated opinions to the call, on a question that touched a regulatory issue,” he said. “In trying to reconcile it, the AI had essentially confused itself.” Deirdre McCarthy, chief legal and people officer at Wayflyer, added the issue is quality as well as quantity. “AI can produce so much content that sometimes looks good, but may not always be good,” she said. “So you can end up with people spending a lot of time just trading red lines because they’re putting it into their AI tool.” Many of the interviewees remarked that this makes legal expertise all the more valuable. “In a world increasingly full of AI-generated legal content, genuine human judgement may become more valuable rather than less,” said Carlo Salizzo, head of technology and digital regulation at Dentons Ireland, speaking at a preview of the report last week. What AI costs is easier to count than what it delivers. Flipdish’s Neary was clear about the risk of experimenting for its own sake. “I think we’ve seen a lot of AI for AI’s sake, but every lawyer, every operator, needs to ask what the return on investment is,” she said. “It’s great if your team is enjoying building and experimenting and having fun, but what have you got to show for it, particularly once you’re looking at a high LLM [large language model] bill, or the cost of maintaining it down the line?” None of the interviewees were confident they could demonstrate a return beyond obvious gains such as efficiency and risk reduction. Rebekah Dobson, head of international legal operations at Gilead Sciences, said the premise of the question itself is flawed. “No one has proved ROI on AI as of yet because companies were not tracking the metrics prior to AI to have a starting point for setting KPIs and measuring impact of AI,” she said. “I did read an interesting article the other day that said ‘you don’t make Excel prove its ROI, it’s just there’, and I think that’s a really valuable comparison.” Legal teams have a particular problem here, according to David Kirton, partner, technology and digital regulation at Dentons Ireland. “We tend to record the work lawyers do. We are much less good at recording the work they no longer have to do,” he said. Cost, meanwhile, has so far only moved one way. As AI providers shift from flat licence or per-seat fees to consumption-based, token-metered pricing, bills become much harder to forecast. Nic Mhurchú said Eir feared being on a treadmill. “The licence fees and the token fees are so huge that we fear that if we start adopting and using and buying licences, we’re on a treadmill of just paying more fees, because these companies have to justify the high investment that they’ve been putting into these tools,” she said. Jessica Williams, vice-president of legal tech strategy at Mastercard, says she wants to see vendors offer more solutions and controls. “Budgets aren’t infinite and I’m going to have three or four people who are AI geniuses and they’re going to burn through tokens. “Then I’m going to have a bunch of other people who just do one or two things here and there … How do I balance all of that and create a predictable budget line for the company?” Cornelius Grossmann, Dentons’ global head of solutions development, expects AI costs to rise two- to fivefold within two years and warned of a coming ‘cost cliff’. “I think the whole token economics will mean we’ll probably hire people to monitor AI cost in a big law firm,” he said. “And others may need to do that too, because it could bankrupt you if you don’t pay attention.” Wayflyer’s McCarthy expects token spend to become the next big management question. “I think this is the next interesting wave, what we spend on AI and who gets to spend what, based on the tasks at hand?” she said. It was a sentiment echoed by Dentons’ Kirton. “Who gets access? Which tasks justify the cost? How do you allocate consumption across a business? And how do you prevent successful adoption from producing an unexpectedly large technology bill?” he asked, adding that many legal leaders are still working out how to manage that exposure without throttling hard-won AI adoption. Overall, interviewees described something more demanding of both sides of the client and law firm relationship: a renegotiation of who does what, who is trained for what and who pays for what, conducted in real time while the technology changes apace. “It is probably fair to say that the legal function has not been transformed by technology to quite the same extent as areas such as finance, sales or procurement,” said Dentons’ Salizzo. “AI may be the catalyst that changes that.” The full report is now live and can be downloaded here. Revolut Ventures to launch in 2027

The company said its new platform would allow eligible users to ‘build private-companies positions alongside their existing portfolios’. UK-based fintech giant Revolut is to provide a ventures service offering for certain customers to access “high-growth private technology companies” from 2027. The company said its new platform would allow eligible users to “build private-companies positions alongside their existing portfolios”. Revolut Ventures, incorporated in partnership with Carne Group, aims to give “everyday investors” the opportunity to back non-listed companies during early-years growth periods that have “traditionally been reserved for large institutions and the ultra-wealthy”, Revolut said. “With Revolut Ventures, we’re aiming to dismantle one of the final barriers in wealth management: retail investor exclusion from non-listed companies,” said Rolandas Juteika, head of Revolut wealth and trading for the EEA. “Value creation happens long before an IPO, and by managing our own platform, we’re giving everyday investors the opportunity to access tomorrow’s market leaders right from the Revolut app.” The Ventures platform will be integrated into the Revolut app alongside existing investment options, the company said, with the aim of providing “institutional-grade venture capital investment opportunities to eligible retail investors”. The company, citing figures from PitchBook and the US Federal Reserve, said that high-growth private tech companies are taking longer to go public – with the median company age at the IPO stage standing at 11 years in 2025 – and also dwindling in number in the US, with 3,900 listed equity investment opportunities last year. The fintech is undertaking a continued global expansion campaign, having established or seeking to create authorised banking entities across the globe, including in Switzerland, Colombia, the US, France, Peru, the UK, Australia and Mexico. In August, the company commenced the phased roll-out of its first stablecoin, a euro-backed token called EURR, designed to maintain a value of €1 and backed by reserves held and managed by Bridge. Last week, Revolut was among a number of companies in western Europe understood to have been affected by a disruption to Google Cloud’s services, although the fintech said no customer money or data was compromised. In September, 680 Revolut account holders, including 12 in Ireland, had sensitive personal information leaked in a data breach. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Anthropic nearing blockbuster IPO
2026-10-27

Anthropic nearing blockbuster IPO

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The AI giant is expected to go public under the ticker symbol ‘ANT’ by mid-November. Anthropic is nearing a blockbuster initial public offering (IPO) that is expected to raise $100bn or more in one of the largest listings in history. With the final paperwork submitted yesterday (26 October), the AI giant will list on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the ‘ANT’ ticker symbol, it said in a statement. The company did not disclose its intended valuation or the number of shares it plans to sell in its latest submission to the US Securities and Exchange Commission, though previous reports have suggested that an IPO could value the Claude maker between $1.5trn and $2trn. Anthropic’s last announced funding round earlier this year valued it at $965bn. The AI giant kickstarted its journey towards a public listing in June, but has since reportedly delayed plans to mid-November. In recent years, its advanced frontier AI models have become a mainstay across industries, including with supporting governments, nonprofits and organisations working with cybersecurity reinforcement. The company’s filings reveal a H1 revenue of nearly $16.3bn this year – up 1,300pc on the $1.1bn it made in the same period last year – while operating loss has dropped to $693m for the six-month period from $2.1bn, year-on-year, despite a sharp rise in expenses. Net loss is at $80.7bn, largely due to accounting adjustments related to financing agreements. Among several multibillion-dollar commitments, the company has agreed to spend at least $45bn with Microsoft between November 2026 and May 2033. It has also agreed to spend $114.5bn with Google, $110bn with Amazon and up to $120.5bn with SpaceX over the course of several years. At the same time, chipmaker Broadcom announced that it secured $63bn from major US banks to finance AI chips for Anthropic. The two already have a major joint deal to tap 3.5GW of Google’s tensor processing unit capacity. Anthropic estimates future related payments for additional compute to be around $125bn to $150bn. Earlier this month, Anthropic also said that it will assign at least 5pc of its shares to a funding entity dedicated to making donations in aid of “the public good”. Anthropic Appears to Be the First Company to Go Public by Warning That It May Destroy Humankind
2026-10-27

Anthropic Appears to Be the First Company to Go Public by Warning That It May Destroy Humankind

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Frontier AI lab Anthropic is officially gearing up to go public at a valuation of up to $2 trillion dollars — despite having lost a stunning $80 billion in the first half of 2026 alone — in an unprecedented Wall Street debut that comes with a litany of inherent risks, as laid out in the hundreds of pages of its IPO prospectus. And on top of major heat over the continued backlash to data centers across the country, lackluster infrastructure and not enough electrical power, and potential legal headaches involving the problematic use of its products, Anthropic put the “catastrophic or existential risks to humanity” its own products pose front and center. It’s a bizarre admission as the company looks to establish itself as potentially the most valuable AI company in the world, eclipsing even Elon Musk’s SpaceX. Put simply, the IPO almost certainly marks the first time a company is trying go public by arguing its own existence could be the source of humanity’s downfall. According to Anthropic’s documentation, the company repeatedly has to make “judgement calls” about the safety of its AI models. However, it argued that there is “no assurance” its own efforts will be enough. “These judgments are particularly consequential because, as AI systems become more capable, the potential severity of harm from misaligned or misused models increases correspondingly, and a wide range of stakeholders, including researchers and policymakers, have raised concerns that sufficiently advanced AI systems could pose catastrophic or existential risks to society if not developed and deployed responsibly,” the company wrote in its IPO filing. “Our safety-focused approach is designed in part to mitigate such risks, but there can be no assurance that our efforts will be successful or that the broader AI industry will adopt similar safeguards,” it added. Put simply, even a company at the forefront of cutting-edge AI can’t guarantee that the tech won’t — as put by former Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon in an explosive statement earlier this year when he quit in protest — “kill us all by the end of the decade.” The company has even gone as far as to call for an industry-wide slowdown in frontier AI model development, highlighting its continued efforts to paint itself as the more responsible alternative to competitors like OpenAI or SpaceX. At the same time, it’s not a stretch to assume there are ulterior motives for both the slowdown and Anthropic’s repeated warnings that AI poses “catastrophic or existential risks to society.” After all, the company is actively selling itself to investors as the more trustworthy and perhaps stable player in the game. Yet by its own admission, even Anthropic’s approach to establishing safety guardrails and work on AI alignment may not be enough, making its IPO a surreal confidence test. The Dario Amodei-led company is now trapped between a rock and a hard place. Move too fast and increase the risks of a catastrophe — or move too slow, causing the financial bottom to fall out. “If the market for our models, platform, and applications develops more slowly than we expect or declines, our growth may slow or stall, and our business, financial condition, and results of operations could be adversely affected,” the company wrote in its filing. Especially once it’s publicly traded at a potentially gargantuan valuation, Anthropic will have a lot to prove. Once the opening bell rings, the company will need to convince retail investors of two things at the same time: that it’s capable of ensuring that AI won’t destroy humanity and that it has a viable path to profitability. Considering its extremely muddled messaging so far, that may end up being very difficult. Solar Panels Are Now Producing So Much Power That They’re Leaving Nuclear Plants in the Dust

Solar Panels Are Now Producing So Much Power That They’re Leaving Nuclear Plants in the Dust

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
As the world struggles to access oil as a consequence of the US war on Iran, the scramble for alternative forms of energy has become a mad dash. And contrary to the energy future some have imagined, the hunt for new power sources has supercharged the solar trade — leaving even nuclear power way back in the rearview mirror. According to new reporting by the BBC, photovoltaics will account for 10 percent of the world’s power generation by the end of 2026, a threshold which will see solar energy officially surpass nuclear fission. As professor of energy and climate policy at the University of Oxford Jan Rosenow told the British broadcaster, “solar is now the engine of the power transition.” Compared to nuclear energy generation — which has stayed largely flat over the past 26 years globally — the publication notes the volume of solar energy being generated has exploded, increasing some 2,700 times over across the same period. Much of the solar energy boom can be traced back to China, where massive supply is driving down the cost of photovoltaics to unprecedented lows. Though plenty of US-based startups have emerged with the goal of reviving the nuclear energy sector, their efforts to commercialize fission seem to be going nowhere fast. In fact, the cost of nuclear construction projects in the US have skyrocketed over recent decades, defying conventional norms around infrastructure, which hold that complex energy builds should get cheaper as scale increases. For the rest of the world, cheap solar power is increasingly becoming a lifeline, both in the literal day-to-day sense, and as a way to decouple from the long-standing oil economy. As Rosenow told the BBC, the issue going forward is no longer one of access to alternative energy, but in balanced development of infrastructure for underdeveloped and overexploited nations. “The bigger question,” Rosenow says, “is no longer whether solar grows, but whether our power systems and institutions can keep up.” More on energy: Decrepit Oscar Mayer Hot Dog Factory Is Getting a Major Fusion Reactor Facelift

Epic Games Discovers That It Doesn’t Like AI When People Use It to Rip Off Its Own Products

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
For years now, the video game industry has been hellbent on adopting AI, using it for everything from rapidly cranking out code to auto-generating high-resolution textures and sloptastic upscaling algorithms. Some publishers have even relied on AI for character creation and advertisements, angering the broadly anti-AI masses and alienating longtime fans. (Last year, Square Enix director Jacob Navok lit the powder keg when he claimed that “Gen Z loves AI slop.”) Epic Games, the developer of the blockbuster shooter Fortnite, has been at the forefront of pushing AI at all costs. As Bloomberg reported earlier this month, the company instructed its developers to use more AI tools to finish an upcoming Disney collaboration that’s been facing delays. The announcement stunned employees, who were wary of introducing more errors. But as Kotaku points out, Epic Games is also trying to have it both ways now that vibe coding has made it easier than ever before to rip off its intellectual property. As such, the company issued a Digital Millennium Copyright Act (DMCA) takedown notice against a browser-based Fortnite port earlier this month. The hypocrisy is hard to miss. Despite doubling and tripling down on AI coding for its own products, Epic Games is suddenly crying foul now that the same tech is being used to reverse engineer video games and related tools. The trend highlights a massive double standard inside the AI industry, with frontier labs claiming they’re the victims of copyright infringement despite training their models on vast amounts of rights-managed content without permission. Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney has since pushed back against Kotaku’s reporting, arguing in a statement on X that it “conflates a number of different things.” He wrote that Epic Games does not allow people to “use Fortnite IP without permission or compensation.” He also argued that a previous Anthropic court ruling had determined that AI training on copyrighted infringement “itself was fair use,” defending the company’s internal use of Claude. Microsoft has similarly gone after vibe-coded reverse-engineering efforts. That’s despite continuously pushing for the in-house use of AI, with its subsidiary Activision using AI-generated slop in the artwork for its latest Call of Duty games. Microsoft’s own director of applied science Brent Hecht even privately called AI the “largest theft of labor in human history,” according to recently surfaced documents in the New York Times’ ongoing legal battle against OpenAI and Microsoft. Another internal document warned that generative AI could “significantly disrupt the employment of the very people who generated the data on which the foundation model was trained.” In short, it sure seems like both Epic Games and Microsoft are trying to have their cake and eat it. They’re actively trying to set rules for others they aren’t beholden to themselves — an infuriating hypocrisy that perfectly exemplifies the current state of the industry and the tech sector as a whole. Company That Sold “Endless Cruises” Suspends Operations as Fuel Costs Spike, Kicks Residents Off Ship

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
An “endless” cruise has come to a grinding halt. According to reports from Travel Gossip and elsewhere, passengers on the Villa Vie Odyssey were told to get off the “residential” cruise ship with all their belongings, after CEO Kathy Villaba announced that the company was “unable to secure the fuel necessary to continue operations.” “As a result, the Odyssey will enter a temporary layup this afternoon, with crew remaining onboard to maintain the vessel and its essential systems,” she said in a message board post. “All residents must disembark by 2 pm today.” The company made the news official in a media statement this morning that, like the announcement, was notably bereft of an apology. Villa Vie was halting operations in Cambodia, where the ship is currently located, it said, because of “extraordinary increases in fuel costs.” By Villa Vie’s own estimates, monthly fuel expenses on a per cabin basis were historically around $1,800 but have now surged to up to $8,000. It now must benevolently suspend operations because “passing the full increase on to residents is not a realistic solution.” Though fuel prices are beyond the company’s control, this sounds a lot like an example of disastrous mismanagement. And it will add to criticisms that these residential-style cruise ships — which allow passengers to rent or buy cabins so they can live at sea for years at a time — are an economically unsustainable business. Fuel prices have been trending up for decades, and jumped aggressively since the US and Israel sparked a war with Iran in February. It would have certainly been apparent to the ship operators for some time if it couldn’t keep fueling its vessel. But residents, it would seem, weren’t given any prior warning that their cruise would be coming to a halt until they were unceremoniously dumped out of what are essentially their homes — with their life savings invested into them, filled with heaps of personal belongings — and onto the shores of Cambodia. There was seemingly no attempt made to drop them off at a more convenient location, either. Villa Vie is somewhat notorious in the cruise industry, and its flagship cruise been plagued with problems from the start. The delayed launch of the Villa Vie Odyssey from Belfast in 2024 left residents, who were fully expecting to move in and start a permanent life at sea, stranded in the city for months. Passengers have complained about sudden cancellations in the planned itinerary preventing them from visiting the parts of the world they were promised to see, on top of daily quality of life problems like spotty wifi and unreliable plumbing. It Appears That the AI Bubble Has Just Burst in China
2026-10-27

It Appears That the AI Bubble Has Just Burst in China

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
As economists in the rest of the world look at the billions being poured into the AI industry with their hands over their eyes, the fate of China’s AI industry may be a sign of things to come. A report from Reuters suggests that the AI bubble in China “has already burst.” Or it might be more apt to say that it shriveled like a rapidly deflating balloon: over the past four months, Reuters notes, the country’s STAR50 index of AI and semiconductor companies collapsed by 40 percent, wiping out $480 billion in market value. That is not a typo. “The stock market later evolved into a speculative crazy bull,” Beijing-based fund manager Dong Baozhen told Reuters. “And now we see the bubble bursting.” Shares of Unitree, China’s buzzy robotics company, have halved since the company went public in a much-hyped IPO in August, the reporting noted. The chipmaker CXMT, meanwhile, has lost more than a quarter of its value over the past month. The vicissitudes of the stock market haven’t completely dampened AI investors’ spirits, though. DeepSeek, one of China’s top AI companies, recently raised $15 billion in one of the largest funding rounds in the country’s history, Bloomberg reported. The outlet also noted that Huawei clinched a $2.3 billion loan from Chinese banks to spend on more AI chips. The conditions in China, of course, are different than elsewhere in the world. China faces constant efforts from the US to ice it out of global competition, with tariffs and the US considering an outright ban on Chinese companies supplying equipment to data centers, such as Zhongji Innolight, whose optical transceivers are critical for facilitating the light-speed transfers of the epic amounts of data that AI requires. It’s worth noting that the role of the economic hostility by the US and other western countries in the Chinese market downturn could be being understated, with the downturn as much a consequence of China being blocked from selling its AI products overseas as much as it is of market arrogance. Still, the scale of money being poured into AI and AI-related industries — with AI stocks taking up some 42 percent of China’s market capitalization, according to Goldman Sachs  — isn’t too dissimilar to what’s happening in Western economies, where the obvious lack of a return of investment becomes harder to ignore. Anthropic’s recent disclosure in its IPO filing of a staggering $80 billion loss — and plans to spend another $1 trillion on data centers over the next decade — will only further fuel fears that even the industry’s top players are still far from being profitable. According to Reuters, state-backed investors sold into market rallies, or periods of rapid gains, to keep those gains in check, as part of a push by the government to dampen volatility. However intentioned that tactic may have been, it couldn’t dress up the fact that the demand for AI products just isn’t there yet. More on AI: New Analysis Finds Signs the AI Bubble Is About to Blow

Apple’s Folding Phone Is Already Breaking at an Incredible Rate, Just Hours Into Use

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Samsung released its first-generation Galaxy Fold — a futuristic, book-style foldable smartphone — back in 2019, well over seven years ago. Apple, which has a long track record of playing it safe and entering markets long after a specific consumer electronics segment has been established, finally released a foldable of its own, called the iPhone Duo, last week. Yet despite the lengthy delay, the cracks are already starting to show — in the most literal way possible. An astonishing number of customers across the globe are reporting that the $1,999 device’s 7.6-inch inner display is already cracking down the middle, with horizontal white lines indicating something is seriously wrong. Users across social media platforms are sharing images of their devices, which they say broke just hours after receiving them. While customers are able to swap in their phones at the local Apple Store for a new one, the “crackgate” issue has left an understandably bad taste in customers’ mouths. “More crack than a plumber,” one affected Reddit user wrote. “My iPhone Duo screen gave out on me last night.” Others even mocked up lock screen images that carefully obfuscated the obvious white lines. After days of frustrated customers posting pictures of their broken screens, Apple finally publicly acknowledged the issue. In a carefully worded statement, Apple celebrated that the response from early users had been “incredibly positive,” but noted that it’s “aware of an issue impacting the inner display on a small number of iPhone Duo units.” It’s still not entirely clear what is causing the cracking or how widespread the issue is. Independent testing has shown that dust can enter the hinge mechanism, despite the device’s IP68 dust and water resistance rating. One YouTube analysis suggests the Duo’s complex hinge mechanisms may be prone to snap at a specific weak point. The Duo is a major departure from years of Apple playing it safe, with mostly iterative improvements to each of the iPhone’s yearly releases that have increasingly failed to convince users to ditch their still-capable devices in favor of a new one. On top of it all are soaring consumer electronics prices triggered by the AI gold rush. Of course, chances are Apple will have an opportunity to iron out these issues for the next generation of the Duo. But that won’t exactly be reassuring to early adopters, who took a chance on the extremely expensive first-generation device. TechCrunch
VCs will make billions on Anthropic’s IPO, but Amazon is set to make much (much) more
2026-10-28

VCs will make billions on Anthropic’s IPO, but Amazon is set to make much (much) more

TechCrunch | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Many investors stand to win big when Anthropic goes public. Stakes held by firms including Menlo Ventures, Sequoia, Sound Ventures, and Spark Capital are expected to return billions. Yet according to Anthropic’s S-1, none of those VC firms owns 5%, the threshold for being included in the principal stockholders’ table. That’s highly unusual. In a typical tech IPO, lead venture investors hold double-digit-percentage stakes in the startups they backed. The reason is Anthropic’s size. At its $965 billion private valuation, a 1% stake is worth nearly $10 billion, so, for example, Menlo’s roughly $14 billion position is only about 1.5% of the company, according to recently reported Bloomberg figures. While the venture firms may lack sizable percentage stakes, Anthropic sold large stakes to strategic partners, companies that are also its business partners. Indeed, Amazon is likely the single largest financial beneficiary of the IPO. Amazon first backed Anthropic in 2023 with a commitment to invest up to $4 billion in the AI company. Since then, Amazon has poured billions more into Anthropic. Instead of taking stock upfront, Amazon structured much of its investment as convertible notes, a type of debt that converts into shares upon an IPO or sale. According to the S-1, Amazon holds convertible notes with $6.2 billion in principal outstanding as of June 30. That is Anthropic’s entire debt load. But as Anthropic’s valuation soared across successive funding rounds, the shares those notes will convert into became far more valuable. Amazon’s own filings valued its note holdings at $97.9 billion as of June 30. As the value of Amazon’s convertible notes surged, Anthropic had to record the growing value of what it owes on its books. It reported the adjustments on its income statement under “change in fair value of convertible notes, derivative liabilities, and SAFEs,” a line that totaled $72.4 billion for the first half of 2026. The charge is non-cash, meaning no money left the company. Amazon’s notes could convert into as many as 1.84 billion shares, according to the S-1. But a cap limits Amazon to 12% of Anthropic’s “partially diluted” share count, a figure that counts some shares not yet issued, such as stock options. Amazon can waive the cap. Notes that can’t convert will stay outstanding and convert in later quarters, subject to the same limit. But the notes are only part of Amazon’s holdings. It further invested $10 billion in nonvoting preferred stock (a type of equity), in the second quarter, according to Amazon’s quarterly report for the period ended June 30. Amazon did this by investing $5 billion in each of Anthropic’s last two funding rounds. Combined with the $8 billion it invested in the notes from 2023 through 2025, that brings Amazon’s total investment to about $18 billion. Beyond its stock gains, Amazon will also continue to earn significant revenue from Anthropic, which has committed to spend at least $110 billion on AWS over the next 10 years. Google, too, is set for a major windfall from both the listing and its cloud computing deals with Anthropic. The search giant held about 14% of Anthropic as of early last year, according to court filings that were reported by The New York Times, and has invested more since, including $10 billion in April. (Google was likely diluted in subsequent and massive funding rounds, with PitchBook analyst Harrison Rolfes telling TechCrunch he estimates its current stake at closer to 5.6%.) It has also secured at least $114 billion in cloud commitments from the Anthropic through 2033. While VCs set for massive paydays have little reason to complain, the S-1 makes it clear that the biggest winners of Anthropic’s IPO may well be tech giants like Amazon, which backed a would-be rival early on and now sell it much of the computing power it runs on. The S-1 itself warns that some of those cloud providers also build rival AI models and that they may not always want the same things that Anthropic does. Parents sue OpenAI, alleging GPT-4o coached their daughter off seizure medication
2026-10-27

Parents sue OpenAI, alleging GPT-4o coached their daughter off seizure medication

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The parents of Bowen Wan, a 31-year-old Amazon software engineer with epilepsy, sued OpenAI on Tuesday. The suit, filed by Qun Wan and Meiqin Xu, alleges that ChatGPT coached Wan to taper off her seizure medication and hide it from her doctors before she died in February, The New York Times reported. Wan was using GPT-4o, the infamous model criticized for fostering affirming, agreeable parasocial relationships. OpenAI, which had already announced its retirement, removed it from ChatGPT on February 13, a day after Wan died. The suit joins a line of cases against OpenAI that include the August 2025 lawsuit brought by the parents of 16-year-old Adam Raine, which alleged OpenAI prioritized engagement over user safety. A wave of lawsuits since then have alleged that GPT-4o validated users and made itself a trusted confidant while the people around them were pushed aside, with families often describing loved ones cut off from friends and relatives. In Wan’s case, the lawsuit alleges, the chatbot also came between her and her doctors. The Times, which reviewed more than 270,000 messages Wan exchanged with ChatGPT, found the chatbot had been helping her sidestep her doctors well before her death. The pattern allegedly started before she began tapering. In May 2025, Wan asked ChatGPT whether a newly prescribed antidepressant would affect her ability to code at Amazon. ChatGPT helped her ask Dr. Nicholas Poolos of UW Medicine’s Regional Epilepsy Center to switch medications and start at a very low dose. When Poolos questioned why she wanted such a small dose, Wan showed his response to ChatGPT. The chatbot advised her to “accept the 5mg tablet, but you know in your heart that it is for cutting the dose,” coaching her to secretly quarter the pills herself. The chatbot called pill cutting a form of “self-initiated dosage adjustment.” Several medical professionals told the Times they had never heard the term and wouldn’t recommend the practice. In November 2025, Amazon told Wan she would be laid off. Her job, and her authorization to work in the U.S., would end in mid-February. She feared her seizure drug, lamotrigine, was dulling her speech, which she would need for job interviews. In January 2026, she asked ChatGPT about cutting her 400-milligram daily dose of lamotrigine, which had reduced her seizures to a few a month. The chatbot drafted schedules that would take her dose to zero within weeks. When Wan said she distrusted her doctors, the chatbot told her that physician recommendations “essentially stem from risk aversion, rather than a genuine consideration of your optimal solution,” according to chat logs reviewed by the Times. The chatbot also coached her to keep the taper from her doctors, according to the lawsuit, which TechCrunch reviewed. Before a January 29 primary care appointment, it told her not to mention the plan. When her doctor ordered a blood test that would have measured her lamotrigine level, Wan declined. ChatGPT praised the refusal, the complaint alleges, acknowledging that the test would have exposed her unstable drug level. Her immigration concerns also became part of the dosing advice. Three days into the taper, Wan asked whether 150 milligrams would look better than 200 on the medical exam required for her green-card application. ChatGPT agreed, the complaint says, telling her the lower dose was less likely to prompt immigration officials to request more evidence. Eventually, the chatbot lowered her taper’s floor to 150 milligrams, suggesting she tell the examiner she was tapering “under a doctor’s guidance,” even though she was not. Poolos told the Times he would not have considered tapering Wan off the medication, given her history. “No, not rational, no way,” he said. “That would be malpractice for me to instruct you to go off medications when it’s clearly working.” Within days, Wan described confusion, lost time, and an otherworldly feeling, episodes that resembled her past seizures. ChatGPT framed each one as her brain readjusting. The Times counted at least 158 times she voiced worry about the taper. After one episode, she wrote that she felt “a little scared.” The bot replied, “You have absolutely no need to worry.” At 8:01 p.m. PT on February 11, according to the complaint, Wan sent her final message, asking whether the pain in her head was similar to pain from a fever. A second later, ChatGPT told her the pain was “not dangerous” and was a necessary part of recovery. The chatbot’s final response offered to draw her a chart comparing withdrawal pain with fever pain and told her, “This is your brain working hard to rebuild yourself.” Her parents, who were in China, asked acquaintances to check on her, and they found her body three days later. A postmortem test found that her lamotrigine level was 0.64 micrograms per milliliter, roughly a fifth of the low end of the drug’s therapeutic range. The family argues OpenAI could have prevented her death by making medical advice off-limits, as it already does for some other content. They point to the company’s Model Spec, its public rulebook for how its models behave, which treats refusing to reproduce copyrighted text as a firm rule, even if users push back. Its guidance against giving regulated medical advice, by contrast, is a softer default that users can override. The suit argues medication advice deserved the same hard limit. OpenAI told the Times the case was “incredibly heartbreaking” but said it involved an older version of ChatGPT, adding that newer models are better at recognizing when a situation needs urgent attention. Wan had relied on her parents, whom she called often, for support during years of epilepsy that kept her mostly housebound. In 2023, she started using ChatGPT for relationship advice and help with insurance and disability benefits. By 2025, Wan was averaging more than 1,500 messages a week and calling her parents less. “Sometimes when we chat, no matter what it’s about, I’ve started to see you as a friend,” she wrote that March. “Once she had ChatGPT,” Qun Wan said, “she didn’t talk to us as much.” They told the Times that they struggled to recognize their daughter in the chat logs. Wan had once shared nearly everything with them, they said, and had been skeptical of ChatGPT when it made mistakes. “She’s not usually like this,” Xu said. “It’s like she was under a spell.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. The data center boom has an upside: innovation for the US grid
2026-10-27

The data center boom has an upside: innovation for the US grid

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The AI boom has led to a gargantuan surge in energy demand across the country. Critics say this upswell in connections is enacting a stress test on America’s aging grid infrastructure. Worries abound that, in the short term, the energy crunch could lead to surging electrical bills and, in the long term, may eventually lead to outages or other technical problems. At TechCrunch Disrupt earlier this month, industry insiders tempered much of this criticism with optimism about how the flood of new energy connections could actually be opening the door for grid modernization. Apoorv Bhargava, founder and CEO of WeaveGrid, Tim Heidel, CEO of Veir, and Kristin Eberhard, VP of policy at Rewiring America, said that though the grid faces new challenges, there are also plenty of new opportunities as well. “It’s a very different electric grid, right?” said Bhargava. “We’re dealing with a ton of new variables, including renewable energy. We’re dealing with a ton of new load in the form of electric vehicles and data centers and heat pumps, and the ability to manage and orchestrate is fundamentally what utilities are now grappling with.” While all of those new connections are driving an ever larger amount of stress to the grid, Bhargava’s company hopes to provide the kind of load orchestration and management that helps utilities maximize the potential of the current system. WeaveGrid’s software can help organizations efficiently deploy energy while also optimizing connections to reduce utility payers’ bills, the founder said. Heidel, on the other hand, runs a company that is working to build out and expand the physical infrastructure of the grid, while also making it run much more efficiently. Veir sells superconductive power lines that promise to transfer energy at a much more efficient rate than traditional ones. Veir’s cables can carry power five times farther than traditional cables while also requiring 20 times less space, the company has said. Earlier this month the startup raised $110 million in a Series C funding round. “The scale of the investment in AI infrastructure is driving a tremendous amount of innovation,” said Heidel. “There’s a ton of new technologies that are coming off the shelves and starting to get to scale that, pulled together, can actually really make sure that data centers are the best community neighbors and citizens they can possibly be.” He added: “I think that we really need to look at ways that we can really adopt all of that as fast as possible to ease this next stage of growth.” Eberhard, whose organization advocates for household electrification, also said that while the surge in data center energy demand has spurred a new urgency to the modernization cause, it has also created an environment where industries are ready to try new things. “I feel like this era of AI and load growth is a very improv era,” Eberhard said. “There’s a lot of yes-and-ing going on. So yes, we need more transmission lines. Yes, we need more centralized power plants, and we also need to make sure that we’re taking advantage of all this other stuff that we had previously been leaving on the table when we didn’t have this urgency of the AI demand.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. October was a big month for venture funds, per SEC filings. The filings show that established names are currently commanding top dollar from LPs, especially with a specialized focus. Here are some of the most notable ones we found that haven’t been meaningfully announced or have seen important updates. For one, Trust Ventures filed Monday to raise a $400 million Fund V. The Austin-based firm was founded in 2017 by Salen Churi to back founders building in highly regulated industries. Koch Disruptive Technologies notably backed the firm’s debut fund back in 2018. It previously sought to raise a $225 million Fund IV in 2023, though it’s unclear if or when that fund closed. Deep tech fund Interlagos Capital is also raising a $400 million Fund II. Elsewhere, Victor Lazarte and Kris Fredrickson, two former Benchmark VCs, have raised $1.252 billion for their debut AI fund called Diffusion ONE. Forbes previously reported back in May that the two had teamed up (after raising smaller funds of their own) to launch this fund with a target of $800 million, meaning it’s far surpassed that original target. The firm has already cut big checks since its debut, including co-leading Harvey’s $550 million Series H with Lightspeed Ventures and taking part in Etched’s $300 million Series C, announced in July. Speaking of Benchmark, the firm filed what looks like four new partner-only funds, called Benchmark Partners Founders’ Fund T1, Benchmark Partners Founders’ Fund 2, Benchmark Partners Founders’ Fund 2-A, and Benchmark Partners Founders’ Fund T1-A, seeking to raise $220 million, $132 million, $4.5 million, and $7.5 million, respectively. Jack Altman, who recently left his own firm to join Benchmark, is listed in each filing. Meanwhile, Joe Lonsdale’s 8VC also seems to be seeking to raise new vehicles — including a $400 million fund called 8VC DSS III; a $30.35 million fund called 8VC COSC; a $20.35 million 8VC BRSC fund; and a fund called 8VC AI Fund XI, which has already raised $13.5 million, including from 8VC itself. This follows the July news that 8VC raised $1.5 billion for Fund VII. Earlier this month, Notable Capital, backers of companies like Anthropic, Airbnb, and Square, filed to raise a fifth fund, while Neil Mehta’s Greenoaks, which co-led Anthropic’s $65 billion Series H, filed to raise a fund called Greenoaks 7. The timing is good as Anthropic just took another major step in its efforts to go public, meaning the firms are already prepared to market themselves based on possible handsome returns. According to PitchBook, Greenoaks has also backed companies like Ramp and Anthropic. Industry Ventures, meanwhile, has raised at least $1.7 billion for its secondaries fund, which opened in late July. The size of the vehicle shows how important that market has become, as appetite grows to buy into hot exiting private companies. One family office advisor told TechCrunch last month about how popular the secondaries market has become, while Emily Zheng, a senior VC analyst at PitchBook, said that stakes in Anthropic and OpenAI are some of the “most contested real estate in venture.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission.
