GM is embracing hybrids now
2026-10-27

GM is embracing hybrids now

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. General Motors plans to invest in its Lansing Delta Township Assembly plant to handle future production of hybrid-electric vehicles, the latest move by the U.S. automaker to claw back market share by appealing to the shifting tastes of consumers. The company said on Tuesday that it plans to invest more than $2 billion over the next several years in the broader Lansing region, an amount that includes $1.25 billion to prepare the assembly plant for “future vehicle production.” The company said it is working with the U.S. Department of Energy to restructure a previously awarded Domestic Manufacturing Conversion Grant and direct those funds to future hybrid production at the Lansing factory. The company was awarded $500 million under the Biden administration to support converting a different factory in Lansing to produce electric vehicles, including hybrid, battery-electric, plug-in electric hybrid, and hydrogen fuel cell electric vehicles. Those funds were never released, according to GM. Today, GM assembles the gas-powered Buick Enclave, Chevrolet Traverse, and GMC Acadia at the Lansing Delta Township factory, which produced a record 291,000 vehicles in 2025. GM didn’t provide specifics on when it would begin producing and selling hybrid vehicles. But insiders told TechCrunch to expect hybrids across multiple brands rather than a single vehicle line. A Reuters report, citing anonymous sources, said the company plans to build hybrid versions of the Chevrolet Traverse, Buick Enclave, and GMC Acadia at the factory. Hybrids have become increasingly popular, thanks in part to rising gas prices. Customers also have more choices than ever before, with most automakers offering a range of hybrid and plug-in hybrid models. Toyota is the U.S. market leader in hybrid sales, followed by Honda. Ford has also gained considerable market share in hybrid sales in the past two years, selling some 228,000 hybrids in 2025. But GM has been slow to adopt hybrids. Until recently, the company’s only electric-hybrid vehicle was the Chevrolet Corvette E-Ray, which launched as a 2024 model. (GM sold 2,444 of those vehicles last year.) The E-Ray has since been discontinued; GM began deliveries of the hybrid Corvette ZR1X in March and next year will launch a hybrid Corvette Grand Sport X. GM eschewed hybrids in favor of battery-electric vehicles. The automaker invested billions of dollars to shift its gas-powered portfolio to an all-electric one. That strategy has changed under the Trump administration with the end of the federal EV tax credit and slower-than-expected demand. GM hasn’t abandoned EVs completely, though it has seen EV sales fall year-over-year. The company still sells numerous battery-electric vehicles across its brands, including the Chevrolet Equinox EV, Blazer EV, and Cadillac Escalade IQ. GM said Tuesday it will share specific details on its hybrid vehicle production plans closer to launch. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. TIME
What Early Voting Data Is Showing About the Midterm Elections
2026-10-27

What Early Voting Data Is Showing About the Midterm Elections

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings People wait in line to participate in early voting for the midterm elections in Cary, North Carolina, on October 25, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Election Day is still a week away. But with early voting for the midterms underway in nearly every state, tens of millions of Americans have already cast their ballots, offering an initial glimpse into how the pivotal elections may play out. As of Tuesday afternoon, nearly 33 million people have voted since early voting windows opened in some states over a month ago, according to tracking by the University of Florida’s Election Lab. Around half of those votes have been cast in person, while the other half were submitted by mail. With days of early voting left to go before Election Day on Nov. 3, that figure already represents more than a quarter of the roughly 122 million Americans who voted in the 2022 midterms. Michael McDonald, a political science professor at the University of Florida who runs the Election Lab, stresses to TIME that early voting data cannot in and of itself be relied on to predict the outcome of the elections. But he says it can reveal other informative aspects of voting behavior. “Once you start seeing consistency across multiple days of voting and across multiple states, it starts giving us more confidence that whatever we're seeing in terms of relative engagement, that's likely going to carry through to election day,” McDonald says. Here are some of the notable trends emerging in the early voting data as Election Day nears. Registered Democrats so far represent a larger share of early voters in the midterms than Republicans, having cast roughly 45% of early ballots compared to 31% cast by GOP voters, according to the University of Florida’s Election Lab data. Another 24% of Americans who have voted early are not registered with either party. This data is only available for the 27 states that have a formal party registration system, and signifies only the party with which a voter is registered, not the candidates selected on their ballot. Experts caution that Democrats’ higher turnout in the early vote does not guarantee they’ll maintain that edge, with tens of millions more Americans still expected to show up at the polls. Republicans are anticipated to make up ground on Election Day, as they have in recent years. A Pew Research survey conducted following the 2024 midterm elections showed that nearly 40% of registered Republican voters cast their ballot in person on Election Day, compared to 28% of registered Democratic voters. “We expect the Democrats to build up a lead in the early vote, and Election Day tends to be a Republican day, so you can't just look at the early vote as it is now,” McDonald tells TIME. Polling has shown, however, that Democratic engagement and enthusiasm is higher than it is for Republicans in the lead-up to the elections. According to a PBS News/NPR/Marist poll released on Tuesday, 52% of likely voters said they would back the Democratic candidate in their House district, while 44% would back the Republican candidate. Independent likely voters favored Democratic candidates by 13 points. More Democrats than Republicans also said in the survey they were very enthusiastic about voting, at 71% and 61% respectively. Another poll from the Pew Research Center earlier this month similarly showed greater engagement and stated motivation from voters backing Democrats: 78% of voters who voiced support for the Democratic candidate in their House district said who controls Congress “really matters,” compared to 69% of voters who backed Republican candidates who said the same. Such polls have suggested a “blue wave” of Democratic support might determine the midterms, but Republicans have pushed back against the numbers. Read More: Polls Point to a Blue Wave. Here's Why Republicans Say They're Wrong Bernard Fraga, a political science professor at Emory University, tells TIME that the relative lack of Republican enthusiasm in polls and trailing GOP turnout in early voting shows that support within the party, and for President Donald Trump, has waned. “This is a story about Democrats who voted in 2024 persisting, and Republicans who voted in 2024 so far at least staying home,” he says. The midterms come as Trump’s approval rating has hit near record lows amid widespread dissatisfaction with his handling of the war in Iran and the economy. A Reuters/Ipsos poll released on Monday put Trump’s approval rating at 32%, up one percentage point from last week, when the same poll recorded the lowest approval rating of his second term. Fraga says that Republicans are frustrated with what he describes as “failures” of the Trump Administration, which he asserts have decreased GOP voters’ support for their party and led them to stay home rather than casting ballots early. “Even for steadfast Republican voters, they look at the state of the economy, for example, and say that they're not too enthusiastic about voting,” he says. “Reading the trends, it's clear that Democrats are enthusiastic and mobilized to a much greater degree than even the Republican voters.” Simon Rosenberg, a long-time Democratic strategist, contends that independents are also more likely to be voting in favor of Democrats, given recent polling, which has shown that more independent likely voters support Democratic candidates. One YouGov poll found that 54% of independents said they’d vote for Democratic candidates, versus 27% who said they’d vote for Republican candidates. Another PBS/NPR/Marist poll showed that independents favored Democrats by a margin of 25 points. Given such polling, Rosenberg believes that the number of people voting for Democratic candidates is actually higher than the partisan breakdown in early voting data explicitly indicates. Rosenberg says that the most significant data coming out of early voting is the number of Hispanic people voting early. So far, Hispanic early voting participation is outpacing its levels at the same point in the election cycle in 2022 and 2024. Presidential election years such as 2024 typically yield higher voter turnout, which Rosenberg says makes the 2026 figures to date all the more notable and surprising. “It's almost certain that this heightened performance will carry over through Election Day, and that this will become one of the big storylines of the election,” he says. “It's a universal trend that we're seeing all across the country.” This trend is most noticeable in states with high Hispanic populations, like Texas. In one of the most closely watched—and expensive—Senate races this year, James Talarico is vying to become the first Democrat elected to the Senate from Texas since 1988 in a tightly contested battle against the state’s Republican Attorney General Ken Paxton. Over 4 million votes have been cast early, or an estimated 41% of the total votes expected in the race, according to tracking by VoteHub. As of Monday, Talarico held a narrow two-point lead in the early vote. The state's Hispanic population, one of the highest in the U.S. per capita, represents 24% of the vote as of Monday, according to VoteHub, a percentage point higher than the share it made up in the final vote tally in the 2024 election. Another analysis reported by The Texas Tribune showed that the Hispanic early vote in Texas has already surpassed the early vote numbers from 2024 and 2022. “What we can see here is just eye-popping numbers for Hispanics,” McDonald said about the group’s early voter turnout not only in Texas, but around the U.S. “If Hispanics are showing up in force now, it's almost with certainty going to change the overall … diversity of the electorate in this election cycle.” As a result, McDonald contends that “we're going to see larger swings to the Democrats in more diverse states.” Polling has indicated that Hispanic and Latino voters, a demographic that helped propel Trump back to the White House two years ago, widely disapprove of the President and the Republican Party heading into midterms. Sixty-six percent of Latino voters surveyed in late September by an NBC News/Telemundo poll said that Trump’s deportation policies have gone too far, while 75% disapprove of his handling of the economy and the cost of living. The poll also found that more Latino voters hold a negative view of the GOP than at any point since 2016. Another Reuters/Ipsos poll released on Oct. 5 showed that just 24% of Hispanic Americans approved of Trump’s performance in the White House, the lowest such rating the President has received across both of his presidential terms. And just 52% of Hispanic voters who responded to the poll planned to vote Democratic, double the level of support for Republicans among the demographic. Rosenberg cautions that early voting “can tell you a lot, but it can't tell you everything,” and that voter turnout can shift from day to day. He notes that early voting returns showed Kamala Harris leading in the 2024 presidential election, and that she still lost after a strong gain in Republican turnout for Trump. But he contends that “the lack of intensity that we're seeing in many rural and Republican counties around the country does not bode well for them for Election Day.” Fraga says that in general, political scientists are skeptical of using early voting data to predict election outcomes. This has been especially true, he notes, in the aftermath of the COVID-19 pandemic, which saw Democrats cast early votes by mail at a significantly higher rate than Republicans, who were more likely to vote early at polls, a dynamic that made it harder to draw on the early vote to confidently predict outcomes on Election Day. Now, though, “the partisan distribution of voters has become somewhat more stable,” he says. “So we can use that information to now have a better comparison, and as a result, early voting data sheds a lot more light on what's likely to happen in November.” “It's not determinative. It's not going to predict what's going to happen,” Rosenberg says. “But every campaign in the country right now is living and dying on this early vote data.”
TIME
The New Relationship Minefield: Your Partner’s AI Chats
2026-10-27

The New Relationship Minefield: Your Partner’s AI Chats

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Lindsey Hall’s boyfriend was asleep on her shoulder when she accidentally discovered what he’d been telling ChatGPT about her. Her phone had died, so she picked up his to look up something for work. Then she noticed a chat titled “Relationship Issues and Uncertainty.” Inside, she says, were complaints about her appearance, doubts about his attraction to her, and a sentence she still remembers verbatim: “I’m just not proud of her.” While he peacefully snoozed beside her, she learned that ChatGPT had issued a verdict on the state of their relationship: Her boyfriend needed to end it. When she first saw the chat title, Hall figured it was about a familiar disagreement: She loved her three cats; he wasn’t an animal person. “This is probably about the cats,” she remembers thinking. She hadn’t expected a list of complaints about her. “Nothing was positive,” she says. “Not one thing.” Hall got up, collected her toothbrush and charger, and slipped out the front door. “I just had nothing to say,” she says. “I had nothing but rage at that point.” Couples have long had ways to discover things they weren’t supposed to know about each other: rifling through a journal, stealing a glance at incoming texts, poking around in Instagram or Snapchat DMs. Now, there’s another place to look—and plenty to wish you hadn’t seen. Therapists say they’re working with couples who have discovered their partners asking AI whether they should break up, sharing sexual fantasies, or contemplating how to bring up something they were too nervous to say out loud. Reading the chats doesn’t necessarily tell you what the other person meant. Was your partner venting after a fight or seriously considering leaving you? Were they asking for help talking to you—or finding a way to avoid it? And who owes whom an apology: the person who read the chats, or the one who shared intimate details about their partner with a chatbot? When Ilana Grines, a licensed marriage and family therapist in Los Angeles, works with couples, her first question is now: “What have you told AI?” She wants to know if and how both people are using it, what they’re sharing, and whether they’ve discussed any of that with each other. Most haven’t, she says. “We don’t have a language around our conversations with AI,” Grines says. Couples may understand that a journal is private without having discussed whether the same goes for a chatbot conversation. When someone discovers their partner’s chats, “it creates this huge rupture where one person accessed the other person’s history,” Grines says. “And then it’s nuclear.” One couple Grines worked with paid for a single AI subscription. The wife, a teacher, used it to grade papers. Her husband used it to explore sexual fantasies, apparently without realizing that conversations from his computer would be visible through their shared account, Grines says. His wife discovered the topic in the platform’s sidebar. “We know about a third person entering our relationship,” Grines says. “But we don’t know about this third space that we’re creating.” Sometimes, one partner doesn’t even know that space exists. They might be aware their partner asks AI for help with work or vacation planning—not that they’re also discussing their sex life or wondering whether to stay together. Hall used chatbots herself, but discussing her relationship with one hadn’t occurred to her. “I just didn’t think of it as a tool for that,” she says. Part of what stings is realizing your partner talked to a chatbot about something they felt like they couldn’t bring up with you, says Shemiah Derrick, a relationship therapist in Chicago. You might also wonder how long they’ve been feeling this way—and whether you would have known if you hadn’t read the chats. Derrick has seen couples finally talk through a problem—only to start fighting when one person admits they used AI to figure out what to say. The other person’s reaction, as she describes it: “You had to go to chat for us to do that?” In those cases, Derrick points out that the person turned to AI because their usual way of bringing up problems wasn’t working. They wanted to get through a tough conversation without having the same old fight. “They were trying to do something different,” she says. Giulia P. Davis, a licensed marriage and family therapist in San Francisco, recalls working with a couple after one partner read the other’s AI chats without permission and found complaints and doubts about their relationship. The person who wrote them was still sorting out how they felt. Their partner assumed they’d already made up their mind. “The partner didn’t find a straightforward confession or plan to leave the relationship,” Davis says. Davis helped them distinguish “what you really think” from “what you might think about.” Someone might complain to a chatbot after a fight or ask what would happen if they broke up, for example, but not feel that way once they’ve cooled down. If you find these types of messages, you should ask your partner how they’re feeling, Davis suggests—not assume everything they typed is something they’ve been hiding from you. “A person can have private thoughts without being deceptive,” she says. “Couples need boundaries around private digital spaces just as they do around journals, therapy, or conversations with trusted friends.” A chatbot also does something a journal doesn’t: It answers. But remember that it’s getting only one side of the story, says Shayce Johnston, a licensed mental health counselor in Gainesville, Fla., and the founder of Human AI Dynamics, which explores how people form emotional and relational bonds with AI. If your partner complains about you and the chatbot agrees, they may feel that someone else has confirmed you’re in the wrong. Johnston describes the reaction this way: “Even the AI agrees with me.” That doesn’t make it true. Don’t assume you and your partner agree on what should stay private, says Tammy Nelson, a psychotherapist in Los Angeles and author of Open Monogamy: A Guide to Co-Creating Your Ideal Relationship Agreement. You might think nothing of opening their ChatGPT account; they might consider it the equivalent of reading their journal. Talk about that before you’re staring at something you wish you hadn’t seen. “What parts are private?” Nelson asks. Keeping a password to yourself, for example, doesn’t automatically mean you’re concealing something that would hurt your partner. “I really do think that it’s important to revisit that conversation pretty often,” she says. Reading the chats is only part of the issue. What’s OK to put into them? Johnston works with couples in which one person uses AI enthusiastically while the other has privacy, ethical, or environmental concerns. Your partner might be fine with you asking for date-night ideas but uncomfortable with you pasting in their texts or describing your sex life. Grines suggests starting with a question: “Can you share something you’ve asked AI about our relationship?” You can also ask whether there are subjects they find easier to discuss with a chatbot than with you. The answers can help you get specific about what you’re both comfortable with: using AI to prepare for a difficult conversation, for example, or talking through an argument afterward. Discuss whether those exchanges will stay private or whether you’d like to share what came out of them. If you’re tempted to skip the conversation and start scrolling through your partner’s chat history, Nelson suggests asking yourself why. Has something happened that makes you distrust them, or are you feeling anxious and hoping what you find will reassure you? As Davis puts it: “What gets in the way of asking your partner directly?” If you’ve read something upsetting, ask your partner what it meant before assuming you know. Davis encourages couples to wait until they’re calm enough to have that conversation. The person who used AI can explain what they were working through; the person who read the chats can explain what hurt. Johnston wants couples to stop treating the chat history as a verdict on their relationship. Instead, she helps them understand “what the need was, what the fear is, and where the disconnection was that led to the conversation in the first place.” That means making room for both people’s feelings: You can be hurt by what your partner wrote, and they can be upset that you read it without permission. There may still be a useful role for AI in the relationship. Grines has worked with couples who use their therapy notes to ask a chatbot for questions they can discuss together at home, for example. Johnston sometimes encourages clients to rehearse a difficult conversation with AI before having it with their partner. “I don’t think AI is inherently anti-relationship,” Grines says. But she wants couples to consider whether it’s helping them talk to each other—or becoming the only place they’re willing to be honest. “AI can be a bridge into communication,” she says, “or it can be a place where communication goes to die.” For Hall, finding out how her boyfriend used AI made her question conversations she’d once felt good about. Before meeting in person, they’d spent months messaging. He’d seemed warm over text, she says, but could be colder face-to-face. She’d wondered: “What am I doing wrong?” His chat history offered a possible explanation. When they’d discussed serious or emotional subjects, she says, he’d pasted her messages into ChatGPT and asked it to help him respond. She remembers his instruction: “Placate her.” She still wonders whether he would have spoken so harshly about her to another person, or whether something about talking to AI encouraged him. “Would you have said all those things to a person in that way?” she asks. After she left his house, he began texting her. She turned off her phone. When he later came to her home, she told him she’d read the chats. By then, she says, she felt there was little left to discuss: “I just read exactly how you feel.”

Why Dangerous Fungi Are on the Rise
2026-10-27

Why Dangerous Fungi Are on the Rise

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A microbiological culture of the yeast Candida auris. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the winter of 2015, Dr. Tom Chiller received a call from public-health officials in Britain. They were panicking. A new fungus was causing devastating infections at a large London hospital. “They weren’t able to get rid of it,” says Chiller, who was then chief of the fungal-diseases branch at the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). “They didn’t know what to do.” The British officials said the fungus—a kind of yeast called Candida auris—appeared to be spreading from patient to patient. The officials said they were going to shut down parts of the hospital to curb its transmission. “That was just mind-boggling,” Chiller says. At first he was more confused than alarmed. Candida is a kind of yeast that causes some of the most common fungal conditions, including thrush and vaginal yeast infections. Some species can cause severe illnesses, including bloodstream infections. But Chiller knew of no Candida species—indeed, no life-threatening fungus of any kind—that could spread so easily among people. “I’ll never forget that call,” he says. “At first, I didn’t really believe it. Suddenly we have a yeast acting like a bacteria? It was weird.” First discovered by doctors in 2009 in the ear of a patient in Japan, Candida auris behaves differently from most other fungi. It can survive at higher temperatures and in saltier environments, making nooks like a human ear, armpit, and groin ideal places to grow. It is exceptionally sticky and clings doggedly to people’s skin and hair follicles, and to surfaces—including slick ones, like hospital floors and beds, that are designed to repel germs. It is highly resistant to antifungal drugs, hard to kill with standard disinfectants, and can cause terrible, often deadly illnesses. Also, as British officials were starting to notice in 2015, it spreads easily through physical contact between people and via contaminated surfaces. At the time of the call, though, much about Candida auris remained unknown. A few countries had reported a smattering of infections, but it hadn’t seemed like a big deal. No cases had yet been reported in the U.S. “It was a wake-up call,” Chiller says now. “We realized that, huh, maybe this little ear fungus isn’t just a little ear fungus anymore.” In the decade since that phone call, that realization has cemented. When about 200 people—mostly doctors and researchers—attended a three-day conference about fungal diseases near Denver in September, Candida auris was the first topic on the agenda. To a nonscientist, the talks were dry, technical presentations on the pathogenesis and epidemiology of the disease, but to the attendees—and “almost everyone who works in mycology is here,” said Dr. Luis Ostrosky-Zeichner, an infectious-disease specialist at UTHealth Houston—they were red flags we should all be taking seriously. Candida auris has rapidly gained ground since 2015. It has sickened tens of thousands of people in over 80 countries and six continents. The World Health Organization (WHO) has designated it one of the world’s highest-priority fungal threats. The CDC on Oct. 27 listed Candida auris as one of four top-priority drug-resistant pathogens that require "urgent and aggressive action." Thousands of cases are now reported in the U.S. every year. The CDC said this summer that the fungus had already infected more than 3,000 people this year in 27 states. Up to 60% of people who are infected die. “In some U.S. states, it’s just endemic now. It’s there to stay,” Chiller says. Deadly fungal diseases were once considered very rare, but the proliferation of Candida auris points to a troubling trend: dangerous fungi are increasingly finding new opportunities to infect us, and human activity is spurring them on. From the climate we’re warming to the medical treatments we use, we are creating conditions that allow fungal pathogens to thrive, infectious-disease experts say. Rising global temperatures, they say, likely led to the emergence of Candida auris and are driving the evolution of other fungal pathogens never seen before. Changing weather patterns are pushing some fungi to expand their geographic range, and the overuse of antifungals in medicine, agriculture, and livestock farming has driven them to develop resistance to these drugs. “We are creating an environment that is favoring pathogenic fungi and drug resistance,” says Dr. Andrej Spec, a fungal-disease specialist at Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis. “That means they are likely becoming better at infecting us, and also at evading our treatments. That’s a bad combination.” Fungi are ubiquitous organisms. They live in, on, and all around us. Every day, we inhale thousands—if not millions—of fungal spores. There are an estimated 6 million species of fungi on the planet, but only about 300 of them cause diseases in people. “We are protected by our temperature and our immunity,” says Dr. Arturo Casadevall, chair of microbiology and immunology at the Johns Hopkins School of Public Health. The human body is too hot for most fungi to thrive, but as the earth warms, fungi are adapting. Researchers think Candida auris, which likely originated in marine ecosystems, evolved a tolerance to salty conditions and high temperatures over time. The yeast can now survive at 107°F. Candida auris is not the only threat finding hot new hosts. A new fungal pathogen is disfiguring cats and people in South America, and certain molds, which once infected only plants, are now sickening people too. Spec published a report in 2021 describing the first case of a person infected by a mold that had previously been known to damage only trees. Since then, two more people in the U.S. have been infected, Spec says. “It’s gone from never causing human disease in the history of medicine to three new cases of it in the last five years,” he says. In October, the CDC said that cases of a different mold—a brain-attacking variety—have jumped fourfold in the past two years. “This is a nasty pathogen,” Spec says. It infects even healthy people and causes brain abscesses that in up to 70% of cases are fatal. “We’re seeing a lot of these molds that were once rare pathogens becoming more common,” Spec says. “A lot of us are leaning toward the idea that this is probably being driven by climate change.” Spec and other doctors are working with public-health officials, patient advocates, and drug developers to try to stem the rise of threatening fungi. They’ve made some progress in recent years: raising awareness, doing more surveillance, and developing new drugs and tests, but the challenge is formidable. As fungi develop mechanisms to better infect us, more people are becoming vulnerable to serious fungal diseases—because of advancements in medicine. Today, to fight autoimmune conditions and diseases like cancer, millions of people take drugs that suppress their immune systems. But immunocompromised people are at higher risk of contracting severe fungal infections. Thanks in part to those therapies, the growing population of immunocompromised people in the U.S. is now estimated to number at least 7 million. “We have people who have immune conditions like multiple sclerosis, Crohn’s disease, and rheumatoid arthritis who might have died from these diseases in the past but aren’t dead thanks to these therapies. But they’re also immunosuppressed, so we have a bigger pool of people who are at risk,” Spec says. More people are also being kept alive by invasive medical devices, such as heart-lung machines and ventilators, which can create pathways for germs including fungi to enter the body. “We’re making all this medical progress,” Casadevall says, “but that progress comes at a cost.” Candida auris fungus, illustration Science Photo Library—Reuters Ryan Zwick, a healthy 22-year-old, had just graduated from nursing school when COVID nearly struck him dead in December 2023. The virus, which research shows increases the risk of cardiovascular disease, severely damaged his heart. Zwick spent a week in an intensive-care unit in Las Vegas before he was airlifted to a hospital in San Diego to receive more advanced care. He was intubated and sedated; his lungs, kidneys, and heart had all started to fail. He was attached to a heart-lung machine, a life-support device that pumps a patient’s blood outside the body, via tubes placed into his sternum. “His chest was basically open for like a week,” his wife Emmy remembers. When Zwick finally woke up more than a month after being admitted to the hospital, doctors told him he had a severe Candida auris infection. The yeast, they said, likely sneaked into his body through the tubes in his chest. “It got into my bloodstream and kept causing a lot of issues throughout my body,” he says. He was treated aggressively with antifungal drugs, which kept the infection under control. But the fungus lingered. Zwick was discharged from the hospital in April 2024, but he returned to the emergency room a couple of weeks later because blisters had appeared at the incision site on his chest. He soon learned that the Candida auris infection was causing osteomyelitis, a serious and painful condition in which bone tissue becomes inflamed. “They took out a bunch of bone and stuff, and put me on more antifungals,” he says. More than two years later, Zwick, who works as a nurse in New Hampshire, continues to receive weekly infusions of a potent antifungal drug to manage his infection. His doctors have warned him that he will likely have to take them for life. “It’s basically chronic now,” says Zwick, who still sometimes has swelling and blistering at the incision site. Zwick worries that available antifungal drugs could one day lose their sting. Candida auris is highly resistant to many antifungals; a growing number of infections are “pan-resistant,” which means no medicines are effective against them. “I just hope these drugs keep working for me,” Zwick says. When Candida auris first emerged in the U.S. a decade ago, it was a very rare and often severe disease whose victims were usually sick, elderly, and immunocompromised people in hospitals and nursing homes. But the yeast has been expanding its reach: not just infecting more people, but a broader range of them, and causing a wider variety of illnesses too. “Ten years ago, we’d maybe see one Candida auris patient every few months,” says UTHealth’s Ostrosky-Zeichner. “Now, we have several cases per day in Houston.” Texas, New York, Nevada, Florida, and California are among the U.S. states with the highest number of cases. More young people, like Zwick, and people who aren’t immunocompromised are getting infected, doctors say. The yeast is also starting to cause new types of hard-to-treat infections. Spec says that this year, for the first time, he treated a woman with a vaginal yeast infection caused by Candida auris. She had diabetes that was well-controlled and was otherwise healthy, says Spec. The woman had come to see him after getting recurrent yeast infections that hadn’t responded to standard treatments, he says. She recovered only after being treated for several days with an IV antifungal drug more powerful than what is usually used to treat such infections, Spec says. Vaginal yeast infections are very common, with an estimated 3 in 4 women having at least one in their lifetime. A class of antifungals known as azoles are typically used to treat such infections, either as a cream, suppository, or oral pill, but Candida auris is more than 90% resistant to azole drugs. “It’s no joke when you have to tell a woman that she has a vaginal yeast infection for which you usually take one pill and move on, but in her case, she needs to get on IV medication,” Spec says. Doctors say Candida auris is also emerging as the cause of another common disease: urinary tract infections (UTIs), which roughly half of women will experience at least once. Doctors have known that Candida auris can cause UTIs in people with catheters, but they are now seeing cases of people with tough-to-treat infections who don’t have any medical devices or major health complications. Public-health experts worry that the fungus could become as widespread as pathogens like MRSA: methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus, a superbug bacteria. “Initially, we tried to stomp it out, but we realized early on that Candida auris was here to stay. It was going to be more like MRSA,” says Chiller. “MRSA used to be hospital-only, and now it’s on every skin boil on the high school wrestling team.” Self-described ‘fun-guy’ Tom Chiller tosses a mushroom plushie in his Atlanta home Irina Rozovsky for TIME After Chiller got off the phone with his British counterparts in 2015, he went on a quest to better understand the fungus that he suspected would soon invade the U.S. He first wanted to know if Candida auris was already on U.S. soil but doctors had just missed it. Under his direction, the CDC sent out an alert to hospitals and researchers, asking them to test samples of unidentified fungal pathogens in their repositories going back decades. “We didn’t find Candida auris historically,” he says. The CDC also collaborated with public-health officials in parts of Asia, Africa, and South America, where Candida auris cases had been detected. Together, they studied samples of the fungus in an attempt to understand how it emerged. They discovered something remarkable: at least three distinct strains of Candida auris appeared nearly simultaneously, over the course of a few years, but separately—in different corners of the globe. Many fungal-disease specialists and researchers now think climate change likely drove the yeast’s transformation from non-threatening environmental fungus to deadly pathogen. “It’s hard to explain the emergence of Candida auris simultaneously on all these continents and not include climate change as part of it. It just has to be. There’s no other good explanation,” says Dr. Peter Pappas, an infectious-disease doctor at the University of Alabama at Birmingham. Fungi are sensitive to heat, but they are also very adaptable to rising temperatures. In laboratory experiments, researchers have put different fungi in incubators and, by steadily raising the temperature, have spawned fungi that are more tolerant to higher heat. “It’s actually not that hard,” says Casadevall, who studies how fungi cause disease and was the first to publish the theory that global warming spurred Candida auris’ rise. “The slower you raise the temperature, the more the fungi can tolerate it. It doesn’t take a lot of mutations,” he says. A study he published with colleagues last year showed how different microclimates within the same city can trigger changes in fungi. “We went to Baltimore and looked for fungi on the sidewalk in a cool part of town, where they had nice trees and it’s shaded, and then the same fungus in a warm part of town,” Casadevall says. “We found that even within one city, just two miles away, the fungi from the warm neighborhood grow at higher temperatures. So, it’s happening.” Fungi are already a major threat to many plants, insects, and cold-blooded animals. Frogs everywhere are being wiped out by a fungal disease; rice and banana crops have been decimated by fungi; and millions of bats, which drop their body temperature when they hibernate, have recently died in North America because of a deadly fungus. Until now, however, most mammals have been protected by our high body temperatures. The emergence of Candida auris is a “shot across the bow of humanity that these things can happen,” Casadevall says. “We can have an organism come out of nowhere, and begin causing disease.” A sample test for Valley Fever in the Carrizo Plain National Monument in Santa Margarita, Calif. Carolyn Van Houten—The Washington Post/Getty Images Many other fungi are changing in ways that are worrying and, in some cases, dumbfounding doctors. As temperatures rise and rainfall patterns shift, some are spreading to new places. The soil-dwelling fungus Histoplasma, which had historically been confined in the U.S. to central and southern regions, has been steadily moving northward, eastward, and westward. U.S. cases have increased twofold in the past decade. Valley fever, caused by inhaling microscopic fungal spores, is estimated to sicken up to 360,000 people in the U.S. yearly. The fungus was primarily found in the arid American Southwest until about a decade or two ago, when it began spreading to states like Washington, once considered too wet or cold for it. Even fungal diseases considered mundane and easily treated, like ringworm, are evolving in alarming ways, doctors say. A new species of ringworm that can be resistant to all antifungal drugs has been spreading from the Indian subcontinent to the Middle East, Europe, and the U.S. in recent years. This new ringworm, which is highly contagious, causes chronic, inflamed, and itchy skin plaques all over the body. “Think of athlete’s foot, but it’s now athlete’s body, and it’s untreatable,” Chiller says. He predicts this new disease, which experts think evolved in response to the overuse of over-the-counter antifungal medications, will become the predominant ringworm found globally in the next decade. In Brazil and South America, outbreaks of a fungus that causes a disfiguring and sometimes fatal skin disease have been sickening cats, dogs, and people. Like Candida auris, the pathogen has mysterious origins. The fungus was first identified as a threat in 1998 after an outbreak in Rio de Janeiro. First, cats started showing up to the city’s clinics very sick and covered with lesions. Then, people and dogs did too. Infectious-disease doctors identified the culprit of these illnesses as a type of Sporothrix fungus. Infected cats were spreading the disease to dogs and humans through scratches, bites, and even sneezes. That explanation didn’t seem to make any sense at first, says Dallas Smith, a fungal-diseases scientist at CDC who tracks emerging pathogens including Sporothrix. A species of Sporothrix fungus called Sporothrix schenckii had long been known to doctors and scientists. The pathogen causes what is often referred to as “rose gardener’s disease” because the fungus, which lives in the environment, typically infects people who work with dirt and plants. “Gardeners would get pricked by a rose thorn, and that would provide the opening for the fungus to enter,” Smith says. The fungus was known to infect cats, but that was exceedingly uncommon. The disease itself was also very rare. At the largest hospital for infectious diseases in Rio de Janeiro, the Evandro Chagas National Institute of Infectious Diseases, doctors typically saw two to three cases a year before 1998, says Dr. Dayvison Freitas, an infectious-dermatology researcher at the hospital. But seemingly out of nowhere, something incited the emergence of a more virulent Sporothrix species spread by cats and not plants. It isn’t clear what pushed it to evolve to so effectively infect cats. “Is it because of climate change or environmental change or some other pressures that we don’t understand?” Smith says. Sporothrix brasiliensis was named as a separate species in 2007. Infections caused by the fungus can result in multiple lesions in people, sometimes all over the body and face. It almost always leaves lasting scars, which are often dark and raised, says Freitas. In up to 10% of cases, usually involving elderly or immunocompromised people, the fungus can enter the bloodstream and cause destructive illnesses. “The fungus can affect any organ or system, including the bones, joints, lungs, throat, brain. It can cause a kind of meningitis,” Freitas says. “If this happens, it is very, very severe, and the death rate is high.” Brazil has seen an explosion of the fungus in recent years. “Cats are spreading this disease among themselves, but also to humans at an alarmingly high rate,” Smith says. From 1998 to 2001, fewer than 180 people, concentrated around Rio de Janeiro, fell sick with the disease. In recent years, thousands of people across the country are infected annually, Smith says. The disease is now even infecting people in remote villages in the Amazon, Freitas says, and has emerged in other countries too. Cases of Sporothrix brasiliensis have been steadily sprouting up across South America in countries including Argentina, Paraguay, and Chile. Uruguay experienced its first outbreak of the disease in 2025, and Peru reported its first case this year, Smith says. “It’s just a matter of time before it ends up in the United States” and beyond, he says. Sporothrix, says Chiller, is one of a growing number of fungi that are “dramatically changing our understanding of how we think fungi are supposed to act.” At the Denver conference, a line of scientists followed Chiller wherever he went. Everyone there wanted to bend his ear. Chiller was decked out in his usual professional attire: a well-cut jacket with a colorful, bespoke fungi-print lining and a matching pocket square. (Chiller, whose grandfather was a tailor, has about 15 fungi-themed custom jackets.) “In every generation, there tends to be a few people who are a force multiplier,” says Spec. “They change the field. In the case of mycology, the success that exists is often due to Tom Chiller.” Chiller has been working for decades to draw the world’s attention to fungi’s gathering threat. He was the deputy director of the CDC’s fungal-diseases branch for about 10 years and its chief for another decade. Under his leadership, the department doubled in size to about 45 people and expanded its work to include surveillance of priority fungal diseases, including Candida auris, and research into emerging pathogens, as well as new treatments and diagnostic tools. He was instrumental in the development and widespread use of a cheap, simple, and accurate test, using just a pinprick of blood, for Cryptococcus—an environmental fungus that can cause meningitis. “It’s definitely saved tens of thousands of lives,” Chiller says. “I’m very proud of that.” Chiller left the CDC last year after 25 years at the agency, months after Robert F. Kennedy Jr. was named head of the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services. Chiller says he had been growing increasingly disenchanted at the agency since COVID. “During the pandemic, it was turmoil for everybody. CDC took a big hit reputationally,” he says. Then Kennedy took the helm, and Chiller’s team was cut by about a quarter, he says. “We had to prioritize what we could do.” Chiller says he felt “handcuffed” by restrictions imposed on CDC staff in the spring of 2025. “They cut all our travel, they canceled all meetings. We didn’t really have budgets,” he recalls. “I thought to myself, If this is the future, it’s not for me.” Chiller is now chief medical officer at IMMY, a company that develops tests for fungal diseases. He’s also a board member of the nonprofit Global Action Fund for Fungal Infections and of MyCARE, a fungal-disease patient-advocacy organization. Now, he gets to do what he loves most: advocating for fungal-disease awareness. In September, he attended a roundtable discussion about fungal diseases with Kennedy and other U.S. health officials. Chiller, who helped coordinate the meeting, says it was the first time in his career that he had been in a room with a U.S. Health Secretary to talk only about fungus. That felt like progress, he says. Chiller knows just how critical it is for health leaders and professionals to be familiar with fungi. “Think Fungus” was the catchphrase of the CDC’s first Fungal Disease Awareness Week, which Chiller helped launch in 2017.  Doctors often miss fungal diseases because they fail to think of them, research shows. Though the spread of fungi in a warming world may seem like an impossible foe, there is hope in the fact that diagnosing fungal infections early makes it much more likely that a patient will survive—and researchers, including Chiller, are working to help patients take advantage of that edge. They’ve got a long way to go: a 2019 paper showed that almost half of some 270 patients in Arizona with valley fever—a fungal disease Chiller studied as a young doctor—weren’t diagnosed correctly for a month or more. Justin John at the hospital with his mother where he was being treated for Valley fever. Courtesy of Justin John Justin John lived that nightmare. The 27-year-old software engineer from Arizona was wrongly diagnosed with COVID, the common cold, and irritable bowel syndrome before doctors realized—more than six months later—that he was sick with valley fever. There were “lesions literally everywhere inside my body,” he says. After lumps caused by the fungus started appearing on his forehead and neck, doctors discovered that the fungus had eaten holes through parts of his spine and skull. He underwent two surgeries to insert a metal plate into his forehead, and replace a part of his vertebrae with a metal disc. “The vertebra was so dissolved, they couldn’t save any of it,” he says. John says he was healthy, active, and had no underlying medical conditions before he got sick. “It’s in the dirt. You can get it anywhere at any time,” he says. “I could have gotten it when I was walking to Walmart.” Blood tests are currently used to diagnose valley fever, but false negative results are common, causing many missed diagnoses. IMMY is developing a new rapid urine test for the disease that it says will be easier to administer and more accurate. “So many patients, even here in Arizona where this disease is endemic, get missed,” says Dr. Fariba Donovan, a valley fever specialist at the University of Arizona who treated John. “This test could really decrease that delay in diagnosis.” These are steps in the right direction, but Chiller says we need to take greater strides if we want to win in the race against fungi. Many decades ago, after encountering very sick and dying valley fever patients, Chiller says he “learned a lot about how devastating and complex fungal diseases can be.” He’s still waiting for the rest of us to catch up.

This 30-Second Habit Can Improve Your Balance

Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Standing on your own two feet might be no problem. Lift one up to balance, though, and you might have a few concerns. Fortunately, there are ways to get a leg up on better balance. “Balance is not a trait—it’s a skill,” says Dr. Cory Calendine, an orthopedic surgeon at the Bone and Joint Institute of Tennessee. One way to practice: Stand beside a sturdy counter, lift one foot, and work toward holding the position for 30 seconds before switching sides. Staying upright can be surprisingly humbling. “It’s free, it’s simple, but it’s also honest, brutally honest,” Calendine says. “It’s a good assessment of where you are.” Here’s why it’s worth practicing, plus how to get started. You might not give your balance much thought, but it helps get you through the day—from stepping into the shower to climbing upstairs when you get home from work. Even walking down the sidewalk involves briefly supporting yourself on one foot with every step. There’s a lot going on to keep you from tipping over: Your brain processes information from your eyes, inner ear, and sensory nerves, while your muscles help keep you steady. When you lift one foot, for example, the muscles along the outside of your standing hip help keep your pelvis level. “All of these things are being coordinated for you to stand in one position without falling,” Calendine says. Your balance can improve with practice: A review of 13 studies found that single-leg balance training improved balance performance in healthy adults. Calendine suggests working toward 30 seconds on each side, starting with however long you can safely manage. Still, preventing falls takes more than good balance—you also need strong muscles to support you. A large Cochrane review found that exercise programs that focused on balance in addition to everyday movements, like getting up from a chair, reduced the rate of falls in older adults by about a quarter. While standing on one leg is a good start, it shouldn’t be the only exercise you do. “Balance is the front door, but strength is the whole house,” Calendine says. Find a sturdy counter you can hold onto, says Kevin Pozzi, an assistant professor in the doctor of physical therapy program at Johnson & Wales University in Providence, R.I. Check that the floor isn’t slippery, and give your footwear some thought: “Sneakers would be ideal,” he says, as long as “they’re tied well and they fit.” If you’ve fallen before or had a significant injury or surgery involving your legs, hips, or spine, talk to a health professional before getting started. Pozzi also recommends getting help if you can’t hold the position for 10 seconds. A physical therapist can assess your balance and suggest a routine that’s safe for you. Otherwise, start with your hands on the counter, stand tall, and lift one foot off the floor. Keep your core engaged and avoid slumping over, Pozzi advises. Bending your lifted knee so your foot is slightly behind you often feels most comfortable, though you can also lift it in front if there’s room. “See how long you can hold first while holding on,” he says. If that feels easy, you can start easing up on your hands. “Maybe you go down to one hand,” he says. “Then lighten your grip even with that one hand so that you’re really not relying on it quite so much.” Keep it close enough to grab the counter if you need to. Work toward 30 seconds, then switch legs. One side might feel easier than the other, especially if you’ve had an injury. “They’re not always the same,” Pozzi says. Once you can comfortably manage 30 seconds on each side, try repeating it a few times throughout the day. And don’t expect to be perfectly still. “It’s normal to have a little bit of wobbling,” he says. You might feel your ankle moving or notice small shifts at your knee and hip as you try to stay upright. That doesn’t mean you’re doing it wrong. But if you’re wobbling so much that you keep grabbing the counter or putting your foot down, hold on and make it easier. Don’t expect immediate results; this balance exercise takes time to work. Pozzi says people may start noticing changes after four to six weeks of consistent practice, including being able to hold the position longer. “Getting their socks and shoes on might be a little easier,” he says. Calendine works on his balance while his coffee brews, both at home and at work, giving each leg a turn. “Nobody even knows I’m doing it,” he says. “They’re standing beside me, but you can kind of hide it, which is nice.” Dr. Vonda Wright, an orthopedic surgeon in Orlando, practices so often that she sometimes catches herself standing on one leg while giving a speech. She also asks rooms full of executives to try it—and watches them struggle. “It may be one of the only things they fail at,” she says. To make practicing easier to remember, she suggests pairing it with something you already do. Once you feel steady enough, try standing on one leg while brushing your teeth or taking a phone call beside your desk, with a sturdy surface nearby. “You’ve got to brush your teeth anyway,” she says. “Do it on one leg.” When holding the position for 30 seconds feels easy, you can gradually increase the challenge. Wright suggests placing a folded towel under your standing foot, which makes the surface less stable. Another option is moving your arms while you balance. Change one thing at a time, with support within reach. Think you’ve gotten pretty good at the whole thing? Try briefly closing your eyes; without being able to see, staying upright gets trickier. “Always be near something you can grab onto,” Wright says. If you lose your balance, put your foot down and start again. “We’re trying to make this a lifestyle,” she says, “not a workout regimen.”

How the War in Iran Could Escalate
2026-10-27

How the War in Iran Could Escalate

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings An Iranian flag is seen as missiles and air defense systems are displayed at Baharestan Square in Tehran, Iran, on September 29, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump’s conflict with Iran has become a political liability for his administration. Since the last U.S. strikes on Iran fell on Sept. 8, Trump has publicly ruled out attacking Iran before the November midterm elections—and promised to end the conflict after them. Then, rumblings grew that U.S. and Israeli military planners had reportedly drawn up new strike options against Iran, including the possible use of the largest non-nuclear weapon in the American arsenal: the GBU-43/B, also dubbed “the Mother of All Bombs”. The targets purportedly would be Iran’s nuclear facilities and energy infrastructure, though there are doubts about whether the bomb could penetrate Iran’s most deeply buried sites. According to these reports, Trump apparently is seeking a strike he could present as a military victory and then end the war without appearing to back down. But such an approach risks triggering a far wider war than Washington intends. Tehran remains unlikely to let Washington end the conflict quickly and, fearing a potential attack from the U.S., could itself strike first. Either scenario could lead to a new round of fighting far more destructive than the last. Reports of military plans involving “the mother of all bombs” came against the backdrop of two other worrying developments. First, Iran has been escalating its naval attacks, likely in response to increased flows through the Strait of Hormuz. Notably, two UAE-owned cargo ships, the GFS Pride and the Cape Dao, were struck in the Strait as part of this sharp uptick. Secondly, diplomatic efforts appear to have hit a wall. In late September, Trump rejected the proposal Tehran had put forward on the sidelines of the U.N. General Assembly: an end to fighting on all fronts, including Lebanon; the lifting of the U.S. naval blockade and oil sanctions; the release of some of Iran’s frozen assets; and the reopening of Hormuz within seven days. Instead, Washington insists that Iran must take steps regarding its nuclear program as part of the package, something Iranian leaders say they will only do at a later stage. The timing of any U.S.-Israeli attack remains uncertain. While Trump has said he will not resume attacks before the midterms, Israel held its election on Oct. 27, a week earlier, and Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu had reportedly sought renewed fighting before both elections. American and Israeli objectives may differ, of course. Trump appears to want something he can portray as a spectacular military victory without having to undergo another prolonged war, while Netanyahu’s rhetoric about “finishing the job” suggests more ambitious goals, potentially including toppling the Islamic Republic. Tehran seems to be preparing for either scenario with its own response: a prolonged, region-wide war designed to impose devastating economic costs. The dominant perspective in Tehran is that the next war must be fought in a way that makes it so costly that a future U.S. president would not contemplate another. Hossein Mohebbi, a spokesperson for the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), said in late September that Iran “will not stop until we have driven the United States out of the region, punished the aggressor, and established permanent deterrence so that no one will dare attack Iran.” That logic, should it prevail, rules out a symbolic, tit-for-tat exchange. In Tehran’s view, a limited American strike on Pickaxe Mountain, the fortified nuclear site near Natanz that Trump has repeatedly evoked, followed by a limited Iranian response, would send Washington precisely the wrong message: that it can attack Iran’s nuclear facilities, register domestic political gain, and pay a limited, acceptable cost. Iranian officials have therefore warned that even a symbolic, non-lethal strike would trigger a broad response, denying Trump the quick exit he may have asked of his war planners. Under this scenario, Iran would target regional energy and economic infrastructure. “In a region where we cannot sell oil, no one will sell oil,” Ghalibaf has warned. The UAE is particularly exposed: Mohsen Rezaei, secretary of the Supreme National Security Council, has accused it of oil “smuggling” in cahoots with U.S. Central Command (CENTCOM). Rezaei also identified new potential targets: undersea cables, data centers, and AI infrastructure in the region, which he claims are worth “more than $2 trillion.” Ghalibaf subsequently extended the threat to regional governments and investors, threatening that anyone providing “funding, equity, land, or energy” to AI companies that “facilitate military aggression” against Iran “will face coercive action.” If raising the cost of war is the first instrument in Iran’s calculus, duration is the second. Ali Balali, an adviser to the IRGC’s Aerospace Force commander, suggested Iran would conserve its arsenal in preparation for a prolonged confrontation, rather than expend it early. Two days later, Iranian Army spokesperson Mohammad Akraminia argued that the enemy’s weak point is a prolonged war. Timing is a third dimension in Iranian thinking. Iranian military officials have increasingly mentioned the possibility of striking first, preemptively. Akraminia stated that if Iran were to conclude that an attack by the U.S. and/or Israel is imminent, “we will certainly engage in preemptive warfare.” Mehdi Mohammadi, strategic affairs adviser to Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, went further, arguing that a successful strategy requires not only striking first but also delivering a decisive blow capable of “changing the balance.” There is thus at least the possibility that Iran might move early, and then fight long. That could occur due to miscalculation resulting from ambiguous American and Israeli signals: leaked strike plans, carrier and amphibious deployments, and aircraft approaching but not entering Iranian airspace. Military experts in Iran see this pattern as an effort to normalize military activity and regain the element of surprise. Tehran could thus mistake military signaling for an imminent attack and strike preemptively, even though it may not want another round of war. The fourth element concerns the range of those Tehran would hold responsible in the event of an attack. On Oct. 5 Ali Abdollahi, chief of staff of Iran’s armed forces, declared that if the United States acted against Iran “directly or indirectly through Israel,” Iranian forces would retaliate against the United States. The warning carries more weight in the wake of Trump’s decision to openly back Netanyahu ahead of Israel’s election. Indeed, some in Tehran interpreted this as a signal that Washington would allow Israel to strike Iran after the midterms. As a result, Tehran has made clear that even were Israel to carry out the attack on its own, U.S. forces and assets in the region would face retaliation. This interpretation suggests that the scope of Iran’s response to a U.S. strike likely would be far wider. Its conduct in Hormuz since late September offers a preview of how it could broaden the conflict. When the UAE, backed by CENTCOM, kept oil moving through a southern corridor along the Omani coast, the IRGC shifted its attacks from small shuttle tankers to supertankers and stated that vessels using the “illegal corridor” would be “pursued and interdicted across the region.” Strikes followed off the UAE coast and near Qatar. An escalation in the region could drive Iran to go after vital energy and other infrastructure across the Gulf. Iran’s regional partners also could widen the conflict, although Tehran does not fully control their actions. In Yemen, the Houthis have already opened a second maritime front in the Red Sea, threatening shipping and increasing pressure on Saudi Arabia. In Iraq, Kataib Sayyid al-Shuhada, an Iran-backed militia, has threatened to fight if Iran faced an existential threat. The conflict could also extend to Europe through indirect operations. There may already have been attempts in this vein: British Prime Minister Andy Burnham said a suspected September plot near RAF Fairford, a base used by U.S. bombers, was “almost certainly” directed by the IRGC. Tehran denies involvement, but the incident raises the prospect of covert operations against American military assets beyond the Middle East. Tehran is also preparing for the possibility of a ground operation inside Iran. According to Iranian Army Ground Forces commander Ali Jahanshahi, more than 50 units are deployed along the borders, from the northwest to the Makran coast, and abandoned airfields have been identified as potential enemy landing sites. Iranian security forces have also conducted preemptive operations against Baluch armed groups in the southeast and launched drone and artillery strikes in Kurdish border areas amid concerns that they could open an internal front during an external attack. Both the U.S. and Iran run the risk of serious misjudgment. The U.S. may believe it can be master of the scope and duration of a war, only to find that Iran holds the key. This power means Iran need not let the conflict end quickly or painlessly. For its part, Tehran—which survived the fierce exchanges that began in late February, continued through April, and have since become more regionalized—may be convinced that a wider conflict this time again would establish lasting deterrence. But conditions that made this strategy relatively effective in the past have changed, partly because of Iran’s own actions.  The more Tehran expands its targets, the more it risks turning neighbors that sought to stay out of the conflict into supporters of its defeat. Saudi Arabia and the UAE have reportedly agreed to coordinate their military support on behalf of anti-Houthi forces in Yemen, while Egyptian President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi has offered to help defend Saudi Arabia against Houthi attacks. For the Gulf monarchies, the stakes are becoming existential: either Iran’s leverage is broken, or their access to world markets will remain dependent on Iranian restraint. In this way, a war intended to deter future American attacks could instead strengthen a regional consensus that Iran must be dealt a serious blow rather than accommodated or bargained with. Ultimately, diplomacy remains the only way to prevent the gap between American and Iranian perceptions and risk calculus from triggering another, potentially even more costly round of confrontation. Without it, Washington could launch a limited strike expecting a quick exit, or Tehran could strike first fearing an imminent attack. Either scenario could trigger a prolonged regional war that the U.S. wishes to avoid and a growing alliance against the Islamic Republic for which Iran would pay a price. Nocturne Is a Slow-Burning Crime Drama That Struggles to Stay Lit
2026-10-27

Nocturne Is a Slow-Burning Crime Drama That Struggles to Stay Lit

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Stephen Graham, left, and Liev Schreiber in Nocturne Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Nocturne is everything you would expect from a 21st century prestige crime drama. Adapted from a series of best-selling Scandinavian mystery novels, the Apple TV thriller, premiering with two episodes on Oct. 30, casts a genuine movie star (Liev Schreiber) as a brilliant yet broken detective immersed in a case that has become an all-consuming obsession. The large ensemble represents multiple law-enforcement agencies, victims, bystanders, and a singular alleged serial killer played by an actor on a hot streak (Stephen Graham). Children’s lives are at stake. A risky undercover operation is undertaken. Relationships simmer, until suddenly they’re at a full boil. Collateral damage piles up. Progress is glacial, with essential bits of backstory meted out in stingy flashbacks. Underlying every scene is an atmosphere heavy with dread, as though this sordid scenario has arisen not merely from the brain of one twisted villain, but out of a rot at the heart of society. It’s a prototypical slow burn. This format, in which one complex investigation spans a season or more, wasn’t new to American TV when AMC’s Nordic noir adaptation The Killing became a sensation in 2011. (In this respect and many others, Twin Peaks, for one, was ahead of its time.) But the convergence of streaming, an ideal venue for binge-friendly serialized storytelling, and an audience for ambitious television that had been growing for more than a decade facilitated its proliferation as a cinematic alternative to the formulaic procedurals that still predominate on broadcast networks. The most acclaimed and discussed mystery shows of the past 15 years—True Detective, Mare of Easttown, Fargo, even Only Murders in the Building—have been, with few exceptions, slow burns. Yet, as Nocturne’s mixed success suggests, the tropes and limitations of the style become more apparent with each new title. In one welcome deviation from the norm, whodunit seems clear from the beginning. Confined in the bowels of a Pennsylvania psychiatric hospital, the serial killer Jurek Walter (Graham) calmly coaxes an inmate in the adjacent solitary cell into suicide. When a boy thought to be one of his victims, Michael (Landon Valentin), turns up, filthy and feeble but alive, years after he and his sister went missing, a new hunt for the girl begins. Schreiber’s Jonah Lynn, a Philly cop and military veteran who is now retired and a bit reclusive, was instrumental in the original investigation. Michael’s return draws him back in, along with an FBI agent, Saga (Zazie Beetz), who is a surrogate daughter to Jonah. As Quinn, a rigorous colleague from the bureau (Poorna Jagannathan), and the weary local police chief (Bill Camp) lead the search, Saga goes undercover as a patient at the hospital and Jonah goes rogue, to the surprise of no one who has ever worked with him. They don’t just want to find the sister; they want to catch Jurek masterminding violent crimes from his cell, thereby proving he’s coherent enough to rot in a real prison. A slow burn can only be as compelling as the personalities and ideas its pace affords it time to explore. Creator Rowan Joffé and his cast, adapting the Joona Linna novels by Lars Kepler (a pen name of Swedish husband-andwife team Alexander Ahndoril and Alexandra Coelho Ahndoril), do craft a few fascinating characters. Beetz’s Saga is a spookily canny mix of actor, detective, and daredevil, inhabiting her cutesy-until-she’s-not patient persona so fully, you might start to doubt she’s fully faking it. Graham weaponizes the soft masculinity that he wielded so movingly as the horrified father of a homicidal teen incel in Adolescence. It’s his cunning performances of compassion that set Jurek apart from the typical thriller psycho and make us believe he has the capacity to pull off logistically inconceivable feats of cruelty. Rory Culkin adds tension as Karl, a sensitive hospital worker whose anger at the way superiors use him as a pawn leaves him vulnerable to Jurek’s manipulation. From left: Poorna Jagannathan, Stephen Graham, and Liev Schreiber in Nocturne Apple TV What happens outside the institution isn’t nearly as gripping. Strong actors like Camp, Jagannathan, and Chrissy Metz, who plays Michael’s mother Ida, are saddled with underwritten characters. Everything we know about Jonah—that he’s brooding, single-minded, and willing to break rules in pursuit of justice—is a cliché of the genre, as is his odd-couple partnership with the stiff but well-intentioned Quinn. I got the sense, around the season’s midpoint (seven out of 10 episodes were provided for review), that Joffé was holding some of these characters at arm’s length in order to shock us with revelations about them later. But the extended delay in supplying basic context about Jonah, in particular, comes off as too much of a contrivance. It also distances us from the show’s implicit questions about whether laws and protocols enable (or prevent) justice, and how people like Jonah and Saga should weigh the rewards of dangerous, personal missions against the threat of harm these crusades pose. Nocturne’s small-town Pennsylvania setting is guaranteed to earn it comparisons to Mare of Easttown and its spiritual sequel, Task. Beyond the dutifully adopted (and, to my ears, pretty accurate) accents, though, it lacks the specificity of those shows, both created by area native Brad Ingelsby, with regard to the region and its people. It doesn’t have the same curiosity about or patience in depicting the characters’ lives outside the Jurek case, either. Viewers got invested in Kate Winslet’s Mare because we got to watch her try to keep up with her precocious teenage daughter and endure the harsh words of her judgmental mom. (It didn’t hurt that said mom was played by Jean Smart.) As Robbie in Task, Tom Pelphrey had space to show us a criminal who was also a lonely, newly single father desperate to provide for his beloved children. As any connoisseur of the hundreds of crime serials TV has produced since The Killing can tell you, there are only so many murder plots. But there are as many unique characters as there are human beings on planet earth. A slow burn that doesn’t make use of such a rich source of fuel is apt to fizzle out.
