Sometimes Trump’s Incompetence Outstrips His Malice. This Is One of Those Times.
2026-10-27

Sometimes Trump’s Incompetence Outstrips His Malice. This Is One of Those Times.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Donald Trump’s schemes are rarely the best-laid plans, but they often go awry all the same. So far, that’s what appears to be happening to the president’s most visible efforts to tilt next week’s midterm elections toward Republicans. His nationwide gerrymandering campaign and attacks on mail voting are threatening to backfire in key states, even as his attempts to pressure election officials fall short in court. It doesn’t mean we’re out of the woods, but it’s a reminder that presidents—even ones bent on exploiting their offices for political advantage—have only limited power to escape accountability for their failures. I thought gerrymandering was a pretty foolproof way to give one party a leg up. How did Trump screw it up? Let’s be clear about one thing from the jump: Nationwide, Trump’s gerrymandering push will probably still help his party hang on to House seats—just fewer than he initially thought. That’s because gerrymandering can backfire when pushed too far. Picture two congressional districts in Texas, which redrew its maps last year at Trump’s behest. In 2024, Trump won one of the districts by about 5 points; Kamala Harris won the other by more than 30. To make both districts more Republican-friendly, the Texas Legislature stretched their boundaries such that Trump would’ve won each by about 10 points—a big enough advantage to help Republican House candidates win in most years, but small enough for a wave election to swamp. In fact, these examples are quite real. They describe Texas’ 34th and 35th Congressional Districts, which encompass heavily Hispanic areas that swung toward Trump in 2024. Except inflation, the war in Iran, and aggressive Immigration and Customs Enforcement actions have soured many Latinos on Trump, giving Democrats an opening. In the redrawn 34th District, Democratic Rep. Vicente Gonzalez sounds confident that he’ll win reelection. In the 35th, polls show Johnny Garcia, a Democratic former sheriff’s deputy, competitive with his GOP opponent. If both Democrats win, the redrawn maps will constitute what political observers call a “dummymander.” Redistricting on the assumption that Latinos would continue to vote as Republican as they did in 2024 may also come back to haunt Republicans in Florida, where polls suggest that Democrats could win some districts the GOP redrew this year to advantage themselves. His attacks on mail voting, for one. The Supreme Court blocked an executive order that would have restricted mail voting, but the president’s cuts to the Postal Service—implemented under Elon Musk’s Department of Government Efficiency—are causing other problems. ProPublica reports that during this year’s primary elections, service reductions increased the rejection rate for late-arriving mail ballots in rural parts of Washington, California, Oregon, and two states with critical Senate elections this year: North Carolina and Ohio. Rural voters overwhelmingly support Republicans, so Trump’s antipathy toward mail voting threatens to undermine his own party’s candidates in the general election too. How about all the lawsuits Trump is filing? They haven’t been going well either. On Monday, a federal appeals court rejected the administration’s effort to force California to segregate thousands of mail ballots, which the government dubiously claims went to noncitizens. A judge in Alaska, meanwhile, sounds mighty skeptical of the Justice Department’s attempt to block certification of that state’s competitive Senate race if the Republican incumbent narrowly loses. And as the New Yorker reported this week, Republican and Democratic election officials have successfully resisted federal pressure to purge voter rolls of supposed nonvoters. So can we mark our elections safe from Trump? Not necessarily. Even if Republican dummymanders backfire next week, they might work as intended in less Democratic years. It’s also probable that we haven’t seen the extent of Trump’s attempts to meddle with the vote. He’s preemptively claiming that certain Republicans can lose only if Democrats “cheat,” which means lawsuits contesting the results are likely. As the New York Times reported today, state officials are bracing for a range of efforts to interfere, from federal officials seizing ballots to ICE raiding polling places. As the aftermath of the 2020 election proved, even Trump’s failed maneuvers can drive down public faith in the results and spark violence. Malevolence tempered by incompetence is still malevolence. The most titillating midterms coverage: What if the election were sexy instead of nauseating? “Maybe during these midterms,” Scaachi Koul writes, “the Democrats are learning how much reward can come from letting a hot guy run his delicious little mouth.” Scaachi argues that after a decade of Trump, what the left needs most is a daddy. Lucky for us, she knows exactly who it should be. A twisted romance: What if, at its core, You Can See Everything is a love story? Sam Adams makes the case that Nathan Fielder’s Elizabeth Holmes documentary is about something more tender than just matters of truth and reality. Your burning questions, answered: In the special segment Decoder Rings Back, host Willa Paskin answers listeners’ queries about cultural mysteries. In this episode, Willa investigates why stores lock everything up now—and whether there’s really a shoplifting epidemic. A clever crossword: What’s a three-letter word for “Jon Husted’s most prominent feature”? (Hint: Luke Winkie opined on this topic!) What’s a three-letter word for “where the door didn’t hit Kristi Noem on the way out”? (Hint: Christina Cauterucci covered this.) Solve these clues and more in today’s puzzle. Several States Made It a Crime to Talk About Abortion. Will Courts Now Enforce It?
2026-10-27

Several States Made It a Crime to Talk About Abortion. Will Courts Now Enforce It?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. A reckoning around reproductive rights and the First Amendment is brewing for after the midterms. Last week, a large group of conservative states announced an effort to defend the constitutionality of South Dakota’s law prohibiting advertising any item “designed, adapted, or intended for producing an abortion.” Enforcement of the law has been blocked by a district court and the 8th Circuit Court of Appeals while proceedings continue. Meanwhile, the 7th Circuit last week affirmed an earlier ruling blocking an Indiana law that made it a crime to aid a minor seeking an out-of-state abortion on similar grounds. These are among many developments that point to an emerging trend: States that have found it impossible to enforce abortion laws are focusing instead on shutting down information about abortion, and are doing so in a way that might pose a back-door challenge to the shield laws that have made abortion pills available even in states where abortion is a crime. Since the fall of Roe v. Wade, states that ban abortion have struggled to enforce their laws. Much of that reflects the availability of telemedicine services. In data published last June, the Society for Family Planning found that telehealth procedures have accounted for roughly 40 percent of the total in states where abortion is legal, and north of 97 percent of abortions in states that criminalize the procedure. The total number of procedures also appears to be on the rise. This state of affairs has been an endless source of rage for the leaders of conservative states, who fought for decades to end the right to choose abortion only to see abortion numbers increase. The easiest solution for ban states would involve intervention by the Trump administration. Current Food and Drug Administration rules authorize telehealth access; the Trump FDA could change that. The Trump Department of Justice could give in to the request of abortion opponents to treat the Comstock Act, a 19th-century obscenity law, as an abortion ban. It is no surprise that anti-abortion groups are hoping that the Trump administration is simply biding its time before imposing new national limits after November’s midterm elections, when President Donald Trump will presumably be less afraid of blowback. The Food and Drug Administration plans to release the results of a study on mifepristone safety in March. Many expect the study to justify an end to the current rules governing telemedicine, even though mifepristone’s safety record is well established. Attorney General Todd Blanche also suggested to a group of religious conservatives that he would use the Comstock Act to crack down on the mailing or receipt of any abortion-related item after the midterms. But there is no guarantee for conservatives that the Trump administration will deliver on any of their priorities. And even if the administration did resurrect the Comstock Act or reimpose in-person dispensation requirements for mifepristone, there would be open questions about enforcement. The FDA and Department of Justice have limited resources, and given what has happened in the first two years of Trump’s second term, there is no reason to think that abortion will instantly become an enforcement priority for the president. And then there are the difficulties involved in proving that an abortion took place—and that any procedure did not fall under one of the narrow statutory life or medical necessity exceptions written into most bans. It is most likely that a federal enforcement strategy—if one ever comes into being—will rely on making an example of a high-profile defendant and hoping others will be too frightened to mail or receive abortion pills. Actually identifying the vast majority of doctors who facilitate abortions would be challenging, to say the least. That’s one of the reasons that attacks on abortion-related speech are gaining steam: Conservatives recognize that it may be more effective to attack shield laws by policing the information available to abortion-seekers. Conservatives are particularly committed to winning on a legal question running through many speech-related cases currently in state or federal court: Can states prohibit speech that discusses something that is legal or even constitutionally protected somewhere else? South Dakota argues that its ban on abortion advertising doesn’t violate the First Amendment because that advertising constitutes speech integral to criminal conduct, like hiring a hitman. Similar logic explained the decision earlier this year of a Florida judge who refused to dismiss a lawsuit filed by James Uthmeier, the Florida attorney general, that seeks more than $500 million from Planned Parenthood for false advertising based on the claim that mifepristone is as safe as Tylenol. Indiana, like Idaho and Tennessee, seeks to prohibit speech they designate as “abortion trafficking,” making certain speech criminal if it facilitates a minor’s out-of-state travel for abortion. Even progressive states are interrogating the line between speech and illegal conduct: In California, a trial that ended in September asks whether crisis pregnancy centers violated state consumer protection law by claiming without evidence that pill-based abortions can be reversed. A verdict there is expected soon, and any appeal will inevitably land in federal court, and raise similar questions about free speech. The cases now before the courts have differences between them. Mifepristone, for example, has a safety record established through years of study. The same is not true of abortion-pill reversal, a protocol in which patients take doses of progesterone after taking the first of two pills in the standard regimen for abortion medication. The only gold-standard study on the safety of the protocol was stopped after patients enrolled in the study experienced serious complications that required a visit to the emergency room. That means that the safety and efficacy of abortion-pill reversal simply aren’t clear, and the evidence we have offers reason for concern. Conservatives have teed up any number of challenges to shield laws in court, but focusing on speech may be another way to undermine them. In each of the speech-related cases, conservatives advance the claim that when state laws clash, it is the ban state’s law that should control. So far, that argument hasn’t carried the day. A district court in South Dakota has temporarily blocked the enforcement of the advertising ban as applied to Mayday Health, which focuses on education about reproductive options. The court reasoned that while a state may be able to prohibit directly advertising for abortion services that are illegal in South Dakota, Mayday instead “spreads what it believes to be accurate information about abortion pills, including links to abortion pill merchants, with the intent of informing people that they have choices.” But there is no predicting what will happen as more claims move into the courts, especially when conservative state or federal judges are the ones making the decision. The upshot is that shield laws will be facing another threat after the midterms that has comparably less to do with the Trump administration. Instead, the fate of these laws may turn partly on which states get to decide when speech about reproductive health is a crime. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Elon Musk’s Future May Depend on the Midterms
2026-10-27

Elon Musk’s Future May Depend on the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. There’s no mystery why Elon Musk has spent an eye-popping $442 million to help Republicans win next week’s midterms—way more than the already-staggering $290 million that he gave GOP campaigns in 2024, including Donald Trump’s bid to regain the White House. Musk’s motives are personal. If the Democrats win back control of the House, they will surely launch investigations into the Trump administration’s many misdeeds and malfeasances. They will very likely include Musk’s multibillion-dollar contracts with military, space, and intelligence agencies—and how their security is affected by Musk’s eccentric behavior and his companies’ business ties with China and Russia. Toward the end of the Biden administration, the New York Times reported that Musk and his companies were under at least three federal investigations—by the Air Force, NASA’s inspector general, and the Office of the Undersecretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security. After Trump’s inauguration and the installation of Musk to head an intensive budget-cutting agency, all of these probes were shut down. Some of the officials in charge of the investigations were fired; others saw their departments slashed or terminated. While the probes were still in process and some of their tentative findings were leaked to the press, members of Congress—mainly Democrats but also a few Republicans—criticized Musk’s violation of federal protocols and questioned whether his management of top-secret programs posed a security risk. They are likely to revive these issues if the Democrats win back control of the House, with the subpoena powers of its investigative committees—or at least Musk has every reason to fear that they will. As the chief executive of SpaceX, Musk holds a “top secret” security clearance, which requires him to report to the U.S. government on all meetings or conversations he has with foreign officials. Yet he has had several with Russian and Chinese officials—including Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping—without notifying anyone before or after, according to anonymous sources close to Musk who spoke to the Times in 2024. These are not small matters. The Times story revealed that the Air Force turned down Musk’s request for a still-higher-level clearance, giving him access to extremely classified special-access programs, precisely because of concerns related to his contact with foreigners and his refusal to explain what they talked about. The Air Force officers were also concerned about Musk’s foreign investments. Gigafactory Shanghai, Tesla’s operational center in China, accounts for over half of the car company’s global deliveries. In 2019, Musk also received a $1.4 billion loan from Chinese state-controlled banks. (He paid back the loan ahead of schedule.) Officers, intelligence agents, and some of Musk’s own employees have wondered whether the head of a major U.S. defense contractor, who holds a high-level security clearance, should depend for so much of his business—and his personal wealth—on China. If the Democrats win back a majority of the House, the chairs of a few House committees can be counted on to wonder as well. Partly through his relationship with Trump, partly through technological prowess, Musk has positioned himself in a place where his companies—especially SpaceX—are invaluable: at least for a while irreplaceable. But Musk himself is not irreplaceable. In fact, if any of those three federal probes had proceeded, and if they’d found Musk guilty of violating security regulations, he might have had his top-secret clearance revoked—and without that clearance, he could no longer have the access to information that a chief executive would need. In short, he would lose his executive perks. This is a crucial election for millions of Americans, whether they realize it or not. Elon Musk understands his own stake in the outcome very deeply. It amounts to billions of dollars of his personal wealth and his continued ability to shape government policy, which could boost his fortunes by tens of billions more. That’s why he’s spending hundreds of millions of dollars. If the Democrats take back Congress, they should also start thinking seriously about whether anyone should be allowed to do this. We Finally Know Why Trump Has Tried So Hard to Hide the Epstein Files
2026-10-27

We Finally Know Why Trump Has Tried So Hard to Hide the Epstein Files

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. One of the biggest mysteries of Donald Trump’s second term in office is why, exactly, he is so touchy about his relationship with Jeffrey Epstein, who is now dead but whose crimes as a child sex predator and shady financier are still unraveling. You’d think the MAGA base would be eager to get to the bottom of this: This slice of the GOP has long been obsessed with pedophiles, mingling with the QAnon movement during Trump’s first term in conspiracy-mongering about an outlandish (and untrue) Democratic pedophile ring run out of a D.C. pizza restaurant, with children being sex-trafficked in Wayfair cabinets. In the lead-up to Trump’s second term, they latched onto a case with actual evidence to back it up: Epstein, and the scores of girls he was accused of grooming, assaulting, and trafficking to the men in his orbit. Trump supporters rallied around a demand to release the Epstein files, millions of documents held by the FBI that Epstein watchers suspected may further incriminate not only him but many other powerful people as well. Members of the Trump administration pledged full transparency and the full release of the files; meanwhile, Trump himself said he had no role here and that the files totally exonerated him from any wrongdoing. And yet the full files remain unreleased, hidden from public view by the Trump administration’s own FBI and Department of Justice as rumors swirled. Now we understand why Trump didn’t want them released. Just a week before the midterm elections, which are already shaping up to be a bloodbath for the GOP, Democrats have dropped a bombshell. “A growing body of evidence demonstrates that Donald Trump was an active participant in Jeffrey Epstein’s sexual abuse of young women during the mid-to-late 1990s,” states a just-released report from the Democrats of the House Judiciary Committee. Additionally, they claim that Trump has leveraged his FBI and DOJ to cover up the evidence, and have now called for the appointment of a special counsel to investigate any potential wrongdoing. The report is stunning. While many of the accusations were previously publicized, the stories have been a drip-drip of allegations; putting them all together in a single report has the effect of connecting a network of abusers, giving a reader a bird’s-eye view of how predatory men allegedly worked together to traffic and assault women and girls. And at the heart of the Democrats’ argument is corruption. After all, this same administration has turned self-dealing into an art form, and has enriched the president to the tune of billions of dollars, all while engaging in a staggering cover-up to protect powerful men who abuse vulnerable girls. And one of those powerful men, they say, was the president himself. Five women, four of them named and one anonymous, claim they were victimized by Epstein and that Trump also raped or sexually assaulted them. Part of the scheme allegedly revolved around Elite Model Management, run by John Casablancas, who was a friend of both Epstein’s and Trump’s. Many of the models recruited by Elite as underage girls say that they were raped or otherwise assaulted by Casablancas and others, and were also given to Epstein, who sexually assaulted them as well. Casablancas partnered with Trump to create Elite’s Look of the Year competition; the competitors were girls aged 14 to 19. According to the report, Casablancas met his third wife at Trump’s Look of the Year competition—a girl who was just 16, though he was in his 50s. The models recruited by Casablancas were trotted out at various events and parties with Trump and often Epstein, including at Mar-a-Lago. A second man, Paolo Zampolli, also worked as a modeling agent who allegedly recruited women and girls for Epstein; he was close friends with Trump and even served in the Trump administration as special envoy for global partnerships until earlier this year. He is also the agent who recruited Slovenian model Melania Knauss, helping with her housing and immigration status; he claims he’s the one who introduced her to her future husband. Some others dispute this, claiming that Epstein was the one who made the introduction (the Trumps deny this). Just as damning as the allegations of sexual assault are those of a cover-up. In one startling interview between Attorney General Todd Blanche (who formerly served as Trump’s personal attorney and defense lawyer before taking over the Justice Department) and Ghislaine Maxwell, Maxwell seems poised to discuss details of one woman’s accusations against Trump, until Blanche interrupts her, instructing her, “Let’s not talk about individuals” and repeating that he doesn’t want to “get into” a “he said, she said” or whatever it is she’s about to disclose. This behavior, the Democrats who wrote the report allege, “potentially constituted witness tampering and obstruction of an official proceeding—serious felonies.” The report also proffers evidence to refute Trump’s claim that he didn’t know Epstein well and had banished him from Mar-a-Lago because of his behavior toward girls and women. According to White House spokeswoman Lauren Bis, “None of these allegations are true. This ‘report’ is nothing more than a desperate attempt by crazed Democrats to distract Americans from the surging Republican momentum driven by President Trump’s historic achievements.” She added that “the President kicked Jeffrey Epstein out of his club at Mar-a-Lago because he was a creep.” Regardless of this denial, the demand for an appointment of a special counsel to investigate the Trump–Epstein ties and any possible cover-up is well overdue. Survivors deserve to see accountability. The public deserves the truth. And the release of the report a week before the midterm is savvy politics. If there’s one issue that unites Americans from left to right, it’s that Epstein was a world-class scumbag, and his death was unfortunate only insofar as it meant he never truly saw justice. I suspect that most Americans would agree that anyone else who enabled Epstein or engaged in crimes with him should be named, shamed, and potentially prosecuted—no matter what side of the political aisle they sit on. But so far, the Trump administration has stymied efforts to do just that. That should be a scandal itself, but it turns out it might also be a much darker red flag about why the administration won’t investigate. Democrats are smart to point that out. If there really was nothing to see in the Epstein files, you’d think that the Trump administration would release them and call it a big win, especially at a moment when they’re down and out. Cost of living remains unaffordable for many Americans, while the president who promised no more foreign wars is engaged in a spectacularly stupid conflict of choice with Iran, leaving American taxpayers footing the bill. It is objectively strange—and obviously suspicious—that the Trump administration isn’t using everything it can to distract from the unpopular reality it’s created for voters. The Epstein files remain one of the most captivating and conspiracy-fueling issues precisely because so much secrecy still surrounds them. And why? The man at the center of the criminal enterprise is dead. His second-in-command is in prison. Many prominent men have already been publicly embarrassed by their ties to Epstein. The continued withholding of the files and the smearing of anyone who demands their release, and the stonewalling of any investigation into why they have not been released, simply stinks. And it reinforces Americans’ cynicism with government and sense that everyone in power is inherently corrupt. Unfortunately, too many people see Trump as simply more of the same, rather than an aberrant figure who makes a mockery of American democracy and who must not just be ousted but ostracized and his party, co-conspirators, and enablers condemned. Part of the problem with the stories of Epstein, Trump, and the corruption all come down to the same thing: The bit-by-bit release of information has created a long record of abhorrent behavior, which means that the bar to shock is so high it’s almost impossible to clear. There is a kind of disgust fatigue at play: Yeah, we know Epstein was a disgusting sex predator, and that some of the men around him probably partook too; what’s new? Those who are ideologically motivated to believe Trump at all costs are also probably motivated to believe him on Epstein too; very little seems like it would change the minds of hardcore MAGA adherents. But persuading hardcore MAGA adherents isn’t the task at hand. Persuading the persuadable is; so is restoring dignity and accountability to the office of the presidency. Neither of those tasks is fully completed by finally, fully, and transparently investigating Epstein and the Trump administration’s treatment of the case. But it would be a good and necessary start. And if this administration refuses—and it seems primed to refuse the appointment of a special counsel—voters should let the administration know what they think about that at the polls next week. The Democrats Need a Daddy. I Know Exactly Who It Should Be.
2026-10-27

The Democrats Need a Daddy. I Know Exactly Who It Should Be.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Finally, a Democrat who actually knows how to have sex. Earlier this month, the New York Post reported that Chris Gallant, the former military pilot and union leader running for Congress in Long Island, New York, was photographed in fetish gear almost 20 years ago. This is the typical kind of sex scandal that would ordinarily trip up a candidate, maybe even for good. But Gallant—who is gay, and already out—hasn’t been significantly impacted by the would-be controversy. The photos were taken consensually, when Gallant was a private citizen, but what’s protecting him more than anything is that he looks good. Ripped and half-hard, there isn’t anything to be embarrassed about here. He’s hot and gay and lightly horny. What exactly is the problem? The dearth of a scandal around Gallant—running as a Democrat in decidedly red Long Island, where he’s still trailing behind his Republican opponent—must be a relief of sorts for the party. Ever since Barack Obama left office, Democrats have been looking for someone with his charm, smarts, and, yes, his good looks. There have been, it seems, a thousand flashes in the pan: Beto O’Rourke’s rolled-up shirtsleeves, Anthony Weiner’s city-kid mouthiness, Graham Platner’s stupid little butch mustache. So far, no one has stuck: Guys like O’Rourke failed to connect with voters in any meaningful way, and others, like Platner, dropped off the face of the earth after being accused of sexual assault. (Don’t get me started on Weiner.) What the Democratic Party—and what Democrats of all ilks—considers attractive these days has changed considerably in the Trump years. There are fewer and fewer intellectual stalwarts like Obama or Pete Buttigieg, and not that many impish party boys/accused harassers like Weiner or Bill Clinton. There’s certainly no one on the national stage who inspires the same kind of insipid loyalty that Donald Trump does. For the last few electoral cycles, it somehow seemed that the Republicans were dominating when it came to hotness, encapsulated through Pete Hegseth struggling with a pair of dumbbells. Republicans owned boobs and lingerie and fucking raw, and while RFK Jr. looks like a raw chicken left in the sun, guys like him have an undeniable confidence that still gets political attention. The Democratic Party was virginized, a group of scolds lecturing everyone about safe sex and the benefits of chaste friendship. The party has long struggled to make sense of what it, and its voters, wants. In the forthcoming midterms, and going into the all-important 2028 election, the Democrats understand that finding a hot candidate is important, namely a straight man who can appeal to both female and male voters through a mix of aesthetics and policy. Take a gander at the TikTok accounts of people like Manny Rutinel and James Talarico, and you’ll find plenty of self-objectifying videos, fan-cams of rolled-up shirtsleeves, compilation clips of dudes being dudes (lifting weights and being mean to Hegseth). But they’re still not nailing it (get it?). It’s like a cursed episode of The Dating Game, with three versions of the Sexy Democrat as contestants. First, a representation of Woke 2 at its worst: Gavin Newsom, governor of California, a presidential prospect for 2028. Lately, he’s been going after the Republicans using millennial and Gen Z internet-speak, but there’s a clear dissonance between his old playbook and new slang. Next: Jon Ossoff, the latest Great White Hope of the Democratic Party. Running for Senate in Georgia, Ossoff is now synonymous with (ugh) clapbacks against Natalie Harp and being a saucy little bitch at confirmation hearings. What Ossoff offers is a pliable blank canvas in the shape of an attractive white man, not vocal enough to be hateable but forceful enough to pin your hopes for the next five generations on this one guy (ask John Kerry how that goes). Finally, one of the newest subjects of political thirst: Abdul El-Sayed, candidate for Michigan Senate, and easily the hottest man the Democrats have offered us in the better part of a decade. He is, objectively, good-looking, and yes, anyone who disagrees is a racist! He’s a doctor, a beefcake, and he’s always walking around in a black V-neck, which is sluttier than anything Shane Gillis complains about on national television. But above all, El-Sayed is a bulldog, happy to bite back in a way we rarely see from any candidate, never mind a Muslim one. As the midterms inch closer—and as we enter another two years of presidential primaries—the Democrats know they need to find a legitimately hot candidate. But it’s not just about finding a good-looking candidate, or a charming candidate, or even a personable candidate. After a decade-plus of living with an abusive father, there’s nothing we need more than a daddy. Gavin Newsom looks like the rat that Lisa Simpson accidentally shears thinking he’s a baby sheep. He is an inherent and visual slimeball: greased-back hair, raspy voice, more teeth than you ever thought someone could have. When his social media accounts started to mock Trump openly and brazenly, it felt like only Newsom—Trump’s spiritual opposite, loose proof of the horseshoe theory—could pull this off. “I, THE PEACETIME GOVERNOR—OUR NATION’S FAVORITE—WILL SAVE AMERICA ONCE AGAIN,” he tweeted in August 2025. “MANY ARE NOW CALLING ME GAVIN CHRISTOPHER ‘COLUMBUS’ NEWSOM (BECAUSE OF THE MAPS!). THANK YOU FOR YOUR ATTENTION TO THIS MATTER. —GCN.” Looking a little like a forgotten McConaughey cousin? Check. Liberal enough to be considered a part of the resistance against the current administration? Sure. Mean to the president? Yeah, that’s the stuff. Newsom never seems more attractive than he does when he’s being a bully. Why else have a 6-foot-whatever nearly 60-year-old guy from the Bay Area in your corner? Newsom has built his iconography around an image of a young man following in his father’s footsteps while also being raised by a long-suffering single mother and overcoming his dyslexia. A more interesting version of Newsom, as both person and candidate, is the guy jeering at the president. What makes Trump attractive to those on the right—at least in spirit, if not his actual looks—is that he’s virtually untouched by consequences. He moves through the world and the meatgrinder of American politics without ever having to account for anything he’s said and done. A man who does whatever the fuck he wants and somehow seems to have fun doing it? Newsom has that down too. Newsom also has an unbearable smarm about him. He has the conventions of attractiveness, from his height to his pearly whites to the almost strategic amount of smile lines he has, but add them up and you somehow don’t leave with much. His political platform remains staunchly centrist, and the image isn’t enough to override the lack of message. You can’t really get someone like Jon Ossoff without Newsom. Less smarmy, less orange, less bitchy, Ossoff recently went viral again for his debate against Senate hopeful Mike Collins after he said Ossoff was a part of the “Epstein class.” In response, Ossoff did what he does, which is look appropriately smug in a tight little suit with a jawline you want to run your tongue along. “Mike, who on your team advised you to raise the Epstein files?” he asked. “You voted to cover up the evidence, Mike.” Somehow, there’s something hot about two casual deployments of “Mike.” Later at a campaign stop, he went after Collins and the Republicans some more. “Mike Collins is a known bigot under federal investigation for the illegal misuse of tax dollars, widely known for his poor character and low integrity, and unfit for any elected office in the United States,” he said. “And now the bigot wants a promotion.” All this while wearing a blue button-up, sleeves rolled up to expose a forearm that you might want to hang from by your teeth, like a dog with a bone. What a slut. What Ossoff and Newsom might not have in effortlessness, they make up for in online clapbacks and debate performances. These are candidates who have decidedly refused Michelle Obama’s earlier edict that we go high when Trump and his ilk go low. Their hotness quotient isn’t just about how they look, but how willing they are to scrap with political candidates who otherwise refuse to play by any rules. If Trump can’t write a tweet like a normal person, then Newsom won’t either. “I don’t think Ossoff is a good-looking man,” said Trump at a rally for Collins last week, suggesting that he probably really, really does. “I actually think Mike Collins is a good-looking man. He’s all man; I’m not sure about Ossoff.” It’s a lot of talk about who is “all man” from a guy whose hands have been melting for the better part of a year. Ossoff and Newsom get under Republicans’ skin in large part because they’re conventionally attractive men who are also adeptly dogging their competition. We find these guys hot because they trigger the same feelings of being attracted to a bully football player, or even the quiet but surprisingly cute nerd who stood up for you in Intro to Calc. It’s hardwired before we’re old enough to vote, a search for a charming protector. Daddies come in many forms, but in this type of politics, a daddy is someone willing to fight for you, even if it’s ugly and even if it’s petty. Where it begins to fall apart is when you start to ask for more than just viral clapbacks. It’s a little like going out with a good-looking guy whose Hinge profile said he loves deep conversation, but he spends the date distracted by his Kalshi bets. Neither Newsom nor Ossoff has much more to offer voters beyond the obvious. Ossoff is happy to fight with Trump’s lackeys, but he’s still a run-of-the mill Democrat. He’s remained somewhat supportive of Israel and is farther to the right than his contemporaries on Medicare for All. Newsom is an obvious antagonist for the Republicans, but it’s hard to forget that he used to roll around on a rug with at least one very famous (and recently disgraced) conservative. These guys are hot insofar as politicians go, but real hotness comes from taking risks. Maybe during these midterms, the Democrats are learning how much reward can come from letting a hot guy run his delicious little mouth. Abdul El-Sayed is kind of an asshole, but what can I say: The ladies love a bad boy!!! Forty-one years old, 5-foot-9 (short-king status; being on the same eye level with someone has a lot of practical uses), and a doctor who benches 315 pounds. He’s been dubbed “Evil Mamdani” while he’s been campaigning for Senate in Michigan. El-Sayed is a hot guy. He’s not just hot for politics, where the bar is so close to hell that even Eliot Spitzer found a hot second wife 12 years his junior to marry. He’s just a hot person, and we should all be so lucky to have him spit in our mouths. But what makes him hotter, undoubtedly, is his huge capacity for being a dick. His own campaign and even the Democratic Party know this and are happy to weaponize it; go browse their respective TikTok accounts and swim in a sea of El-Sayed clapbacks. His third-most-popular TikTok video is from July Fourth, when he danced around in his trademark black V-neck and American-flag cowboy hat, lipsyncing to Taylor Swift. (Also doing a lot of rotations on my personal TikTok is this video of him doing the Zara Larsson dolphin lift—you can see all his tricep definition.) Last week, Vice President J.D. Vance called El-Sayed a “little weasel.” In response, El-Sayed called himself “a fucking wolverine.” El-Sayed has fused two key components of political hotness into one candidate, and it’s something so few political hopefuls do successfully: He’s ready to bite back at any and all Republicans (including those in the crowds of his rallies), and he’s encapsulating hotness through a prism of traditional masculinity. It’s something Graham Platner tried to do while he was running for Senate in Maine: Throughout his campaign, he wore henleys (the straight-male equivalent of a woman wearing lingerie and holding a whip) and T-shirts over his Nazi tattoo, a thick mustache completing a kind of progressive Ron Swanson look. He was the image of the Brawny man right up until sexual assault allegations (not the Nazi stuff????) derailed his campaign. El-Sayed has adopted some of these same visual cues of traditional male behavior. He’s lifting weights in campaign videos, he’s taking his jacket off to show off his biceps, he’s making fun of Vance for having a beard that doesn’t connect. His interpretation of an attractive candidate isn’t just one in tight jeans and T-shirts (which, again, thank you), but one who acts like a rough-and-tumble boy. El-Sayed is always throwing around a football or flexing a muscle or being mean to another man, punching up with a smile on his face. It’s traditional masculinity retrofitted for a progressive audience. Just because his voters want access to healthcare and to get ICE out of the streets doesn’t mean they don’t appreciate seeing him lift something heavy just for the thirst of it. And while Zohran Mamdani is handling idiotic questions about his religion and his wife with grace and a sincere smile, El-Sayed is here to tell you, essentially, to shut the fuck up. He recently went viral for a conversation he had with “Tommy,” an attendee at one of his events who wanted him to publicly denounce the Muslim Brotherhood. (Tommy, it turned out, was a former Josh Hawley aide and the son of a Republican strategist.) Tommy refused to be upfront about why he would assume El-Sayed, of all people, would support them to begin with. “Hi Tommy. How are you? Do you know what the Constitution of the United States is?” he asked. What an icky watch, witnessing Tommy get his ass gently handed to him. But it’s also pretty hot to watch El-Sayed activate his righteous condescending, hands slipping into his jean pockets, rolling his eyes at the absurdity of such a question and refusing to let it turn into a warped John McCain moment. El-Sayed has learned a lot from more centrist candidates in the party. From Newsom, he learned the art of being an antagonist, of how effectively a man can run as Dennis the Menace against the domineering Republican Party. From Ossoff, he learned how to braid in being an intellectual and polished orator, how debate-club tactics can work as long as you eschew the tie and show an inch of clavicle. In deploying a Republican-style aggression toward his political opponents, he’s found a way to merge all of that with a broad chest that I want to rub my face in like I’m just a tiny woman and he’s an amorous bear. The result isn’t just a political leader you want to vote for, but a daddy—someone you can trust to make the bad guys go away, to make the right decisions, and to shove anyone who stands in the way. The condescension is part of it. It’s been a long time since the Democrats had someone who was willing to be as aggressive as Trump, someone willing to use the tools of combative oration to try to win an election. In a few days, we’ll have some kind of answer for whether it was effective. But if you’re worried about the midterms, about whether there is any one Democrat who can swoop in and save all of us, you just need a little self-care. May I suggest this video that includes several close-ups of El-Sayed’s bicep? It says it’s 17 seconds long, but on my computer it seems to last for hours. There, there, kitten. Daddy’s coming. Big Red gets cozy with Google to offer TPUs to AI customers
2026-10-27

Big Red gets cozy with Google to offer TPUs to AI customers

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Oracle plans to add those chips alongside new Nvidia hardware and OpenAI services as businesses await AI payoffs Oracle Cloud Infrastructure (OCI) customers will soon have a raft of new choices. Big Red is hosting an event in Las Vegas this week to inject more air into the AI bubble explain its latest AI investments, and front and center of those efforts are an expanded partnership with OpenAI and plans to diversify its chip offerings beyond Nvidia by bringing Google's Tensor Processing Units (TPUs) to OCI. Oracle says its deal to add Google TPUs to OCI will make it the first hyperscale cloud platform outside Google's own cloud services to offer the chips. Those chips will be available for selection within OCI beginning in 2027, with capacity increasing over the course of several years. While not mentioning the quantity of Google TPUs that will be available next year or beyond, it does seem that Oracle is attempting to diversify away from being so heavily reliant on Nvidia, with whom it has inked deals worth tens of billions of dollars to outfit its datacenters, including the reportedly troubled Texas project it’s planning with the GPU maker and OpenAI. “Different AI workloads have different compute needs,” said OCI president Mahesh Thiagarajan. “Bringing Google TPUs to OCI gives customers … the flexibility to choose the right infrastructure for the performance, throughput, and economics of each AI workload.” Oracle isn't moving away from its partnership with OpenAI, either, announcing new ChatGPT integrations on Tuesday alongside two products that incorporate models from the House of Altman. The first of those, Oracle AI Workspace, is billed as a “single user experience that brings OpenAI frontier models together with Oracle applications, enterprise data, and cloud services” through ChatGPT plugins that basically turn Oracle’s existing business platforms into ChatGPT-accessible resources. “The Oracle AI Workspace will make the breadth of Oracle’s capabilities agent-ready and accessible to ChatGPT users,” the company explained. “People can describe business goals and these plugins in ChatGPT will bring together the right Oracle tools and information to help them accomplish it.” Along with giving OpenAI-powered tools access to selected business data, Oracle is hoping customers will also trust the models to help handle cybersecurity with the second new product, Oracle AI Security Mesh, "a managed platform that combines frontier AI models for cybersecurity … with Oracle's proven security expertise." As you might assume from its name and description, Oracle AI Security Mesh will use OpenAI cybersecurity models to find vulnerabilities, test whether they can be exploited, and recommend fixes in areas of a company's tech stack that it authorizes the Oracle-managed service to examine. Oracle also announced a deal with Snowflake to bring its AI data cloud to OCI as a native service. The new partnership will allow Oracle customers to purchase and use Snowflake directly from Oracle, which will handle all pricing, discounts, and billing. Oracle Universal Credits will also be usable through Snowflake on OCI, allowing users who already spend money on Oracle credits to use them for the newly available offering. Oracle may be diversifying its OCI chip offerings, but it still announced new Nvidia hardware in Las Vegas, too. Big Red announced the general availability of Nvidia Vera Rubin NVL72 systems and Nvidia Vera CPUs on Tuesday, along with plans to introduce HGX Rubin NVL8 systems, though it did not specify when those would become available. “By combining NVIDIA Vera Rubin GPUs and Vera CPUs with Oracle Acceleron networking and AI-optimized data centers, we’re helping customers keep GPUs productive, secure, and scale efficiently as their needs grow,” Thiagarajan said. Oracle posted record earnings in the quarter ending August 31, with revenue up 30 percent year over year as its AI investments have begun to pay dividends. Those dividends appear to be paying off for other AI firms as well, though there's little evidence of similar returns outside the tech sector, raising fresh concerns about an AI bubble that's starting to look a bit taut. Anthropic now lets Claude users jump out of plane without parachute
2026-10-27

Anthropic now lets Claude users jump out of plane without parachute

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Meet CLAUDE_CODE_EVEN_MORE_DANGEROUSLY_SKIP_PERMISSIONS Anthropic, prone to pronouncements about model safety, has given developers using Claude Code the opportunity to throw its guardrails out the window. The company's latest Claude Code build (v2.1.311) supports an environmental variable designed to allow the AI agent to run the rm command, which deletes files, without asking for approval. The designated environmental variable (a value developers set in their shell) is called: CLAUDE_CODE_EVEN_MORE_DANGEROUSLY_SKIP_PERMISSIONS. Daisy Hollman, a member of technical staff at Anthropic, announced the change in a social media post. "Fun fact: --dangerously-skip-permissions doesn't actually approve EVERYTHING – we still block some obvious things like rm -rf ~," she wrote. "But today's release will let you disable this if you really want, by setting a new env var CLAUDE_CODE_EVEN_MORE_DANGEROUSLY_SKIP_PERMISSIONS=1" Not everyone believed Hollman, perhaps due to the absurdity of the variable name or because the change isn't listed in the v2.1.311 change log. But she offered reassurance that it's real. "Not a joke!" she responded. "I hope this helps, and I hope the variable name is memorable." The name is easy to remember because it is very much like the aforementioned Claude Code flag --dangerously-skip-permissions. That command line flag enables bypassPermissions mode, which first surfaced in Claude Code v1.0.18, on June 9, 2025. Claude Code has several permission modes: default, acceptEdits, plan, auto, dontAsk, and bypassPermissions. Auto mode, which nominally allows everything but runs background safety checks, became the default in August; bypassPermissions also nominally allows everything, without the safety checks. But there are still exceptions that do not get automatically approved and require a permission prompt, such as rm and rmdir. The new CLAUDE_CODE_EVEN_MORE_DANGEROUSLY_SKIP_PERMISSIONS environmental variable removes this last agentic restraint, though other things like managed settings may impose their own limitations. It's perhaps worth noting that there are numerous accounts of AI agents issuing commands like rm -rf ~ that wipe hard drives or take other action that results in data loss. AI 'hacker' finds 4 severe bugs in popular industrial control software in under 4 hours
2026-10-27

AI 'hacker' finds 4 severe bugs in popular industrial control software in under 4 hours

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
One critical, three high-severity EPICS bugs could disrupt research experiments An autonomous AI agent discovered four vulnerabilities in the decades-old, widely used EPICS (Experimental Physics and Industrial Control System) open source software toolkit, including a critical flaw that could allow remote attackers to execute code on systems used to control particle accelerators and fusion experiments. The bugs include a critical, 9.8-rated under CVSS v3.1 integer overflow vulnerability (CVE-2026-114595), leading to an attacker-controlled out-of-bounds heap write and potentially remote code execution; an 8.2-rated, high-severity, improper message-length validation flaw (CVE-2026-114596), causing an out-of-bounds memory write and controller-process crash; a 7.5-rated, high-severity insufficient bounds checking bug (CVE-2026-114597) that allows out-of-bounds memory reads and process crashes; and a 7.5-rated, high-severity type confusion flaw (CVE-2026-114598) that can lead to denial of service and could expose adjacent memory contents. All four affect EPICS Base, which is used to operate some of the world's largest scientific research projects and facilities, and organizations should update to version 7.0.11, which includes the fixes. “Successful exploitation of these vulnerabilities could allow an attacker to cause a denial-of-service condition, corrupt memory, disclose sensitive information, or potentially execute arbitrary code,” the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) warned in a Tuesday advisory. An AI agent called Cipher independently found all four previously unknown security holes - investigating and testing the unfamiliar EPICS system, and validating the four exploits - in three hours and 49 minutes, Causal Security co-founder Shubham Raj told The Register. “The initial investigation required no human intervention, followed by one instruction authorizing deeper exploitability testing in a secure environment,” Raj said. Earlier this year, Causal Security’s Cipher, which Raj described as “a fully autonomous super hacker that can reason across the technology stack,” found multiple critical attack paths in power-grid systems used across North America and Europe, prompting an October 8 security advisory from CISA. EPICS, originally developed by Los Alamos National Laboratory and Argonne National Laboratory, is used to build and remotely control distributed Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) systems, connecting operators to physical devices. It’s used in around 90 major scientific, university, and industrial projects and facilities globally including Argonne National Laboratory's Advanced Photon Source and ITER's fusion reactor control system. All four of these flaws illustrate how AI agents are really good at finding vulnerabilities in industrial and operational technologies - systems and equipment that used to be able to rely on obscurity to protect them from hackers - and add to defenders' concerns that it's only a matter of time until an autonomous cyberattack shuts down critical services, like power grids or water systems. These are the types of digital intrusions that can cause physical harm. Raj points to CVE-2026-114595, the critical integer-overflow vulnerability, as an example. “It allows attacker-controlled network data to cause an out-of-bounds heap memory write, with potential for remote code execution,” he told us. “EPICS controllers sit between operators and physical equipment, handling measurements, commands, and control functions,” he continued. “Operationally: Imagine a particle accelerator in the middle of an expensive experiment. An attacker could potentially crash a controller, interrupt critical measurements or control functions, and force operators to halt the experiment. That could mean lost experimental work, significant downtime, and expensive recovery.” Plus, if an attacker was able to develop an RCE exploit, this could allow them to “take control of the affected process, manipulate readings, interfere with control functions, or disrupt the equipment connected to it,” Raj said. The prerequisite for exploiting these vulns in a real scientific facility is being able to reach the vulnerable EPICS services on a facility's control network. But this could occur if the networks weren’t sufficiently segmented, or another system had access to the vulnerable EPICS network. “Once an attacker is able to find a network, which is either misconfigured or they are able to reach, it’s pretty easy to carry out the attacks,” Raj said, adding that exploitation wouldn’t require deep OT knowledge, or understanding of how particle accelerators or fusion reactors work. “Someone with conventional exploitation skills could investigate them, and increasingly capable AI systems could help develop exploits without requiring specialized OT expertise.” Manipulating specific physical processes does require additional knowledge, he added. “But an attacker doesn't need to understand the physics of a particle accelerator to disrupt the software controlling it. Crashing critical processes or interrupting monitoring and control can already have serious operational consequences. What's changing is that attackers may no longer need years of specialized expertise just to discover these weaknesses.” ® Oracle plans to add those chips alongside new Nvidia hardware and OpenAI services as businesses await AI payoffs Meet CLAUDE_CODE_EVEN_MORE_DANGEROUSLY_SKIP_PERMISSIONS PARTNER CONTENT: Learn what the flaws are, how far exploitation has gone, and how to get to full coverage fast with Tanium One critical, three high-severity EPICS bugs could disrupt research experiments Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again 6th-gen M-series silicon promises more cores, faster memory, a fatter NPU, and better connectivity, now in more form factors Investigation finds businesses dominated govt meetings while communities were left out of decisions about AI Growth Zones It's more important than chips, 'leccy and the net, after all Not a bombshell, but it rivals pricier models on some tasks at a tenth of Astra's API price New rules also shut out solo developers, fledgling projects, and anyone who doesn't speak English The Magnificent Seven are galloping off with the profits, while the rest of the S&P 500 see no benefit, claims Apollo PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Apple takes powerful M6 chip to the MacBook Pro, iMac
2026-10-27

Apple takes powerful M6 chip to the MacBook Pro, iMac

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
6th-gen M-series silicon promises more cores, faster memory, a fatter NPU, and better connectivity, now in more form factors If you were waiting to upgrade your Mac until you could get one with the latest CPU, the wait is over. Apple’s 6th-gen M-series processors are finally trickling down to its 14-inch MacBook Pro and iMac family early next month. Available in either “space black” or silver, Apple’s base-model MacBook Pro is largely unchanged from last gen. This is a mid-cycle refresh and so most of the improvements are under the hood. It sports the same high-res mini-LED display, chiclet keyboard, and port selection as its predecessor. Most of the improvements come from the silicon itself. Announced alongside a refreshed Mac mini in August, and fabbed on TSMC’s 2nm process tech, the M6 features a refreshed CPU with 12 CPU and 12 GPU cores, and is now complemented by a dual 16-core Neural Engine (what Apple calls its NPUs) which promises better performance for the slew of Apple Intelligence features now baked into macOS Golden Gate. The M6 MacBook Pro comes standard with 16 GB of unified memory, good for 153 GB/s of bandwidth. Memory can be specced up to 24 GB or 32 GB, boosting bandwidth to 170 GB/s for those with more demanding workloads or, as is more often the case, an aversion to closing Chrome tabs. Compared to the outgoing M5, which for the moment is still available in the MacBook Air, Apple says the M6 delivers significant performance gains thanks to the addition of two more cores. In Xcode, Apple boasts up to 30 percent higher performance versus a similarly equipped M5 MacBook Pro. The GPU enjoys similar gains in Blender ray tracing, local AI inference, and image processing, delivering 1.3x faster prompt processing and 1.3x faster rendering, and 1.2x faster performance in the Affinity creative suite. Despite higher performance, Apple boasts the same 24-hour battery life as the outgoing M5 model. Pricing also remains unchanged. A 16 GB model with 1 TB of solid state storage will set you back $1,999. One area of improvement not related to performance is connectivity. The M6 finally brings Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6 connectivity to the 14-inch MacBook Pro. The majority of these improvements also come to Apple’s most colorful of all-in-one desktops: the iMac. Here the performance jump is even larger, owing to the fact the machine never got the M5. Apple claims 1.5x faster performance in productivity apps like Excel, 2x faster graphics performance and 5x faster in local AI. Continuing the trend of better connectivity, along with Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6, certain iMac models also gain 2.5 Gbps Ethernet. As with the MacBook Pro, pricing remains unchanged, with iMacs starting at $1,499. EU privacy advocates accuse US data annotation firm of snooping on contractors
2026-10-27

EU privacy advocates accuse US data annotation firm of snooping on contractors

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Noyb files five complaints in four member states over alleged tracking of workers on Scale AI's Outlier platform US data annotation operation Scale AI faces five GDPR complaints in four EU countries over allegations that it unlawfully monitors European contractors. Privacy campaigner noyb alleges that Scale tracks contractors who perform data annotation work on its Outlier AI platform. Like workers on Amazon's Mechanical Turk platform of yore, Outlier contractors label data, evaluate AI-generated responses, and perform other tasks to improve AI models. Scale counts companies including OpenAI, xAI, Anthropic, and Google, as well as the US government, among its customers. Scale AI faces allegations similar to those noyb has made against other US tech firms since the EU’s General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) went into effect in 2018, namely violations of said regulation. With all that work being done digitally and remotely, Scale has extensive data collection practices to not only verify the identity of Outlier workers, but also to extensively track their activity. “Platform work seems to be fertile ground for a whole new type of workplace surveillance,” said Thomas Le Bonniec, sociology researcher at French privacy research group DIPLab, who co-authored a report earlier this month examining Outlier's data collection practices. “This would be seen as unacceptable in any other form of employment in the European Union.” Among the issues that raised the hackles of noyb's lawyers was identity verification conducted by Persona, a company that has faced scrutiny over an exposed test environment associated with its efforts to secure US government business. Researchers speculated that the system could be used to build a people tracker, but Persona rejected that interpretation and denied having active US government contracts. Separately, Persona's relationship with chat app Discord soured ahead of the platform's broader age verification rollout, with Discord saying the provider failed to meet its requirements for on-device facial age estimation. According to noyb, Persona collects biometric data to verify Outlier contractors are who they say they are, as well as gathering IP addresses, device info, geolocation data, addresses, and credit data of Outlier users. In addition to the biometric identification data, noyb accuses Scale of tracking Outlier workers by collecting system data and device activity (including activity outside of Outlier work), and transmitting that to third parties including LinkedIn, TikTok, Facebook, and other analytics services as often as every few seconds. Noyb warns that the information could be used to profile individuals and serve targeted advertising. “Such constant and extensive tracking while working really goes against all reasonable expectations,” noyb data protection lawyer Lisa Steinfeld Leiser said of the allegations. “Why should LinkedIn or Facebook know which tasks you're working on?” Noyb has filed complaints in France, Italy, the Netherlands, and Austria against Scale, arguing that it lacks a legal basis to install tracking software on contractors’ devices. The US company also stands accused of violating GDPR rules on data minimization, which requires that companies only collect data that’s strictly necessary for specific purposes. The complaints also assert Scale violated GDPR rights to data access after ignoring complainants’ requests to access the records of what data Scale had stored on them, as well as country-specific laws in the nations where the complaints were filed. Noyb is asking data protection authorities in those nations to conduct a full investigation and, if necessary, fine Scale for its alleged violations. ""The report’s claims are unfounded and inaccurate," Scale told us after this article was first published. "Outlier’s practices are disclosed to contributors and require their consent. We use standard tools to verify identities, prevent fraud, protect confidential project information, and ensure accurate payment." The complaint is likely to give further fuel to the digital sovereignty movement sweeping the EU, which has begun to realize in the past couple of years that it may not be in the best interest of the bloc to be entirely beholden to US tech companies, even if such a separation may be painfully difficult. ® Updated at 1730 UTC to include comment from Scale. ZacOS 9 and Mavernix: Two classic Apple desktops find their Wayland to Linux
2026-10-27

ZacOS 9 and Mavernix: Two classic Apple desktops find their Wayland to Linux

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Classic Platinum or lickable Mavericks-era Aqua – it's your choice You wait decades for a Mac-like desktop for Linux, and then two of them come along at once. AI might be involved, but even so, it's still shiny. ZacOS 9 and Mavernix are two very new, independent, and separate projects, but there is a common thread linking them: they are both brand new efforts to bring vintage Macintosh-like desktop environments to Linux. One is explicitly built using LLMs, and we suspect it was probably involved with the other as well. "ZacOS" is just a couple of weeks old, and both to forestall what is becoming a very frequently asked question, and also to credit to its inventor Adam Lawson, the project's GitHub page acknowledges two of the more visible LLM coding assistances: both Claude and Copilot are there. In the Hacker News discussion, he acknowledged that he used LLM assistance to make the software, and even his online comments. It aims to recreate the “Platinum” appearance of MacOS 8 and 9 – as we described last month, one of the few things that Apple successfully salvaged from the Copland project to build a true multitasking successor to MacOS 7. ZacOS includes a window manager, a filer, a global menu bar, a Control Panel app, a Control Strip, desk accessories and other additional apps, and more. It can network with Macs, and integrate with the existing SheepShaver emulator to run classic Mac apps. We previously mentioned SheepShaver when looking at the remarkable MacOS9.app web-based emulator, and if you don’t remember what it looked like, that’s the easiest way to find out. The Sourceforge downloads contain a complete pre-installed Debian Linux image. On Github, there are much smaller Debian packages if you want to try installing it yourself. It's an all-new environment, based on Wayland. (Its own icons are based on Lawson's dog, Zac.) As far as we can see, ZacOS doesn't draw on any older MacOS-like code. That's slightly surprising, because this is not the first project of its kind that we have ever seen. Although ZacOS does look to be one of the more complete ones, there's quite a bit of prior art. "Once upon a time, a very long time ago now, about last Friday," (as A. A. Milne put it, a century ago this month) – or at least, a few months before OpenAI launched ChatGPT – we mourned the lack of Linux desktop diversity. In that article, we linked to some projects that tried to bring a Mac-like UI to Linux. That included Ryu Kojiro’s classic-wm, which is a Mac System 6-styled window manager, and also Morgan Aldridge’s mlvwm, which aims at the later grayscale MacOS 7 and MacOS 8 look. Although it targets the modern Unix-based macOS, we also mentioned Classic Mac Finder, which aims to recreate the older Finder on the modern OS. A more modest but easier approach would start with one of the more versatile desktops out there. As we covered in our two-part workshop last month, that would be Xfce, which can already emulate the macOS desktop layout and with a few extra tweaks make a very usable alternative. We intentionally didn’t look at themes and skins, but they do exist, and one of the most thorough is what “Grassmunk” did with the Platinum9 theme for Xubuntu. Although Windows 95 itself is long obsolete, the Chicago95 theme makes Xfce look remarkably like the classic OS, and Chicago's developers still actively maintain it. There is a lot of nostalgia for the Classic MacOS look and feel out there, as websites such as MacOS 9 Lives! shows. However, there’s also nostalgia for how Mac OS X used to look, as well. When we looked at how to Bring back your old Mac a year ago, we mentioned Mavericks Forever – a fan site for the last OS X version to have the “lickable” Aqua look. Mavericks is notable because it was the first release of OS X that was available completely for free, as The Register reported in 2013. It’s not easy to find today, but you can still download it. The creator of Mavernix demonstrates it on Youtube We mention this because you will need a copy if you want to try the second new vintage Apple OS remake. It’s called Mavernix, and for now, it’s only available for Arch Linux. Unlike ZacOS 9, Mavernix is built on top of existing software: its windows are drawn by the Wayfire compositor, and its panels by Quickshell. So, yes, this too is entirely Wayland-based. The main novelty in Mavernix is that it contains a tool to extract and use the images, icons, textures, wallpapers and so on from a real copy of Apple OS X, so that it can draw a Mac-like desktop using real Mac resources. To use it, you need to have a copy of OS X to hand – which is why we point out where you can get one. The license agreement prohibits running it on non-Apple hardware, but since this does not require running it at all, we don’t see any IP violations here. Mavernix is by “Pruefsumme”, who is also a YouTuber, and on their channel, they’ve uploaded a demo video of the desktop in use. We can’t deny that it does look pretty. How well it works, we can’t say, but the Gershwin desktop that we reported on last year is still in active development and may provide a deeper, richer Mac-like UI in time. Its development is also LLM-assisted, but the majority of the tools are human-written code, much of it from the GNUstep project – which started in 1991, making it older than Xfce, KDE, GNOME, and around the same age as the Linux kernel itself. ® Oracle plans to add those chips alongside new Nvidia hardware and OpenAI services as businesses await AI payoffs Meet CLAUDE_CODE_EVEN_MORE_DANGEROUSLY_SKIP_PERMISSIONS PARTNER CONTENT: Learn what the flaws are, how far exploitation has gone, and how to get to full coverage fast with Tanium One critical, three high-severity EPICS bugs could disrupt research experiments Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again 6th-gen M-series silicon promises more cores, faster memory, a fatter NPU, and better connectivity, now in more form factors Investigation finds businesses dominated govt meetings while communities were left out of decisions about AI Growth Zones It's more important than chips, 'leccy and the net, after all Not a bombshell, but it rivals pricier models on some tasks at a tenth of Astra's API price New rules also shut out solo developers, fledgling projects, and anyone who doesn't speak English The Magnificent Seven are galloping off with the profits, while the rest of the S&P 500 see no benefit, claims Apollo PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Here’s why the iPhone Duo’s hinge could be cracking the screen
2026-10-27

Here’s why the iPhone Duo’s hinge could be cracking the screen

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Inside Apple’s first folding iPhone, contact with the delicate inner screen could cause a stress ‘hotspot.’ Inside Apple’s first folding iPhone, contact with the delicate inner screen could cause a stress ‘hotspot.’ Within hours of the iPhone Duo launch last Friday, people reported that the inner screen on some brand new foldable phones was already broken. The issue was similar across many photos shared on social media: some kind of issue or crack would appear in the middle of the screen above the hinge, with lines appearing on the right side of the display. On Monday, Apple acknowledged an “issue” and said it’s replacing damaged units, without going into much detail about how many broken phones have been reported so far. Shahram Mokhtari, the lead teardown technician at iFixit, has actually taken apart the iPhone Duo and has a pretty good idea about the potential cause, telling The Verge that it’s due to an issue along the hinge, where a series of gears, springs, and joints work together to let the device close flat, while also trying to avoid creating a deeper crease by giving that screen space to bend more gently inside while it’s closed. At the troublesome spot, it appears that the flexible inner screen is pushing up against a part of the hinge. That inner screen is already “extremely sensitive,” he says — if you took a pick or a sharp tool and poked at the edges of the screen, “you’re going to break that screen.” If you look at the pictures of damaged Duos on social media, there’s often a black spot where the damage is focused on the display, and that’s where the metal is hitting the screen, Mokhtari says. You can see a visual representation of what Mokhtari is talking about in iFixit’s written and video Duo teardowns, which reveal where the hinge transitions, as the center hinge support makes space for a crossing flex cable, creating several edges that could ultimately be the culprit. The damage iFixit has been seeing is at a point where the center support bar might not always drop down far enough while the screen folds, possibly creating a stress “hot spot” between the flat bar and the bent screen inside a closed iPhone Duo. At least one user with a broken Duo screen said they “felt something around the internal hinge” while showing it to a friend, kept opening it, and heard an “audible crack,” with the screen breaking. YouTuber CuriousHagi, who covered a Duo with dirt as part of a strenuous durability test, captured something similar on video, and the screen break appears in a similar spot. In a teardown video, CuriousHagi found that a part of the hinge that connects to the central bar had snapped. Apple says the issue affects a “small number” of Duo units, and Mokhtari says that “In terms of numbers, we don’t know. Apple definitely has all the data here. And I suspect they know more than they’re telling us as well.” But he notes that because foldable screens are so delicate, a “certain number” of them will fail, as will a number of the hinges because they’re also “extremely complicated and delicate.” And while Apple isn’t recalling the Duos, “If three months from now, we’re still seeing the same kind of failure, then it’s a more widespread problem, and that might lead to a recall,” Mokhtari says. Mokhtari points out that since this is the first generation of the iPhone Duo, it’s the first time Apple’s engineers are getting data on the hinge, and he says that “I guarantee you” Apple will redesign what’s at fault here for its next-generation foldable. He also notes that, generally speaking, “the phones do seem to be in people’s hands and people are using them, and they’re not running into any issues whatsoever.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most.
